Detaching that path in the backward pass (while leaving the forward pass unchanged) keeps the gradient stable at $10^{-4}$ (cosine similarity 0.97 or above across all five measurements), lowers the median pre-clipping gradient norm from 8.5 to 0.63, and improves validation loss on a CT lesion-grounding task from 0.336 $\pm$ 0.019 to 0.296 $\pm$ 0.003 (four seeds; every modified run beats every baseline run). The gain disappears when the lower layers are frozen, and an fp32 run still exhibits gradient spikes (maximum norm 39,862) and fails to match this improvement. These training results cover one task and one model.

new A preference for local interaction between layers emerges during training

Authors: Jiheon Lee

Abstract: A deep neural network is a stack of layers, and all layers are trained to lower one shared error. A change in one layer can therefore reach every other layer. Because the error is shared by all layers, one would expect the other layers to respond alike, whether they are next to the changed layer or far from it. We find that early in training the response is weak and its order by distance is not stable. As training proceeds, the response grows, and neighboring layers come to respond to changes in each other more strongly than layers further apart, in almost every network. This locality forms during training, although training links every layer to every other. We show this by pushing one layer of a copy of the network at many stages of training and measuring how much of the push reaches each of the other layers. The experiments use 512 five-layer networks trained on modular addition with each of two optimizers, AdamW and stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics.

new Preserving Pretrained CFG Structure in RL for Diffusion Models with Conditional Contrast Regularization

Authors: Yunfei Zheng, Xiaolong Wang, Dandan Zheng, Xianjun Deng, Shenghao Liu, Wei Xiang, Yuqian Li, Laurence Tianruo Yang

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has become an important component in the post-training of diffusion models. In practice, however, standard RL often improves the target reward without reliably preserving holistic generation quality, leading to unstable reward-quality trade-offs that we view as a form of reward hacking. Since Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) is known to improve visual clarity, prompt alignment, and perceptual quality in generation, we investigate whether its underlying structure can serve as a useful prior during RL. We present a unified geometric view that interprets both CFG and RL as correction vectors in a common prediction space, and identify the projection of the RL-induced correction onto the pretrained CFG correction as a useful diagnostic of quality-related structural preservation. Geometric analysis and rotation intervention further show that the pretrained CFG direction carries information useful for generation quality that reward-driven RL updates do not necessarily preserve. Based on this insight, we propose Conditional Contrast Regularization (CCR), which uses the frozen reference model's conditional contrast as a local structural prior during RL. Experiments across multiple reward settings and backbones show that CCR more consistently preserves generation quality while maintaining competitive reward, yielding a better reward-quality trade-off than standard RL and generic regularization methods.

new Gumbel Substitution: Removing Sampler Drift from RL Without Trusting the Sampler

Authors: Romain Fanucci, Malik El Hassani

Abstract: When RL rollouts come from untrusted samplers on arbitrary hardware, precision and checkpoint lag, the sampling distribution $q$ differs from the trainer's policy $p$, and training on the sampler's tokens adds a drift term equal to supervised fine-tuning toward $q$. Published corrections (TIS, MIS, IcePop, score centering, GSPO, CISPO) all need $q(y)$, which a permissionless sampler reports and can misreport. We introduce Gumbel substitution. The coordinator fixes public Gumbel noise for every token position, bit-identical across GPUs, and the sampler draws $y=\arg\max(\ell^q+G)$. The trainer recomputes $v^*=\arg\max(\ell^p+G)$, the token its own policy would have drawn with the same noise, and scores $v^*$ in the sampler's context. For a sampler that follows the protocol, $v^*$ is an exact sample from $p$ given the prefix, so the drift vanishes in expectation for any $q$, without sampler-reported probabilities. We measure the residual left by the deployed heads, bound the one added by admission gates, and state what an adaptive sampler can still do. The same comparison gives a per-token match rate that admits and pays samplers. In the lab, on a 9B model with the same head on both sides, the expected drift of uncorrected training is at least 170 to 4,800 times that of substitution. On a 2B model under strong mismatch, uncorrected training collapses while substitution stays stable, comparable to MIS with score centering given the true $q$. On a permissionless network, a 9B model was trained for 158 optimizer steps on 15.75B tokens from heterogeneous samplers, with no skipped step and 1.21% of tokens substituted; it improved on all eight held-out benchmarks.

new Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation of RL Experts: Toward Continual Post-Training

Authors: Zhen Zhang, Changyi Yang, Xuehai He, Chengzhi Liu, Yutong Yin, Ziyue Liu, Zhuoran Yin, Yangmin Li, Zhihan Liu, Jisen Li, Mingyu Jin, Chuhan Li, Zhaotian Weng, Ruoyu He, Wenyao Gao, Jiaxin Deng, Chenwei Wu, Hui Shen, Wenhao Chai, Chenyang Zhao, Zhuofan Ying, Kieran Liu, Andrew Chen, Pony Ma, Zhaoran Wang, Alkesh Patel, Zhe Gan, Xin Eric Wang

Abstract: RL post-training typically produces domain experts that must then be consolidated into one model, and as new experts keep arriving, this consolidation becomes a continual process. Weight merging and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) must re-consolidate the whole model whenever an expert is added, and can erode capabilities outside the target domains. We propose Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation (CSPD), which keeps the pre-RL model frozen, distills each RL expert independently into a soft prompt, and composes experts by concatenating their prompts without joint training. A single soft prompt suffices to distill an RL expert, recovering at least 90% of the teacher's gain in 22 of 23 math, code, and agent settings. Independently trained prompts compose well. In a multi-expert setting, the average rises with every expert added, and the full composition matches MOPD (67.83 vs. 67.79). The composition generalizes best across six held-out benchmarks. Input-space adaptation adds little serving overhead, keeping throughput within 1.5% of the unadapted backbone and reaching 2.40x that of rank-4 LoRA. These results point toward continual post-training on a fixed backbone.

new HR-Merge: Model Merging with Headroom-Weighted Residual Refinement

Authors: Jiakang Huang, Xueyan Zhang, Renyi Cai, Jingcheng Niu, Jiaru Li, Yining Wang, Josh Zhang, Jiahe Jiang, Jinman Zhao, Gerald Penn, Baharan Mirzasoleiman

Abstract: Model merging combines experts fine-tuned from a shared backbone, such as LoRA adapters of a large language model, into one multi-task model without further training. Existing methods apply one rule to every task, yet the accuracy they lose is uneven across tasks. We show that the lost accuracy remains recoverable from each task's residual, its task vector minus the merged update, and that a task's validation headroom, the share of its expert validation accuracy the merge loses, indicates where recovery pays off. Residuals from different tasks interfere, so they must be merged rather than summed. We propose HR-Merge, a training-free wrapper around any merging method. It weights each residual by its task's headroom, re-merges the weighted residuals with the base operator, and adds the result back at a strength selected on validation, falling back to the base merge when no strength helps. On six NLI adapters of LLaMA-3 8B, HR-Merge lifts TIES from 93.29 to 94.29 normalized accuracy, above all tested bases, mainly by recovering its weakest task. In vision, on three CLIP backbones, up to 20 tasks, and LoRA merging, it generally improves or preserves base accuracy and yields the best model in every setting.

new Degradation of Language Models under Controlled Load: Synchronization, Rule Execution and Misfires on the Background

Authors: Hennadii Kutomanov

Abstract: As the load grows, a language model makes more and more errors, but different processes stand behind this decline in quality. The model may lose the correspondence with the source text, it may keep its place in the text but execute a rule wrongly, or it may change a word that no rule concerns. In this work the degradation of a model under controlled load is separated into these three components: synchronization, rule execution and misfires. The load is set by rules that replace one word with another in a meaningless text, and each kind of load (the text length, the position of a word in the answer, the replacement density, the number of rules and the place of a rule in the list) is set separately. The alignment of the answer with the reference makes it possible to follow the outcome of every word, and a statistical model that takes into account that the spread of difficulty between requests itself depends on the load restores, for each component, the laws of its dependence on every kind of load. These laws make up the passport of a model. The paper presents the method of the separation, the principles of constructing the laws and the passports of seven models built by this method; the separation opens the way to studying the fine structure of the behavior of a neural network.

new Learned Network Dynamics for Model Predictive Traffic Signal Control under Incidents

Authors: Dang Viet Anh Nguyen, Carlos Lima Azevedo, Tomer Toledo, Filipe Rodrigues

Abstract: Model predictive control (MPC) of urban traffic signals relies on a model of how queues respond to signal decisions, which is hard to specify and calibrate in heterogeneous networks, particularly when incidents change capacities and turning shares. Measurements add a second limitation: the controlled lanes at the stop line are often short, so queues extending upstream of them fall outside lane-level measurements. We propose GNN-MPC, a receding-horizon controller that plans with action-conditioned network dynamics learned by graph neural networks. Phase sequences are coordinated across intersections by approximate best responses, with an acceptance test that keeps the predicted cost no higher than that of the initial plan. In microscopic simulation under non-recurrent incidents, GNN-MPC reduces travel time and increases completed trips relative to all tested controllers, on a homogeneous grid and a heterogeneous 21-intersection network. Controlled comparisons on the heterogeneous network distinguish the roles of observation and prediction. With nearly as many vehicles entering the network, GNN-MPC reduces travel time by 13% relative to MaxPressure with the same upstream measurement, and GNN-MPC without the upstream representation does not reproduce its performance. Under the same traffic measurements, planner and objective, replacing the learned dynamics with calibrated store-and-forward predictors increases travel time by 31-34%. Observing hidden demand and predicting its response to control are thus complementary requirements of network-level predictive control.

new THRIFTY: Cost-Constrained Reasoning-Data Synthesis for Small Language Models

Authors: Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina

Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for efficient, specialized deployment, and are typically trained on reasoning trajectories distilled from a larger teacher LLM. Generating this synthetic data is a major cost of the pipeline, especially when the teacher is a frontier model. Existing collection methods, however, are largely cost-unaware, or at best spread a fixed sampling budget uniformly across queries. Such allocation ignores the wide variation in problem difficulty and the strategic over- or under-sampling it invites. Thus, we propose THRIFTY, a Thompson-sampling bandit controller that spends a fixed generation-token budget to collect diverse correct reasoning trajectories, casting the task as a bandits-with-knapsacks problem with a novel diversity-aware objective. It tracks each problem's success probability with an online Beta posterior and spends each token where it expects the most new distinct correct solutions. Because repeated solutions add progressively less value, THRIFTY adaptively stops resampling saturated problems, concentrates budget on under-covered ones, and retires the unsolvable at the budget's opportunity cost. On math (AIME, MATH-500) and code (LiveCodeBench) benchmarks, THRIFTY gathers up to 22% more diverse correct trajectories per generation token than both the standard uniform-sampling recipe and the strongest adaptive baseline, with the largest gains in the scarce-budget and hard-problem regimes.

new Learning When to Ask: Advisor-Aware Post-Training for SLM Agents

Authors: Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina

Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for cheap, high-throughput agentic deployment, but they remain weak at multi-step reasoning and tool use that long-horizon tasks demand. We study how to post-train an SLM to seek advice from a stronger model and act on that advice itself, following an advisor-executor pattern. Out of the box, this pattern fails across open- and closed-source small-model families: wiring an SLM to an advisor is a harness-design matter that is not enough on its own. A post-training recipe is needed to teach the model when to ask, in a cost-aware fashion, and how to follow advice across steps. Our method, ADAPT (ADvisor-Aware Post-Training), is a multi-stage recipe that instills this behavior, learning entirely from the policy's own rollouts supervised by the environment's outcome, with no learned reward model or human annotation. Across three domains, compared to an untrained harness baseline, ADAPT improves success rate in embodied text reasoning (TextWorld) from 24.5% to 77.3%, code generation (LiveCodeBench) from 21.8% to 56.0%, and deep web search (GAIA) from 17.5% to 49.3%, while querying the advisor sparingly (~1.0-2.1 calls per task). On the two long-horizon domains it closes 70-85% of the gap to an oracle that perfectly times when to ask for advice, and on code generation it exceeds that oracle. The gains persist when the advisor is replaced by one unseen in training, showing that the learned help-seeking policy is robust to a changing advisor rather than tied to the one it was trained with.

new Cannot Wait to Verify: Parallel Drafting to Eliminate Verifier Idle Time in Speculative Decoding

Authors: Divya Jyoti Bajpai, Arun Verma, Manjesh Kumar Hanawal

Abstract: Autoregressive inference in large transformer models is limited by token-level sequential execution, resulting in high latency and low GPU utilization. Speculative decoding (SD) mitigates this bottleneck by introducing a draft-and-verifier model. However, the drafting stage remains sequential, leaving the verifier idle and underutilizing compute. To address this, we propose a plug-and-play drafting adaptive drafting method that parallelizes inference across transformer layers during draft generation. Starting from a shared hidden state, our approach evaluates multiple layers in parallel and verifies consistency by measuring divergence in hidden-state space. Layer-wise draft states are accepted when deviations are small; otherwise, a correction step is applied. By exploiting redundancy across transformer layers, our method accelerates draft generation and improves hardware utilization without modifying architectures or requiring retraining. Experimental results on NLP tasks show that our method speeds up the drafting stage by up to $1.65\times$ with minimal degradation in accuracy, while maintaining the same performance as SD.

new Verifying and Strengthening Neural Tangent Kernel Theory

Authors: Hao Zhu

Abstract: Writing Neural tangent kernel (NTK) theory explains why very wide neural networks trained by gradient methods converge in the lazy-training regime. Its standard proof combines matrix concentration, random Gram matrices, continuous-time dynamics, spectral perturbation, and a bootstrap argument, but has lacked machine-checked verification. We present a Lean\~4 formalisation for two-layer networks, proving an end-to-end theorem: for a smooth bounded activation (instantiated with $\sigma=\tanh$), frozen biases, paired $\pm 1$ initialisation, and explicit width conditions, gradient flow exists uniquely with probability at least $1-\delta$, and $$ \|r(t)\|^2 \leq \|y\|^2 e^{-\lambda_0 t/2}. $$ The sole data assumption is population NTK non-degeneracy, $\lambda_0>0$. Initial coercivity, kernel drift, the movement bootstrap, the residual ODE, and flow existence and uniqueness are formally proved. The formalisation also sharpens the standard analysis in two ways. First, replacing the conventional matrix-Bernstein variance proxy with the classical row-covariance proxy yields estimated concentration-width certificates for $\tanh$ that are $36$--$53\times$ smaller at $(m,d)=(16,8)$ and $94$--$121\times$ smaller at $(32,16)$. Lean certifies the inequality; the ratios are numerical. Second, the network Hessian has operator norm $\Theta(\sqrt{p})$ on the sup-norm parameter ball (a paper calculation), preventing the expected $O(1/\sqrt{p})$ linearisation bound via sup-norm Taylor estimates. An $L^2$ parameter geometry resolves this issue. Required widths remain polynomial but far beyond practical scales, limiting applicability.

new Learning Unknown Oscillators From Bandit Feedback

Authors: Priyank Jayraj, Poonam Goyal, Navneet Goyal

Abstract: We study semi-bandits driven by unknown oscillators. Each round, a learner selects two of four reward channels and observes their individual rewards. Frequency errors change future reward rankings and the resulting measurements. We show when committing to one fitted oscillator lets its greedy choices expose prediction errors. Our controller bounds reward loss (regret) over a continuous confidence set and holds the longest block within budget. An online regression policy gathers observations when the check fails. Regret grows at a square-root rate, up to logarithmic factors, across all frequencies and extends to two competing oscillators. On 96 fresh synthetic instances, checked commitment uses 49 fits instead of 1,023, with 1.5% higher mean regret than fitting every round. Matched isolated timings give a 4.69-fold speedup. The confidence sets also certify trajectory regret.

new Turnout: Adaptive Attention Switching for Efficient Reasoning

Authors: Anna Kuzina, Mart van Baalen, Paul N. Whatmough, Babak Ehteshami Bejnordi

Abstract: Post-hoc transformer linearization replaces the key-value cache of full attention, which grows linearly with generation length, with a fixed size state. This change reduces memory consumption but often hurts accuracy. In reasoning tasks, which require long generations, this effect is especially pronounced. In this work we introduce Turnout, a method that mitigates accuracy loss as a result of linearization, while maintaining its memory-reduction benefits. We apply post-hoc linearization to a pretrained full-attention model, training a linear path through distillation while leaving the original full-attention path unchanged. During response generation, the resulting hybrid model retains both paths and uses a learned policy to control when each layer switches from full to linear attention. We train the policy with reinforcement learning using a reward that balances answer correctness and memory use. Empirical evaluation of our method shows that the learned adaptive policy achieves a better accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier on reasoning tasks compared to fixed switching and static hybrid baselines.

new Breaking the Barrier of Hard Samples: A Data-Centric Approach to Synthetic Data for Medical Tasks

Authors: Maynara Donato de Souza, Cleber Zanchettin

Abstract: Data scarcity and quality issues remain significant barriers to developing robust predictive models in medical research. Traditional reliance on real-world data often leads to biased models with poor generalizability across diverse patient populations. Synthetic data generation has emerged as a promising solution, yet challenges related to these samples' representativeness and effective utilization persist. This paper introduces Profile2Gen, a novel data-centric framework designed to guide the generation and refinement of synthetic data, focusing on addressing hard-to-learn samples in regression tasks. We conducted approximately 18,000 experiments to validate its effectiveness across six medical datasets, utilizing seven state-of-the-art generative models. Results demonstrate that refined synthetic samples can reduce predictive errors and enhance model reliability. Additionally, we generalize the DataIQ framework to support regression tasks, enabling its application in broader contexts. Statistical analyses confirm that our approach achieves equal or superior performance compared to models trained exclusively on real data.

new On the Limits of Observability for Evaluating Coverage in Machine Unlearning

Authors: Ziluowen Luo, Jun Yin, Songyang Liu, Ji Qi, Chuang Hu, Kyrie You Wu, Chaozhuo Li, Senzhang Wang

Abstract: Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of any requested subset of training data while preserving utility on the remaining data. Empirical evaluations measure predictive accuracy and infer training-set membership (MIAs) from model outputs or intermediate activations. However, by focusing on selected forget sets, these evaluations can overlook Coverage, the range of requests a method can fulfill. To assess this coverage, we first conduct a stress test of observability with 502 retrained models, finding that hidden embeddings reveal known deletions missed in predictions. To characterize capacity limits on coverage, we show that distinguishing all $n$ singleton deletions with linear scores can require a shared representation of rank $\Omega(\log n)$. We further extend the bound to arbitrary subsets and show when partial deletion can appear complete. Building on this analysis, we propose CEO, a framework for evaluating unlearning coverage under limited observability. Its compositional color-shape benchmark makes forgetting and retained utility measurable from the full output distribution and provides a leave-one-out retraining oracle for each target. From a fixed number of oracle observables, we estimate empirical target coverage, the fraction of targets reliably flagged when deleted and rarely flagged under any other deletions. To recover missed deletions, CEO further expands each observable representation into high-dimensional activation space with the frozen encoder of a SAE trained on the original model. Extensive experiments of 20 baselines shows that even on this simple synthetic task, only retraining from scratch covers all deletion targets.

new WEIR: Watershed Exploration and Drift-Aware Prefix Shrinkage for Multi-Turn Agent Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Pixel Nomand, Elena Voss, Marcus Hale, Sofia Reyes

Abstract: Multi-turn language model agents often receive only a single binary reward at the end of a long interaction trajectory, making it difficult to separate decisive decisions from routine steps or estimate accurate intermediate values. Prefix-tree methods improve sample efficiency by merging trajectories that share action histories, yet two structural flaws remain: naive pooling mixes stale off-policy outcomes into critic targets with fixed weights, and uniform or entropy-driven branching wastes continuations on dead-end states whose outcome is already settled. We introduce WEIR (Watershed Exploration and Intrinsic-Drift Shrinkage), an actor-critic reinforcement learning framework grounded in a Doob turn-variance conservation identity. WEIR maintains a cross-iteration canonical action-prefix tree with exponentially discounted visit counts and trains a prefix-conditioned critic toward a minimax drift-aware shrinkage target that adapts from single-trajectory returns at rare nodes to pooled empirical means at dense nodes. Using the same pre-update critic, WEIR allocates rollout budgets across tasks via Neyman stratification $B_q \propto \sqrt{\hat{p}_q(1-\hat{p}_q)}$ and directs within-task branches to watershed forks where high survival-headroom uncertainty coincides with steep value cliffs. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, ALFWorld, and WebShop on Qwen2.5-3B/7B-Instruct, WEIR achieves $32.18\%$ and $37.85\%$ average QA exact match and $84.83\%$ and $87.81\%$ ALFWorld success rates, outperforming CAPO by $1.50$--$3.74$ points while generating $28$--$29\%$ fewer rollout tokens than chain sampling.

new Minimax-Optimal Small-Loss Regret in Online Convex Optimization and Applications to Contextual Search and Online Inverse Optimization

Authors: Shinsaku Sakaue

Abstract: We give small-loss regret bounds for online convex optimization (OCO), using one subgradient per round. Let $\Delta_T^\star$ denote the cumulative loss of the best fixed decision in hindsight, measured relative to the per-round minima. Our algorithm achieves the regret bound $O(1+\sqrt{\Delta_T^\star})$ without knowing $\Delta_T^\star$, up to domain and gradient-scale factors. The bound is minimax optimal, including its domain dependence, on $\ell_p$ balls for every $1\le p\le\infty$. No smoothness or condition bounding gradient norms by loss values is assumed, and neither loss values nor the time horizon are needed. Algorithmically, we extend the metric-update revocation of Gupta et al. (2026), originally developed for online inverse optimization, to general OCO using the follow-the-regularized-leader framework and a mixture of multiple base learners. We also apply our algorithm to contextual search and online inverse optimization. In $d$-dimensional contextual search with comparison flips of unknown bias $\kappa>0$, we obtain minimax expected cumulative absolute error $\Theta(\sqrt{d}/\kappa^2)$ under Euclidean normalization using polynomially many arithmetic operations, resolving an open question of Liu et al. (2021). For online inverse optimization with the Euclidean unit-ball utility domain, our algorithm bounds the regret relative to observed actions by $O(\sqrt{d}+d^{1/4}\sqrt{D_T})$, where $D_T$ is their cumulative suboptimality; this matches the regret rate of Gupta et al. (2026) when $D_T=0$. Our guarantee extends to other utility domains; for example, on the probability simplex, the bound improves to $O(\log(d+1)+\sqrt{D_T\log(d+1)})$.

new PRISM: Validation-Free Online Batch Selection for LLM Fine-Tuning via Preconditioned Conic Ghost Matching

Authors: Lin Tang, Wei Zhang, Jing Li, Hongyu Chen, Ming Zhao, Yuxuan Wang

Abstract: Online batch selection lowers the cost of fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) by replacing each candidate batch with a smaller informative subset. Existing selectors typically depend on a held-out validation or proxy set, or else rely on Euclidean facility-location objectives over unpreconditioned gradients whose diameter-shifted similarities are distorted by heavy-tailed gradient norms and cross-layer anisotropy. We introduce PRISM (Preconditioned Ray-Isotropic Submodular Matching), a validation-free online batch selector that aligns candidate gradients in a factored second-moment metric and casts subset construction as conic facility location over positive ray projections. Computing the required pairwise geometry through factored preconditioned ghost inner products avoids materializing per-example parameter gradients, while the conic objective remains normalized, nonnegative, and monotone submodular---guaranteeing a $(1-1/e)$ greedy approximation whose additive error scales with total batch gradient energy rather than the worst-case outlier diameter. Assigning each candidate to its best-aligned selected ray induces closed-form radial projection weights that automatically shrink orthogonal or noisy examples toward zero. We derive a nonconvex convergence bound showing that optimization error is governed directly by the conic representation cost. Across four LLM fine-tuning configurations spanning 1.5B to 8B parameters, PRISM outperforms full-batch training, Euclidean facility location, and zeroth-order selectors, and rivals or surpasses validation-guided baselines without held-out data.

new Adapting AI Weather Foundation Models to a Changing Climate

Authors: Xinlei Xiong, Wenbo Hu, Shuxun Zhou, Lingxi Xie, Richang Hong, Qi Tian

Abstract: AI weather foundation models learn from historical archives, while the climate distribution encountered during deployment continues to evolve. This growing model--climate mismatch motivates continual adaptation without repeated full-model retraining, but the structure of weather forecast errors determines how such adaptation should be performed. We develop an online adaptation framework that exploits two complementary error structures: geographically persistent biases and flow-dependent errors that vary with the atmospheric state. Analysis of Aurora-1.3B reveals strongly concentrated forecast-loss sensitivity in its decoder, motivating flow-dependent corrections within fixed low-dimensional sensitivity subspaces. A residual memory tracks persistent errors remaining after this correction, while a variable-wise online gate controls the contribution of the persistent component. We evaluate Aurora-1.3B using ERA5 from 1959--1968 for offline initialization and a strictly chronological 1969--2022 stream for online evaluation, with updates restricted to previously available verification targets. For 2-metre temperature, distributional departure from the historical reference, measured directly from ERA5, is associated with larger frozen-model errors and greater adaptation gains, while the adapted model maintains more stable skill across evaluation periods. Across 69 output fields and five lead times from 6 to 72 hours, our method reduces MSE and MAE by 6.90\% and 6.45\%, respectively, relative to $\delta$-Adapter, the strongest of six online adaptation baselines.

new Quantum-Informed Regularized Poisson Learning for Graph-Based Semi-Supervised Classification

Authors: Hamed Gholipour, Farid Bozorgnia, Hamzeh Mohammadigheymasi, Joao Neves, Moharram Challenger

Abstract: We introduce IPQSSL, a quantum-informed regularized Poisson framework for graph-based semi-supervised classification under scarce labels. Local graph feature contexts are QR factorized, lifted to unitary encodings, and processed by a shared variational circuit whose measurements yield node wise diagonal weights that stabilize a symmetrized Poisson system. We establish positive definiteness of the symmetrized, regularized operator under explicit spectral conditions and derive a Hermitian, block encoding compatible formulation that separates the classical NISQ oriented implementation from a future fault-tolerant pathway. Across seven benchmarks spanning tabular data and a citation network, the effect of regularization is dataset- and budget-dependent: IPQSSL improves over the Poisson baselines on Iris and Wine at all but the single-label budget, reaching 0.946 accuracy on Iris at ten labels per class and at every budget on German Credit, where, however, all methods remain below the majority class rate. Original Poisson remains preferable on Breast Cancer, Heart Disease, Optdigits, and Cora, with the deficit narrowing as the label budget grows on the two largest graphs. Ablations attribute the gains to diagonal stabilization rather than variational training. The result is a resource transparent bridge between Poisson graph learning and quantum linear algebra, with an explicit empirical delineation of when diagonal regularization helps.

new Circular Predictive Coding Unlocks Effective Depth

Authors: Tommaso Salvatori, Matteo Forasassi

Abstract: Predictive coding networks perform credit assignment through an energy minimization process. Despite their ability to train models with arbitrary network topologies, they have mostly been used to train feedforward architectures to match the performance of backpropagation. However, they fail to do so when the models become too deep, because error signals become unstable or vanish as they travel through many layers. We address this limitation with circular networks, which get their depth directly from the energy minimization, rather than from stacking layers. Formally, we prove that convergence to an equilibrium of the energy functional of a circular predictive coding network approximates the fixed-point iteration process of a deep equilibrium model, showing that circularity alone suffices to unlock effective depth. Empirically, we show that circular models (i) outperform other predictive coding models of the same size on image classification tasks, matching the accuracy of state-of-the-art predictive coding ResNets and VGG networks despite having substantially fewer parameters; (ii) solve constraint satisfaction problems that require many steps of iterative reasoning, a task not feasible with feedforward models at all, reaching 99.3% test accuracy on Sudoku-hard via nested energy minimization loops; and (iii) scale to autoregressive language modeling by training a circular transformer on TinyStories and WikiText-103, before out of reach for predictive coding models. These results have direct implications for physical neural networks, where computational depth can be traded for recursion on a fixed-size circuit.

new Potential emergence in LLM agents: a statistical physics perspective

Authors: Zhuo-Yang Song, Hua Xing Zhu

Abstract: There has been significant recent interest in understanding the behaviour of LLM agents from the perspective of human preferences or corpus statistics. In this paper we propose agent states as intermediate variables through which post-training can be measured and predicted. From a statistical physics perspective, we study the emergence of a global potential which governs the transition of state in LLM agent reasoning. We introduce a partition function to model the ideal KL-regularised transition distribution and track potential structure, mean reward and state entropy. We demonstrate our approach in two Qwen3.5-9B tasks: safety-test selection and physics-reasoning auditing. We observe that the potential structure strengthens during post-training as predicted from our framework, while joint changes in reward and state entropy provide consistency evidence. States thus provide a quantitative description of how post-training organises agent behaviour.

new Efficient VLA Reinforcement Learning via Subchunk Rollouts in Latent World Models

Authors: Nan Tang, Han-Tao Zhang, Jing-Cheng Pang, Hao-Nan Wang, Hao-Xiang Ren, Yi-Fei Sheng, Jun-Yin Ye, Tian Xu, Fan-Ming Luo, Hao-Xin Lin, Yang Yu

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) can improve pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policies through imagined experiences. However, existing VLA-MBRL methods primarily rely on large-scale image or video generation world models, incurring large computational overhead. Latent world models offer a more efficient alternative but face two challenges: compounding errors during recursive prediction and incompatibility with VLAs' visual input interfaces. In this work, we propose LS-MBPO, a lightweight residual MBRL framework that leverages latent world models to improve upon pretrained VLAs. LS-MBPO introduces two key techniques: (1) Hierarchical Forcing, which combines dense causal supervision with autoregressive feedback to mitigate compounding prediction errors; and (2) Latent Subchunk Rollouts, which leverage action chunks from a frozen VLA policy and a latent residual policy for successive subchunk refinement, bridging the gap between latent imagination and VLA policy execution. We further introduce a Progress-Conditioned Branch-Start Curriculum that progressively shifts rollout starting points from later to earlier task stages, enabling effective policy learning from short imagined rollouts. Extensive experiments on LIBERO, RoboTwin, and a real-world ALOHA robot show that LS-MBPO achieves superior task performance and training efficiency compared to existing methods.

new Late-Time Dynamics of Flow Matching: Distance Geometry and Learning

Authors: Umberto Biccari, Junqing Huang, Kang Liu, Enrique Zuazua

Abstract: Generative models transform simple random inputs into samples that resemble observed data. For a finite training set, a central question is how its geometry shapes the terminal dynamics and the probabilities assigned to individual samples. We address this question for flow matching with linear interpolation between independent Gaussian and empirical samples. The squared distance to the training set generates explicit paths contracting exponentially toward the nearest sample. We prove that exact trajectories restarted at prescribed late-time states approximate these paths uniformly over all elapsed times and compact sets of starting states away from Voronoi interfaces, with an error exponentially small in the inverse squared restart noise scale. The initial mass near interfaces controls the quadratic Wasserstein error between the exact and reference laws. For Gaussian and fixed Gaussian-tube restart laws, this gives a square-root rate in the restart noise scale, uniform over time, which a two-site example shows to be sharp in general. At ties, the Clarke subdifferential characterizes the graphical limit of the rescaled velocity field. This geometry motivates two learning methods: fitting the distance potential with value and gradient supervision, and fitting velocities along explicit contraction paths. A transport estimate separates initialization mismatch, velocity error, incomplete contraction, and endpoint allocation. MNIST experiments illustrate the distinct roles of support proximity, nearest-site retention, and feature fidelity. These results connect flow matching and score-based diffusion through a common limiting geometry and show how asymptotic analysis can inform the design and evaluation of generative models.

new Beyond Sample Hardness: Adaptive Data Selection through Learning Dynamics

Authors: Hai Gan, Suorong Yang, Guang Li, Baile Xu, Furao Shen

Abstract: Data selection has emerged as an effective approach to reducing the model training costs. However, most existing methods often rely heavily on sample hardness, which may overlook differences in sample learning dynamics and lead to suboptimal sample selection. In this paper, we propose LeDAS, an end-to-end Learning Dynamics-Aware Selection framework for dynamic data selection, which jointly characterizes sample importance through hardness, learning progress, and deterioration, enabling adaptive sample prioritization throughout training with marginal additional computational overhead. Specifically, we track per-sample loss dynamics using exponential moving averages (EMA) to obtain three complementary signals that capture current learning difficulty, ongoing learning improvements, and potential degradation. To accommodate evolving training dynamics, we introduce Adaptive Metric Fusion, a contextual bandit-based mechanism that dynamically adjusts the relative importance of these signals based on training feedback. Furthermore, a bounded staleness compensation mechanism accounts for historical selection patterns by increasing the selection priority of samples that have not been selected for extended periods. Extensive experiments across diverse vision and language tasks demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of LeDAS. Compared with state-of-the-art baselines, LeDAS consistently achieves superior performance, matching or surpassing full-dataset training at a 50\% selection ratio on ImageNet-1K and in LLM supervised fine-tuning. Moreover, its modular and task-agnostic design enables the integration of complementary selection signals, demonstrating strong robustness to noisy and corrupted datasets.

new SMSD: A Semantic Misinformation Spreader Detection Approach

Authors: Zeeshan Bin Siddique, Michael Bewong, Md Geaur Rahman, Sajal Halder, Selasi Kwashie

Abstract: Misinformation spreaders on social media significantly amplify false narratives, making early detection critical to mitigate their spread. Existing approaches often overlook evolving user behavior, introduce semantic noise by ignoring user stance, and fail to handle newly emerging users effectively. To address these limitations, we propose a \underline{S}emantic \underline{M}isinformation \underline{S}preader \underline{D}etection approach (SMSD) that models misinformation propagation as a temporal, user-centric, and behavior-driven learning problem. SMSD adopts a multimodal architecture that incorporates three categories of features: user-level, network-level, and conversation propagation. A stance-aware labeling strategy is also introduced to differentiate supportive, opposing, questioning, and neutral users towards misinformation claims. Additionally, a monthly quantile analysis is introduced to simulate realistic, evolving user participation. Initial experimental results on the FibVid COVID-19 benchmark dataset demonstrate that SMSD improves recall by 45.0\%, F1 by 49.5\%, and MCC by 74.6\% under stance-aware labeling and by 17.7\%, 7.4\%, and 11.4\%, respectively, under threshold-based labeling. It consistently outperforms all baselines in both settings.

new Geometrically Constrained Learning II: Agnostic Proper Learning of Lipschitz Functions

Authors: Jane Lange, Cassandra Marcussen, Arsen Vasilyan

Abstract: We give a proper learner for Lipschitz functions over the Gaussian distribution on $\mathbb{R}^d$. Given random Gaussian examples labeled by a $\lambda$-Lipschitz function, $\lambda \ge 1$, the learner returns a function $h:\mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{R}$ that is globally $\lambda$-Lipschitz and has loss (absolute or squared) at most $\varepsilon$. The sample complexity, running time, and representation size are \[ (d+1)^{\mathsf{poly}(\lambda/\varepsilon)}. \] The learning algorithm can be made \emph{agnostic}, given a known bound on the variance of the label marginal distribution. Some known control of label scale is information-theoretically necessary. For arbitrary labels with variance at most $V^2 \ge 1$, the complexity is \[ (d+1)^{\mathsf{poly}(V\lambda/\varepsilon)}, \] and the error is $\mathsf{opt} + \varepsilon$, where $\mathsf{opt}$ is the minimal error among Lipschitz approximators. Our approach combines $L_1$ polynomial regression under a convex gradient constraint with local correction of the Lipschitz property. The learner finds a polynomial that, after smoothing and clipping its range, is very close to a canonical Lipschitz correction of itself, the lower Lipschitz envelope. Our local corrector modifies the lower envelope by replacing distances to sublevel sets with a Gaussian sampling proxy, allowing the construction to be locally and stably estimated from samples.

new Solving the pre-image problem in kernel PCA: The reproducing property is all you need

Authors: Joel-Pascal Ntwali N'konzi, Stefan Klus

Abstract: Kernel PCA extends PCA to nonlinear dimensionality reduction by implicitly mapping data into a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) via the kernel trick, with applications ranging from image denoising to out-of-distribution detection. While its projection map is well understood, appropriately defining the associated reconstruction map is a longstanding challenge since projecting back from the RKHS to the input space is non-trivial. This is known as the pre-image problem. Existing approaches have tackled this problem by either relying on nonlinear optimization, which is prone to numerical instabilities and sensitive to initialization, or by solving a separate supervised learning problem, incurring additional computational overhead. Furthermore, although the projection map of kernel PCA reduces to that of standard PCA when a linear kernel is used, most of these approaches are not guaranteed to recover PCA's reconstruction map in this case, a consistency property that one would naturally expect to hold. In this paper, we leverage the reproducing property of the RKHS to derive a reconstruction map for kernel PCA that is provably consistent. Our derivations rely on the mild assumption that the RKHS either contains linear functions or the kernel is universal, which holds for standard kernels. We further extend the method to the random Fourier features (RFFs) approximation of kernel PCA by introducing a corresponding notion of RFF reproducing property. Numerical experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that the proposed method performs comparably with state-of-the-art methods across kernel choices, while remaining more computationally efficient and maintaining consistent performance in settings where these baseline methods drastically fail.

new Beyond Math, Code, and Logic: Do Reasoning Models Transfer to Scientific Argumentation?

Authors: Buse Sibel Korkmaz, Antonio del Rio Chanona

Abstract: Scientific discourse depends on argumentative reasoning: identifying claims, evaluating evidence, and constructing coherent responses. While recent advances in reasoning-capable language models have demonstrated strong performance on mathematical, coding, and logical benchmarks, their ability to engage in scientific argumentation remains unclear. We present the first systematic evaluation of language models across seven tasks spanning argument mining, rebuttal generation, and discourse-level reasoning using research papers, peer reviews, and grant proposals. Our study reveals that even frontier models with strong general reasoning skills struggle with domain-specific argumentative tasks, highlighting a fundamental capability gap. To address this, we introduce a training framework that explicitly scaffolds the argumentative reasoning process in language models, substantially improving their competence in scientific discourse. The resulting compact models approach or exceed the performance of much larger proprietary systems and generalize to unseen conversational settings, demonstrating reasoning transfer beyond task-specific supervision. These findings underscore that effective scientific argumentation is not an emergent property of scale, but requires explicit reasoning-aware training, and they point toward practical pathways for building AI systems that can contribute meaningfully to scientific discourse.

new A Switching Mechanism for Selecting Environment-Adaptive Personalized Gains in Hearing Aids

Authors: Nafisa Zarrin Tasnim, Nasser Kehtarnavaz

Abstract: Personalized amplification gains for hearing aids have been shown to be preferred over prescriptive gains. Since the preferred settings can vary depending on the background audio environment, this paper presents a switching mechanism or system that automatically selects personalized gains for a current background audio environment. To prevent foreground speech from interfering with background environment analysis, a one-class voice activity detector is designed to identify speech activity from speech mixed with noise. A consistency check is conducted to enable distinguishing foreground speech from background speech, preserving babble as a background environment of interest because hearing aid users often struggle to understand speech in background babble. Sound segments without foreground speech are then analyzed by an online discovery method combining Bayesian change-point detection with a memory of previously encountered environments. This discovery method detects changes, recognizes returning environments, and discovers new ones without a predefined number of environments. Experimental results show that three-seed mean macro-averaged F1 scores of 85.53%-91.98% across eight speech/background-corpus pairs covering 36 background noise types were achieved. Furthermore, the discovery method achieved adjusted Rand indices of 0.91 and 0.96 on two families of test streams. In the same-recording-return condition of audio streams combining speech with four background audio environments often encountered by hearing aid users, the switching mechanism selected the correct personalized gain profile after 105 environment changes, recalled the stored gain profile at every return, and made no switches to an incorrect gain profile.

new DPMA: Learning to Compress Mixture-of-Experts Models

Authors: Jon Irureta, Jon Imaz, Philipp Wiesner, Gorka Azkune, Javier Fernandez-Marques, Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanovi\'c, Aizea Lojo, Nicholas D. Lane

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures offer high computational efficiency, but storing all experts requires substantial memory and limits deployment on resource-constrained hardware. Most retraining-free MoE compression methods commit to either pruning or merging experts, often with a uniform compression ratio across layers. We introduce DPMA (Differentiable Prune Merge Absorb), which jointly learns compression operations and layer-wise expert allocation through gradient descent under a global expert budget. Separate learnable coefficients allow DPMA to prune experts, merge their parameters, and absorb their routing contributions by forwarding routing weights to retained experts. These coefficients and expert assignments are optimised to match the original model's outputs on calibration data while its weights remain frozen. Across four MoE models, eight multiple-choice benchmarks, and five compression ratios, DPMA achieves the highest accuracy averaged over benchmarks and compression ratios on each model among the evaluated methods. At $75\%$ expert reduction, it outperforms the strongest baseline on every model, with gains of up to $6.7$ percentage points.

new $\mathtt{DistP}$: Scaling Local-Update Training with Zero-Shot Hyperparameter Transfer

Authors: Andrej Jovanovi\'c, Alex Iacob, Philipp Wiesner, Christophe Roux, Max Zimmer, Lorenzo Sani, Sebastian Pokutta, Nicholas D. Lane

Abstract: Local-update methods offer a communication-efficient alternative to Distributed Data Parallelism ($\mathtt{DDP}$), but introduce additional hyperparameters that require costly tuning. For $\mathtt{DDP}$, zero-shot hyperparameter transfer reduces this cost by reusing settings tuned on smaller models, but its applicability to local-update training remains unclear. We propose $\mathtt{DistP}$, a theoretical framework extending existing transfer rules from $\mathtt{DDP}$ to local-update training with varying worker counts, local steps, and outer optimizers. With averaging as the outer optimizer, $\mathtt{DistP}$ supports learning-rate transfer across width and depth while improving model quality. The tuned inner learning rate also directly transfers across synchronization intervals, but follows two regimes when increasing the global batch size: square-root learning-rate scaling for larger local batches and a fixed reference rate for additional workers. We then generalize width and depth transfer to other outer optimizers, enabling stable scaling with $\mathtt{Adam}$. However, with momentum in the outer optimizer, changing batch size or local steps can require inner--outer learning-rate tuning, consistent with prior work. In language-modeling experiments at $823$M parameters, $\mathtt{DistP}$ reduces validation loss by $8.1\%$ and improves mean downstream accuracy by $10\%$ relative to baselines without retuning at the larger scale. Fitted scaling laws suggest that this advantage grows with the compute budget, reinforcing the benefits of $\mathtt{DistP}$ for communication-efficient training.

new Mixture of Latent Recursions for Low-Rank Adaptation

Authors: Weixun Luo, Ye Mao, Ranran Huang, Junpeng Jing, Krystian Mikolajczyk

Abstract: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) performs task-specific adaptation using a fixed low-rank architecture, whose expressiveness is typically increased by enlarging or adding learnable modules with a proportional parameter overhead. We introduce Mixture of Latent Recursions (MoLR), an adaptive recursion framework that enhances adaptation expressiveness while preserving parameter efficiency. MoLR recursively refines the down-projected latent representation of each input using a shared transformation. A lightweight threshold-based router independently determines whether each token should undergo further refinement, while dynamically adjusted thresholds encourage balanced utilization across recursion depths. Extensive experiments across diverse language and vision tasks demonstrate that MoLR consistently outperforms representative LoRA variants with negligible parameter overhead over LoRA. Further analyses show that MoLR learns input-dependent recursion-depth allocation and enables a controllable inference-time compute-performance trade-off without requiring retraining. Code will be publicly available.

new Graph Signal Generation via Graph-Aware Diffusion Models

Authors: Vimal K B, Sergio Rozada, Antonio G. Marques, Elvin Isufi, Hadi J. Rad

Abstract: Many signals are defined on the nodes of a graph, such as traffic flow at road sensors or user purchases of related products. They are often partly observed, and learning their distribution enables imputing missing values and forecasting future ones. Diffusion models learn such distributions, but their forward noise is independent across nodes and does not depend on the graph. We introduce graph-aware diffusion (GAD), whose forward process is a heat diffusion on the graph driven by Gaussian noise. In the graph Fourier basis, the forward transition is a scalar Gaussian channel at each frequency. With a shared diffusion rate, high graph frequencies are attenuated faster than low ones. We propose Mode-Adaptive SNR Scheduling (MASS), which assigns each graph frequency its own diffusion rate and keeps all of them at the same SNR. For graph-stationary signals, MASS equalizes the normalized error of the optimal linear denoiser across frequencies. We bound reverse-SDE stiffness as a function of the (shifted) Laplacian's condition number, with a logarithmic upper bound for MASS and a linear lower bound for shared rates. GAD with MASS improves recommendation accuracy by 5-19%, reduces traffic-imputation RMSE by up to 7.8% with half as many reverse steps, and improves forecasting MAE and RMSE using only 3 reverse steps.

new DistDNA: Language Model Fingerprinting under Stochastic Decoding

Authors: Lifan Hu, Qian Yu, Zhaomin Wu, Bingsheng He

Abstract: Behavioral fingerprints identify a large language model (LLM) from its responses to a shared set of prompts, enabling applications such as verifying whether a deployed model corresponds to the checkpoint it claims to be. Deployed models, however, are commonly served with stochastic decoding, under which the same model may produce different responses to an identical prompt, depending on an unknown temperature and top-$p$ setting. Existing representations such as LLM DNA provide limited treatment of this variation. A natural remedy is to characterize models by their response distributions, which, however, raises two challenges. First, each distribution must be estimated from only a small number of sampled responses. Second, a change in decoding setting can alter responses to the same extent as a change of checkpoint. Consequently, fingerprint distances alone provide an incomplete basis for model identity, and identification typically relies on a classifier trained jointly on fingerprints from many checkpoints. We propose DistDNA, which represents a model by its prompt-conditional response distributions and compares models by promptwise maximum mean discrepancy (MMD). The model under examination is assigned to the known checkpoint whose reference fingerprints, collected at several decoding settings, lie nearest to its own, without training a classifier. We show that finite sampling adds variance-dependent terms to the expected squared fingerprint distance, with these terms decreasing as $O(1/R)$ for equal response counts, where $R$ is the number of responses per prompt. Experiments across 115 model checkpoint entries demonstrate that DistDNA improves identification accuracy over the strongest baseline, LLM DNA, by 10.36%.

new Beyond Visual Fidelity: Comparing Categorical and Continuous World Models

Authors: Sarah Parisot, Melanie Fernandez Pradier, David Bignell, Sergio Valcarcel Macua, Katja Hofmann

Abstract: Visual world models can now generate compelling interactive worlds, but visual realism does not guarantee faithful dynamics. We systematically compare how categorical and continuous prediction shape learned dynamics under both next-token and iterative whole-frame generation. These choices are often bundled in approaches such as autoregressive token models and whole-frame flow matching. We design controlled 2D and 3D environments and evaluate dynamics validity, calibration, and visual quality. In 2D with lossless visual encoding, categorical models achieve higher rollout validity with less training compute, particularly for stochastic entities. Among categorical models, greedy decoding produces the highest rollout validity, while sampling from a next-token model yields the best-calibrated stochastic predictions. Failures in continuous models, such as disappearing game characters, echo those seen in more complex environments. Here, known dynamics let us measure their impact. In 3D, categorical models also achieve stronger long-horizon performance with less training compute. Even models with lossy tokenization remain competitive on semantic accuracy and FVD, despite lower next-frame RGB accuracy. Together, these findings make a strong case for categorical prediction as a compute-efficient route to learning world dynamics.

new From Gist to Verbatim: The Controllable Path of One Image Through a Diffusion Prior

Authors: Luca Saglietti, Luca Giammanco, Marc Mezard, Chiara Plizzari, Luca Biggio

Abstract: Modern generative models are typically studied for their ability to generalize, while memorization is treated as a failure mode. We invert this perspective and ask how a generative model can recall a single datum. We frame memorization as a control problem: a learned conditioning signal steers a frozen diffusion model toward a target image, and we follow the formation of this memory throughout training. Before converging to exact reproduction (verbatim), memory passes through a regime of abstraction: samples preserve the target's semantic content (gist) while remaining diverse in appearance, leaving predictable detail to the generative prior. This semantic organization emerges despite the absence of labels, captions, or any explicit semantic objective. In the abstraction regime, the learned memory approaches ordinary text conditioning and supports editing, one-shot recognition, and retrieval more effectively than in the near-perfect reconstruction regime. These results suggest that memorization can serve as a probe of the relevance structure encoded by a generative model: what must be stored about a datum, what can be reconstructed from prior knowledge, and how learned memories interpolate between reconstructive recall and literal data storage.

new Renormalized Entropy from Symmetries: Simplicity Bias and Neural Heat Death

Authors: Vasco Portilheiro, Samuel Liebana, Andrew Saxe, Peter Orbanz

Abstract: Neural networks have parameter symmetries: parameter transformations that do not affect the represented function. How do these symmetries affect the functions that neural networks ultimately learn? We study the implicit bias arising from these symmetries by calculating the equilibrium distribution over represented functions under Langevin dynamics with Gaussian regularization. To isolate the effect of symmetries on this distribution, we study the limit in which the strength of the regularization is taken to zero. In certain cases, we show that the zero-regularization and infinite-time limits commute. In the low temperature limit, we recover known effective regularizations. At positive temperature, a renormalization procedure allows us to derive a novel entropy which predicts symmetry-induced preference for certain functions. For linear networks, we obtain explicit entropy terms that favor singular and low-rank functions. For networks with homogeneous activations, we find entropy terms that favor inactive neurons, which can lead to neural "heat death" in which equilibrium mass concentrates on networks with vanishing output. We interpret these results through the neural tangent kernel, as a simplicity bias selecting for functions that vary slowly under variations in the parameters.

new FairITR: Fairness-Aware Learning of Multicategory Individualized Treatment Rules

Authors: Zeyu Chen, Wei Qian, Bintong Chen

Abstract: Individualized treatment rules (ITRs) recommend treatment decisions based on individual characteristics and can improve treatment outcomes beyond traditional one-size-fits-all strategies. However, similar to other commonly used algorithmic decision-making methods, ITRs without considering model fairness may result in unfair decisions across groups defined by sensitive attributes, such as race or gender. Although extensive methods have been developed for ITR estimation, most focus solely on optimizing treatment outcomes, while research on fairness-aware ITRs remains limited. Moreover, the practical scenario of multicategory treatments is rarely considered in existing fair ITR studies. To address these gaps, we introduce a fairness notion for multicategory ITRs that requires treatment decisions to be independent of sensitive attributes, and then formulate the corresponding problem of estimating fair ITRs. To solve this problem, we further propose FairITR, a fairness-aware learning framework that jointly optimizes treatment outcomes and fairness. FairITR builds on angle-based classification for multicategory ITR learning and integrates adversarial learning with covariance-based regularization to mitigate unfairness in treatment decisions. A theoretical justification for the fairness properties induced by the adversarial debiasing mechanism is also provided. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets show that FairITR substantially reduces unfairness in ITRs while maintaining competitive treatment values relative to existing baselines.

new SUAF: Spectral Universal Advantage Features for Zero-Shot Policy Improvement

Authors: Huaze Tang, Runqing Miao, Tianren Peng, Lam Nguyen, Chao Wang, Jairong Li, Wenbo Ding

Abstract: Reward-independent representations enable reinforcement learning agents to reuse knowledge across tasks, but limited representation capacity raises three fundamental questions: what statistical dependence should these representations capture, which components of that dependence should be retained for policy improvement, and how does discarding the remaining components affect decision-making? We address these questions through canonical dependence analysis. For a reference policy, we construct an action-centered, positive-lag canonical dependence matrix (CDM) that isolates the additional predictive contribution of the current action beyond the current state. Its singular value decomposition yields Spectral Universal Advantage Features (SUAF), paired features that recover delayed advantage through linear reward projections. We show that the spectrum quantifies mutual information between current action and future state-action visitation conditioned on current state and determines reward specific approximation errors. Retaining the leading modes gives optimal rank-constrained approximations under both isotropic average-case and reward-uniform minimax criteria. Separating the immediate reward contrast also avoids the spectral floor arising from direct compression of the full advantage operator. To learn SUAF from reward-free data, we develop an action-centered Soft-HGR objective with bidirectional temporal-difference estimation. We further connect spectral approximation errors to sufficient conditions for policy improvement. Finite-state experiments validate the spectral predictions and feature-learning method, while zero-shot evaluations on discrete tasks and continuous-control benchmarks demonstrate the utility of the learned representations across rewards.

new The Optimal Sample Complexity of Agnostic PAC Learning for Partial Concept Classes

Authors: Liang-Yu Zou

Abstract: Let $\mathcal H\subseteq\{-1,+1,\star\}^{\mathcal X}$ be a partial concept class of finite VC dimension $d$. We construct a deterministic agnostic PAC learner that, for every $\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)$, uses $O((d+\log(1/\delta))/\varepsilon^2)$ i.i.d. examples from an arbitrary distribution $\mathcal D$ over $\mathcal X\times\{-1,+1\}$ and returns a total concept $\hat h:\mathcal X\to\{-1,+1\}$ satisfying $\text{er}_{\mathcal D}(\hat h)\leq \min_{h\in\mathcal H}\text{er}_{\mathcal D}(h)+\varepsilon$ with probability at least $1-\delta$, where $\text{er}_{\mathcal D}(h)=\mathbb P_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal D}[h(x)\neq y]$. This establishes the optimal sample complexity, matching the lower bound of Alon et al. (FOCS 2021).

new Topology Hub Optimal Transport: Eliminating Long Context Dilution at Flash Speed

Authors: Dylan Forde

Abstract: Long-context models use sparse or structured attention to avoid quadratic cost, yet retrieving a small set of relevant facts embedded among hundreds of thousands of distractors degrades as normalization mass disperses with length. Hub-and-spoke optimal transport (OT) replaces the dense $L \times L$ matrix with a linear-size graph over local windows and $K$ shared hubs balanced by Sinkhorn scaling. Building on block-wise tail refinement and fixed-support transport topology (arXiv:2605.08123, arXiv:2609.07954), we introduce Topology Hub Optimal Transport (THOT): (1) bounded-support capacity gating via simplex sparsemax ($O(L\log L)$) or sorting-free token-local thresholded ReLU ($O(L)$), driving excess distractor mass to zero; (2) a three-sweep $1+2$ annealed row-closed Sinkhorn schedule that damps bipartite residuals; (3) an exact rank-$(D+2R+1)$ One-Reference-Tile adjoint in batched split-factor Triton kernels (BF16 Tensor Cores, FP32 potentials); and (4) a chunked exclusive-prefix causal variant with verified zero future leakage. On NVIDIA L4, sparsemax THOT trained at $L=128$ reaches 97.40% zero-shot recall of fixed bindings at $L=262{,}144$ under pure task loss ($\alpha=0$) and 98.83% at $\alpha=0.05$ (matched by $O(L)$ thresholded ReLU at $\alpha=0.05$, vs. 80.60% at $\alpha=0$), while softmax-gated hub OT drops to 3.52%. In bidirectional operator microbenchmarks, our kernel runs forward+VJP in 100.1 ms at $H=8, D=64, L=262\mathrm{K}$ (2.2x faster with 4.0x smaller compiler temporaries than latent softmax) and 1.34 ms vs. 4.37 ms for cuDNN FlashAttention at $L=4{,}096$; multi-layer LM runs use the unfused path. Zero-shot WikiText-103 multi-needle retrieval hits 100.00% (128/128 queries at $L=65\mathrm{K}$) vs. 0.78% for short-trained RoPE FlashAttention, and compact 4-layer WikiText-4K runs show modest causal and masked LM gains.

new Ranking-Preserving Coreset Selection for Efficient Evolutionary Hyperparameter Optimization

Authors: Mamoona Tasadduq, Maryam Bashir

Abstract: Hyperparameter optimization (HPO) is essential for high predictive performance, but its computational cost becomes prohibitive when candidate configurations require repeated training on large datasets. This paper proposes a ranking-preserving coreset (RPC) selection framework for accelerating evolutionary HPO. The method constructs a compact coreset that preserves the relative ranking of hyperparameter configurations observed on the full training data. Coreset selection is formulated as an evolutionary optimization problem that maximizes Spearman rank correlation between full-data and subset-based configuration rankings while penalizing subset size. The selected coreset is then used for HPO with three evolutionary algorithms: Genetic Algorithm (GA), Cross-generational elitist Selection, Heterogeneous Recombination and Cataclysmic Mutation (CHC), and Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO). Experiments on seven tabular, two text, and two image benchmark datasets show that RPC substantially reduces HPO time while maintaining test accuracy comparable to full-dataset HPO across decision tree, text classification, and image classification tasks. Across datasets and optimizers, HPO-stage speedups range from approximately 1.14x to 8.80x, while the one-time coreset construction cost is accounted for separately through end-to-end runtime and break-even analysis. On 20 held-out configurations excluded from coreset construction, RPC achieves higher ranking correlation than same-size random sampling on 8 of 11 datasets (dataset-level Wilcoxon signed-rank test, p = 0.014). These results show that ranking preservation extends beyond the configurations used during selection. The one-time construction cost can be amortized when the coreset is reused across HPO runs.

new Model Carving: Training Transformers That Optimize Their Own Topology

Authors: Kaden Zheng, Pratyusha Sharma

Abstract: Machine learning has largely kept separate the choice of architecture and the process of training. A classic Transformer, for instance, trains a fixed set of attention and MLP sublayers at every layer, in the same order, and optimizes the weights within. This uniformity is a convention rather than a principled design choice, and it is likely suboptimal: the best architecture for a given task may be smaller, more efficient, and far from uniform. We believe that, ideally, training itself should shape the model's topology. To this end, we introduce Model Carving, which learns both a Transformer's weights and its architecture in a single run. By applying learnable, scalar gates to each sublayer, we allow the training loss to remove the model's own components during training -- freeing a smaller heterogeneous network inside. We evaluate Model Carving by pre-training standard Transformers from 140M to 2.7B parameters and looped Transformers from 140M to 1.3B. At equal training compute, carved models reach a lower validation loss than dense models trained with up to 1.7 times more compute. Furthermore, carved models also outperform size-matched models produced by pruning or distillation, suggesting that the best way to obtain small models for edge devices is to carve them during training, not to compress a large one afterwards. This advantage both grows with scale and carries over to downstream tasks such as few-shot evaluation and generation. Because the removed sublayers no longer run, carved models also cost up to 34% fewer FLOPs per token and decode up to 1.6 times faster without cost in quality. Our results show that architecture, like weights, should be learned and not fixed in advance, and that inside every large transformer is a smaller, faster, stronger one waiting to be carved out.

new On the Complete Feature Set for ADHD Classification in Children and Adolescents Based on EEG Signals

Authors: Javier Sanchis, \'Alvaro Navarro, Jorge Garc\'ia-Carrasco, Tahar Kechadi, Juan Trujillo

Abstract: Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder is a neurodevelopmental condition that can affect attention, activity and impulse control. Electroencephalography (EEG) records brain activity as children and adolescents perform cognitive tasks. However, comparing features derived from these recordings requires consistent signal preparation, shared analysis windows and documented quality decisions. We curated 669 existing BALLADEER recordings from 156 participants across five task conditions so that researchers can compare seven EEG feature families on the same time intervals for each participant and task. The dataset links continuous signals to precomputed measurements from 115 feature definitions or templates and records the quality decisions made for each recording. Analyses must still account for repeated measurements within participants, residual artefacts and differences between acquisition systems. Technical validation covered signal integrity, numerical checks on feature estimators and completeness of the released files. In every task, the median proportion of recording time retained after preprocessing was 100%. To illustrate reuse, we report saved participant-held-out evaluations comparing XGBoost using all seven families on eight-second windows with a predictor based only on recruitment-group prevalence in the training fold. Recruitment-group discrimination was stronger in Robots than in the three Slackline conditions. These descriptive results from overlapping cohorts do not establish diagnostic validity. The dataset, curation software and aggregate results for the predictive example are publicly available.

new A Methodologically Rigorous Benchmark of Linear Regression,RNN, and LSTM for S&P 500 Forecasting:Identifying Sources of Performance Inflation in Financial DeepLearning

Authors: Hari Sitaula, Joseph Opitz

Abstract: This study examines one-day-ahead forecasting of the S&P 500 closing level using a 2006-2025 dataset that includes fundamental price data, macroeconomic indicators, and engineered technical features. We benchmark three model families, namely linear regression, vanilla recurrent neural networks (RNN), and long short-term memory networks (LSTM), within a unified preprocessing and chronological evaluation pipeline. A central contribution of this work is a methodological audit of common practices in the deep-learning-for-stock-prediction literature. We identify three sources of result inflation that frequently appear in published methodologies: wavelet-denoised evaluation targets, the absence of a naive persistence baseline, and best-of-N replicate reporting. We then re-evaluate all model families under a corrected protocol that controls for each. Performance is reported using root mean squared error, mean absolute percentage error, and the Pearson correlation coefficient, averaged across N = 30 independent replicates per configuration. We compare models on accuracy, stability across replicates, and architectural depth sensitivity, using the naive persistence forecast (predicting today's close as tomorrow's close) as a primary baseline against which all candidate models must demonstrate improvement.

Authors: Marta Niedostatek, Ferran Hernandez Caralt, Runyue Wang, Federica Baccini, Lorenzo Giambagli, Pietro Li\'o, Ginestra Bianconi

Abstract: Topological Deep Learning (TDL) is designed to learn features associated not only with nodes but also with edges, triangles and higher dimensional simplices. However, most TDL approaches, being based on boundary operators and Hodge Laplacians, have the limitation that features and signals cannot be lifted or lowered across more than one dimension per layer.
