cross To predict or to persist: how decoherence shapes learning in a spin-qubit quantum reservoir

Authors: Nikolaos Petropoulos, Elena Blokhina

Abstract: In this work we investigate how dephasing and the choice of measured observables affect learning in a simulated six-qubit spin chain with Markovian dissipation and exact expectation-value readout. For externally driven nonlinear autoregressive moving-average signals, combining single- and two-qubit observables gives better predictions than a population-only readout at comparable polynomial feature counts. Most of the configurations we tested become more accurate than a reference with relaxation and dephasing disabled, most strongly at $T_2=1\,\mu$s, where total memory capacity drops, and still at $T_2=5$-$10\,\mu$s, where memory is only redistributed across delays. For autonomous generation, we train the readout with scheduled sampling and iterative dataset aggregation to expose it to states reached under its own feedback. We do not find a clear systematic dependence of limit-cycle persistence on coherence across the reservoir ensemble. For chaotic forecasting, in contrast, prediction horizons at $T_2=1\,\mu$s shorten in all 10 reservoir realizations relative to that reference. For the noise-driven strange non-chaotic attractor, the model produces a smooth, approximately closed orbit, and neither captures the target's scatter nor follows its trajectory pointwise. We conclude that, within this simulated reservoir, the impact of coherence depends strongly on the task.

cross SDG: State-Dependent Gating at Chunk Boundaries for Linear Attention

Authors: Renjia Deng, Yuxi Liu, Yutong He, Xue Wang, Kun Yuan

Abstract: Recent gated linear attention models use state-independent gates, computed from token representations without directly reading the recurrent memory. This design supports efficient chunkwise computation: sequences are divided into chunks of consecutive tokens, with parallel computation within each chunk. However, the same token representation produces the same gate, even when the layer stores different information. We introduce State-Dependent Gating (SDG), which computes element-wise gates from the change in memory over a chunk and applies them at the chunk boundary. This provides state feedback for forgetting while preserving parallel computation within each chunk. We develop fused kernels for Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, Gated DeltaNet-2, and Mamba-3. Our theoretical analysis quantifies the potential reconstruction benefits of state-dependent, element-wise gating under a signal--noise model. We also show that boundary decay is an element-wise ridge proximal step when the gate is held fixed. Empirical evaluation of 1.3B linear attention and hybrid models trained on 100B tokens shows improvements across language modeling, commonsense reasoning, synthetic and real-world retrieval, and long-context understanding. These results demonstrate that SDG is an effective approach to enhancing existing linear attention models.

cross Distributional Deep Hedging

Authors: El-Amine Cherrat, Skander Kazdaghli, Iordanis Kerenidis

Abstract: Deep hedging learns a hedging strategy by optimising a risk preference over a neural-network policy. The preference is fixed before training, so each new preference requires a new policy and, in actor-critic methods, a new critic. We propose distributional deep hedging, in which the distribution of the hedging profit and loss is learned through its quantiles and the risk preference is applied afterwards, as a weight on those quantiles. We characterise the preferences that admit this representation, which include expected shortfall and its mixtures, and show that the entropic utility is computed from the same quantiles. At the current policy, each of these preferences is bounded below by an expected utility with the same gradient. This bound reduces risk-sensitive hedging to a sequence of risk-neutral problems, from which we derive policy-search and actor-critic algorithms. In numerical experiments, the learned hedges approach references computed by dynamic programming, and a single distributional critic trained without a preference can serve preferences chosen after training.

cross SparseHPS: A Unified Model for Human Pose and Shape across Depth Cameras and LiDARs

Authors: Ilya Zaychuk

Abstract: Recovering 3D human pose and shape from point clouds is central to motion capture, robotics and self-driving vehicles, because depth cameras and LiDARs sense a person directly in metric 3D and work under any lighting. However, current approaches are each tailored to one sensor type: point density differs by more than ten times between devices, public datasets are labeled with incompatible body models, and LiDAR approaches need long input sequences to offset sparse scans. In this work, we present SparseHPS, a novel sensor-agnostic network that recovers SMPL-X pose, shape and global translation from a human point cloud with one set of weights, for single frames and streams alike. A single model is trained on ten datasets that mix rendered clouds with real depth-camera and LiDAR captures, all converted to one body model. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art results on the SLOPER4D and FreeMotion LiDAR benchmarks, where SparseHPS lowers the root-relative joint error by 22% and 11%, respectively, compared with the strongest prior method, which needs 32 frames. cross Pharos: Learning to Control Rare Events at Scale

Authors: Jiajun He, Jacky Chen, Weiliang Luo, Heather Kulik, David R. Koes, Carles Domingo-Enrich, Yuanqi Du

Abstract: Rare events drive transformations across physics, chemistry and biology, yet predicting their mechanisms and rates remains a fundamental challenge. Here we introduce Pharos, a framework that learns to make rare transitions accessible while predicting how frequently they occur under physical dynamics. Through stochastic optimal control, Pharos jointly learns the probability of completing a transition and how to steer simulations toward it, allowing sampling and learning to improve one another. Across biomolecular benchmarks, Pharos learns accurate transition probabilities with order-of-magnitude gains in convergence speed over previous algorithms. By combining efficient sampling, quantitative kinetics, and transferability, Pharos opens a route toward foundation models of rare event mechanisms and rates across molecular systems.

cross CADENCE:Temporally Coherent Whole-Body Human Mesh Recovery that Preserves Expressive Hand Motion

Authors: Keniel Peart, Jagmohan Chauhan, Indu P. Bodala, Shelly Vishwakarma

Abstract: Whole-body SMPL-X estimators recover body, hand, and face meshes from single images, but applied frame by frame to video they produce jittery motion. cross Memorization and Generalization in Generative Models: A Perspective From Energy-based Modeling and Associative Memory

Authors: Bao Pham

Abstract: Generative models now write working code, produce realistic images, and hold long conversations, yet the mechanisms behind their intelligence still evade our understanding. This thesis explores that puzzle through Associative Memory (AM), in which patterns are stored as energy minima and retrieved through attractor dynamics. Beyond their capacity, AMs form new minima known as spurious states, long regarded as a failure of memory. We argue that this failure is where creativity begins, and that memorization and generalization can coexist as outcomes of one retrieval process. First, we show that diffusion models behave as AMs. With little data, a diffusion model forms a distinct basin of attraction around each training sample. As the data grows, these basins shrink, and spurious states emerge as attractors that blend stored samples yet match none of them. They are the first signs of generative capability. We characterize this transition by measuring the volume, energy, and curvature of these basins across datasets and architectures, including Stable Diffusion. We then turn to language and move beyond the energy-based view of AM. We show that uniform-state discrete diffusion models are AMs capable of retrieving unseen data, with basins of attraction that arise from conditional likelihood maximization alone. As the training set grows, basins around training examples contract while basins around unseen examples expand, until corrupted test sentences are recovered as reliably as training ones. Conditional entropy exposes this coexistence within a single sequence and cheaply diagnoses where a model lies along the transition. Together, these results suggest that AM is a general principle defined by basins of attraction, and that memorization and generalization can coexist within one model. AM thus offers a framework for asking when a generative model recalls and when it creates.

cross Search From the Start: Search-Native Pretraining for Language Models

Authors: Kaustubh Ponkshe, Gyung Hyun Je, Stefan Taga, Colin Raffel, Martin Jaggi

Abstract: Modern language models (LMs) are increasingly deployed with access to search tools at inference. Yet pretraining remains predominantly focused on predicting static text, with search capabilities typically introduced during post-training. We propose Search-Native Pretraining as a practical route to teach search capability during pretraining from ordinary text corpora. Specifically, we augment pretraining corpus at scale with web-search queries and retrieve web response using a local index. Training on this augmented corpus teaches models to generate search calls and incorporate retrieved evidence during language modeling. Across Wikipedia and DCLM-Edu corpora and 360M to 3.3B parameter scales, search-pretrained models ("QueryLM") show improved performance on knowledge-intensive tasks and higher steerability toward the retrieved evidence. Crucially, their advantage persists after supervised fine-tuning of both search-pretrained and conventionally pretrained models, indicating that the tested post-training procedures do not fully recover the capabilities developed during pretraining. Throughout our experiments, we find that a 1.4B QueryLM consistently beats or matches the performance of a FLOP-matched 3.3B baseline model across all knowledge intensive tasks. These results establish search-pretraining as a practical way to develop foundational search capabilities during pretraining. We release the training artifacts, including model checkpoints and data.

cross Skill-JEPA: Learning Planner-Aligned Action Spaces for World Models

Authors: Tom\'as Couso Coddou, Christ Devia, Rex G. Liu, Rodrigo Toro Icarte

Abstract: World models based on Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) learn from pixels to predict the outcome of actions in a latent space. To reach a goal, a planner samples many candidate action sequences, uses the model to predict where each one leads, and executes the best one. However, standard planners draw their candidates from a Gaussian, while the model has only seen the structured, state-dependent actions of its training data. Its predictions for such candidates are unreliable, and planning fails more often as goals move farther away. We propose Skill-JEPA, which learns an action space aligned with the planner. A variational autoencoder conditioned on the current state encodes short action sequences into latent skills with a standard Gaussian prior, and decodes them back into actions. The world model predicts the effect of skills rather than raw actions, and the planner searches over skills starting from that same Gaussian, so its candidates resemble the model's training data. Skill-JEPA adds a single module, trained end-to-end with the world model, and leaves the planner untouched, with no subgoals, hierarchy, or hand-designed skills. Across three JEPA world models and four benchmarks, average success on goals 80 steps away rises from 42% with raw actions to 62%; the gains grow with goal distance and hold across four planners. On AntMaze, a quadruped locomotion task, success on goals two maze cells away rises from 0.4% to 38%. This suggests that part of the difficulty of reaching distant goals lies in the action space, and can be removed without changing the planner.

Authors: Eric Todd, Tal Haklay, Nikhil Prakash, Matthew Finlayson, Michael Hanna, Fenil R. Doshi, Daniel Wurgaft, Thomas McGrath, Owen Lewis, David Bau, Jack Merullo, Ekdeep Singh Lubana, Matthew Kowal, Thomas Fel, Atticus Geiger

Abstract: We investigate whether the geometric structure of neural representations can provide a sufficient learning signal for improving model behavior without any supervision on model outputs. cross LoCo-WAM: Test-Time Training as Long Context for World-Action Models

Authors: Sheng Cheng, Deqian Kong

Abstract: World-action models guide robot actions by predicting what happens next, but typically condition on a short observation history. Extending this history increases computation at every decision and can require a separately trained model. cross LLMs Memorize Their Own Hallucinations During Post-Training

Authors: Anirudh Atmakuru, Tanya Goyal

Abstract: Reasoning domains have shaped the post-training recipes of frontier models even though they are trained and deployed in domains beyond math and code. In this paper, we study the consequences of directly adopting reasoning-centric training recipes, e.g., the use of outcome-based rewards for reinforcement learning (RL), injection of "cognitive" behaviors such as proposing and backtracking from hypotheses in reasoning chains, etc., for knowledge tasks. We hypothesize that these decisions, while useful for reasoning, inadvertently encourage models to memorize hallucinations they themselves generate in their long reasoning chains when trained on knowledge tasks. To study this, we train models with standard post-training, extract their self-generated hallucinations during RL, and track memorization throughout the rest of training. Our experiments on six open models across two model families and two architectures confirm our hypothesis. We find that models' recall of hallucinations reinforced during training increases by 4.83% on average, compared to just 0.17% for self-hallucinations that receive no training signal. In fact, models even memorize false facts that they immediately retract in their reasoning; a backtracking template inherited from reasoning-centric training that has no benefit for knowledge recall. Furthermore, we find that memorization is strongest on the hardest training questions, and rewarding a hallucination makes the model less likely to abstain from it later (by 7.23% on average). Overall, our work identifies and systematically studies a previously unexamined pathway through which models learn hallucinations during training.

cross How Language Models Compress and Compare: Understanding Selection with Token Covariance Maps

Authors: Moshe Eliasof

Abstract: Many language-model tasks reduce to selecting among structured alternatives, yet we still lack a simple picture of how a decoder-only transformer organizes an entire selection prompt across depth. We introduce token covariance maps, a prompt-level geometric view that tracks how token positions co-vary in hidden-state space at each layer. This turns a structured prompt into a depth-indexed sequence of maps whose spectral and block structure can be aligned with instructions, context, and candidate answers. Across 14 decoder-only models from 4 families and 12 structured-selection benchmarks, token covariance maps reveal a robust compress-then-compare organization. Early layers represent prompts diffusely, middle layers compress them into a shared low-rank bottleneck, and late layers re-expand into context-option and option-option geometry. This organization recurs across larger fixed-template samples, deterministic prompt rewrites, pairwise reranking reformulations, and controlled answer-set manipulations, while also emerging during training from an initially flat depth profile. Its timing aligns with behavior: useful answer discrimination appears well after the bottleneck, and low-rank intervention around the bottleneck preferentially disrupts late comparison geometry. We additionally show that the same geometric profile appears in instruction-tuned models, across several languages, and during open-ended generation. We further show that changing the internal representation of one candidate around the compressed region can shift which candidate the model later prefers. These results show that token covariance maps are a diagnostic tool for comparing how models organize information across prompts, model families, and stages of training.

cross Your Judge Inherits Your Prompt: In-Context Label Bias in an LLM-as-Judge Is Directional, Not Symmetric

Authors: Abdullah Ahmad, Ahmed J. O. Ahmed, Luca Citi

Abstract: LLM-as-judge has become a standard way to score open-ended model outputs, yet the judge is itself a frozen language model steered by a few in-context examples, and such models are known to absorb the label balance of their demonstrations rather than treat them as neutral illustrations. We ask whether this label bias reaches an LLM judge used inside a multimodal (video question answering) evaluation pipeline, and what form it takes. Using a fully frozen generator-judge pipeline, we hold the generated answers fixed and vary only the Yes/No balance of the judge's few-shot examples across four demonstration sets. Each verdict is validated against a two-annotator human consensus on 200 examples per dataset. The judge's acceptance rate stays close to human consensus when its examples are balanced or skewed toward rejection, but jumps sharply (by about 30 points on NExT-QA and 20 on MSVD-QA, with a strongly one-sided error profile) when the examples are skewed toward acceptance; the opposite skew produces no matching drop. The bias is therefore one-directional within the tested range of label ratios. A separate five-draw analysis shows that results from a single example draw are unreliable, varying by up to 18 points, more than any difference between prompting conditions. We conclude that demonstration label bias in an LLM judge is a directional, inflation-only failure mode that can fabricate apparent benchmark gains but not losses, and that it is cheaply avoided by balancing the judge's examples and reporting results averaged over several draws.

cross Perceive Once, Escalate on Demand: Handing Off Visual KV Memory from Small to Large VLMs

Authors: Qiang Zhou, Jianing Deng, Siyan Dong, Jingxian Chen, Han-Yi Lin, Jingtong Hu

Abstract: An embodied agent cannot afford a large vision-language model (VLM) at every step of a long video, so a cascade runs a small VLM continuously and escalates only uncertain decisions to a large one. Each escalation, however, forces the large model to re-encode a history the small model has already perceived, and this dominates its cost. We present Baton, which hands the large model the small model's visual memory, its key-value (KV) cache over the history. Baton rests on one finding: the large model needs a usable memory, not a faithful copy of its own. A map trained to reproduce the large model's cache matches it most closely, yet brings no gain over the small model alone. Baton instead distills a per-layer linear map, without labels, from the large model's own answers, with the large model encoding only the current frame. Interventions on the map trace the gain to one direction, carried by the middle third of the large model's layers. On RoboMME, Baton beats the small model alone, a pruned or shortened re-watch, and a text memory; as the escalation channel of a cascade it matches re-watching to within about one point at 28-47% less compute, and 8x less per escalation at 64 frames. Its gain grows with the large model, from 8B to 32B, tracks the large model's headroom across eight model pairs from four families, and transfers zero-shot to OpenEQA.

cross Budget-Aware Predict-and-Verify Theorem Proving with Heterogeneous LLM Solvers

Authors: Wei Jiang, Ziran Liu, Wei Wang

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) theorem solvers differ in both proving capability and inference cost. Applying an expensive solver to every subgoal wastes computation when cheaper models are sufficient, while always starting from the cheapest solver wastes attempts on subgoals that require stronger models. We present BudgetPV, an agentic budget-aware predict-and-verify system that allocates computation across unresolved subgoals and heterogeneous LLM solvers. BudgetPV uses a cross-solver predictor to guide solver selection for each subgoal and revises the allocation as verification feedback becomes available. The allocation problem changes as proving progresses. Early decisions mainly determine which solver should handle an untried subgoal, while later decisions determine which previously attempted subgoals should receive additional computation. We train the predictor with two objectives for these two stages. Class-weighted cross-entropy improves assignment to less frequent stronger solvers during the initial stage, while plain cross-entropy provides better calibrated probabilities for later allocation among previously attempted subgoals. We evaluate BudgetPV on Lean Workbook, miniF2F, ProofNet, and PutnamBench using three progressively expanded solver settings: a strong but expensive solver alone, adding a medium-cost solver with intermediate solving capability, and further adding a cheap solver with lower solving capability. BudgetPV requires less LLM generation computation than uniform allocation to solve the same number of subgoals across the evaluated settings. The experiments further show the computation savings obtained as medium- and lower-cost solvers are added.

cross Surface Cues Over Structure: Probing LLM Behavior on Brain Connectivity Graphs

Authors: Prapti Roy, Khaled Mohammed Saifuddin

Abstract: Resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (rs-fMRI) provides a noninvasive view of functional interactions across the brain, which can be represented as connectivity graphs for neurological disease analysis. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being paired with such representations, yet it remains unclear whether they genuinely reason over the underlying connectivity or simply respond to surface-level cues that may trigger learned priors. We probe this question through a controlled study of Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) diagnosis from resting-state functional connectivity, isolating one factor at a time to determine what drives an LLM's predictions on brain connectivity graphs. Across six models, from small open-weight LLMs to a proprietary one, the same pattern holds: the results provide little evidence that predictions are consistently grounded in subject-specific connectivity. Brain region names, numeric formatting, and prompt framing shift what a model predicts without improving how well it separates ADHD subjects from Controls; perturbing the graph changes predictions no more for clinically meaningful regions than for random ones; and the models' own justifications contradict the values they cite, inconsistently interpreting the connectivity evidence provided to them. Across every probe, predictions follow the model's built-in class bias and the surface form of the prompt rather than the subject's connectivity, calling into question a premise underlying current LLM brain integration work. Our code is available at https://github.com/Roy025/ADHD-code.

URLs: https://github.com/Roy025/ADHD-code.

cross What Does the Visual Encoder Buy? Visual Information Gain in Encoder-Free Multimodal Pretraining

Authors: Zhixuan Ge, Anqi Li, Wei Qiu

Abstract: Recent scaling studies ask whether vision-language models still need a visual encoder by comparing encoder-free and encoder-based models on the next-token loss of the text in image-text data. This loss can fall without any use of the image, and two runs with the same loss can differ in what they take from it. Visual information gain (VIG) is the loss increase when the image is replaced by a uniform one. It splits exactly into global information, carried by image statistics, and layout information, which shuffling the patches within the image destroys. We apply it to decoders with 158M to 1.04B parameters that read pixel patches through a linear embedding, evaluated every billion tokens up to 40B, and to 19 public checkpoints. In our decoders the image lowers the loss by 3% to 46%, depending on the image type. Global information is present after the first billion tokens. Layout information starts near zero, grows slowly with training and does not grow with model size on five of six image types, and a larger batch reaches the same loss with less of it. With a frozen SigLIP 2 encoder in front of the same decoder the loss is lower only when the arrangement of the image is intact. On shuffled images the losses with and without the encoder agree to within 0.04 nats on five of six image types, so the net advantage of the encoder in the loss is layout information, above all the ability to read text in images. Public encoder-free models at production scale (Gemma 4 12B, NEO, Mono-InternVL) do acquire layout information. On the same documents they have 0.53 to 0.76 nats of it, against 0.65 to 0.82 for recent encoder-based models and 0.04 to 0.07 for our decoders, and their released training stages show it rising. These results agree with the reported parity of the two families when both are trained from scratch and place the reported advantage of a pretrained encoder in layout information.

cross ERA: Embed, Route, Adapt for Cold-Start Cross-Model Latent Retrieval

Authors: Venkata Chivukula, Zifeng Wang, Jinsung Yoon

Abstract: Production retrieval systems are tied to the embedding model that indexed their corpus. Moving queries to a different encoder normally requires re-embedding the whole corpus, and at cold start there are no query logs while every document embedded by the new encoder incurs an API or compute cost. We present ERA (Embed, Route, Adapt), a system for cold-start cross-model latent retrieval. ERA spends a small anchor budget on maximally informative documents, selected from the existing target index via Cosine Farthest Point Sampling, trains a lightweight adapter with a Manifold Alignment Loss that couples directional alignment with orthogonal regularization to prevent representation collapse, and pairs the adapter with a deterministic router that escalates the rare, hard queries that define perceived retrieval quality. Across five BEIR datasets, ERA matches or outperforms point-wise L2 and embedding-conversion baselines, with relative gains of up to 35% nDCG@10 on Arguana, and on clustered corpora its anchor selection also improves prior methods under a fixed anchor budget. With 1.2 ms of adapter and router overhead on top of a ~20 ms local encoder, ERA at a 20% nominal escalation budget reduces end-to-end query latency from roughly 160 ms to 38-86 ms while concentrating escalations on the queries where the projection fails most.

cross SecretAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Privacy Protection against Vision Language Models

Authors: Minda Zhao, Yue Zhang, Mengyu Wang

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can infer private information from shared images, including locations, personally identifiable information (PII), and personal attributes such as age and sex, even from medical images. Adversarial perturbations can protect such information against black-box VLMs, but existing methods overlook image quality and introduce visible artifacts into the images they protect. We present SecretAgent, the first general multi-agent framework that protects image privacy against VLMs while preserving visual fidelity. LLM agents iteratively locate the visual evidence that reveals private information and adapt an invisible perturbation to the target VLM's answers and to an appearance reviewer agent, which rejects any candidate with visible artifacts. We instantiate SecretAgent with two complementary variants: SecretAgent-Gradient optimizes the perturbation through six surrogate encoders for cross-model transferability, and the gradient-free SecretAgent-Tool composes procedural noise and achieves the best perceptual fidelity on three of the five datasets. Experiments on four privacy applications (geolocation, PII, facial, and medical) across five datasets and six VLMs show that SecretAgent preserves image quality substantially better than existing methods while achieving state-of-the-art privacy protection in every application, and that its protection transfers to other VLMs.

cross Spectral Estimation with Deformed Decompression

Authors: Siavash Ameli, Chris van der Heide, Liam Hodgkinson, Michael W. Mahoney

Abstract: Sample covariance matrices are fundamental objects in machine learning and statistics, with valuable information encoded into their eigenvalues. When treating modern problems, the scale of these matrices can become prohibitive for tractable computation. This creates a critical need for "spectral decompression": a method for inferring the eigenspectrum of a very large-scale model using only information from its smaller realizations. Existing methods fall into two main categories: those relying on spectral inversion; and those that rely on Free Decompression techniques. On the one hand, spectral inversion is a fundamentally ill-posed problem that is notoriously unstable, often failing to yield reliable results in finite-size settings. On the other hand, while Free Decompression was recently proposed to address this scaling, its reliance on the Nica-Speicher framework restricts its use to unitarily invariant ensembles, a symmetry condition that subsampled covariance matrices fail to satisfy. In this work, we introduce Deformed Decompression (DD), a novel framework for the stable, forward-extrapolation of spectral densities. By leveraging properties of the companion matrix, DD recasts the spectral decompression problem as a directed evolution of the Stieltjes transform of the companion matrix. This approach bypasses the instabilities of traditional inversion and provides a robust methodology for scaling covariance matrices and even their generalized variants (including Hessian matrices) that lack unitary invariance. We demonstrate the utility of our framework through experiments on a variety of random matrix examples, showing that DD successfully captures the complex spectral signatures of large-scale systems.

cross Run One, Get Two: Near-Free Streaming Safety Checks for Local LLMs

Authors: Soheil Zibakhsh, Pedram Aghazadeh, Farinaz Koushanfar

Abstract: Local LLM assistants decode for one user at a time, so decoding is bound by memory bandwidth and leaves compute idle. Streaming safety checks today trade accuracy against cost. A dedicated guard model is accurate but nearly doubles the latency per token, while a hidden-state probe is cheap but less accurate. We present ToRA, an adapter that runs a second model as an extra row in the generator's decode step, sharing its weights and KV cache. Dual-row kernels read each weight once for both rows. On Qwen3-0.6B, ToRA matches or beats a dedicated streaming guard, with higher F1 on 12 of 17 benchmarks. It detects 11.8 points more unsafe responses than the probe at a similar cost, under 4% added latency per token.

cross Loop-OPF: Solver-Informed Learning for Optimal Power Flow with Inference-Time Topology Adaptivity

Authors: Mohamad Chehade, Hao Zhu

Abstract: Learning for optimal power flow (OPF) has recently been advocated to accelerate OPF solutions by using a pre-trained neural network (NN) to predict directly from given inputs such as the load demand. However, existing learning-for-OPF methods largely fall short of ensuring the optimality and feasibility of their predictions. They also struggle to adapt at inference time to unseen topology scenarios, such as those caused by line or generator outages. This work puts forth Loop-OPF, a solver-informed NN that builds its layers from the iterations of the primal-dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) method. Under standard convexity and regularity conditions on DC-OPF, the resultant fixed-point output of Loop-OPF is thus optimal and feasible. To speed up PDHG across diverse OPF scenarios, Loop-OPF learns certain PDHG parameters in a self-supervised manner, without any OPF solution labels. We adopt the deep equilibrium (DEQ) framework for training so that the memory cost does not grow with the number of layers. As the grid topology enters every layer as an input, the same trained Loop-OPF directly adapts to unseen contingencies without retraining. Numerical tests on the IEEE 300-bus and PEGASE 1354-, 2869-, and 9241-bus networks have demonstrated the accuracy and topology adaptivity of the proposed Loop-OPF over competing learning-for-OPF methods.

cross Integral Multipliers and Arithmetic Obstructions for Two-Hidden-Layer ReLU Maxima

Authors: Pengcheng Cheng

Abstract: We study exact representations of maximum functions by ReLU networks with two hidden layers and integer parameters. The least positive output multiplier is two for the six-input maximum, and either two or six for the seven-input maximum. We obtain these bounds from integral cubical fillings and a coefficient-preserving passage from polytope identities to networks. For the coordinate-facet complex of the six-simplex, we compute a coning index of six over the integers and three after inverting two, allowing arbitrary integral directions within each facet. A class in the homology of the unrestricted kernel complex characterizes the remaining seven-input problem; its vanishing is equivalent to an integral filling of a fixed target. We also construct a finite character that separates rational from three-local representability in a dictionary containing two known constructions and all pairs of edge-disjoint three-edge forests. This obstruction permits nonsymmetric coefficients and is confined to the specified dictionary. Control of lower-dimensional remainders yields a sufficient criterion in the top-dimensional quotient. Exact certificates and verification programs accompany the finite calculations.

cross A Continuous Relaxation of Quantization-Aware Training

Authors: Kartikeya Bhardwaj, Mohammed Almousa, Chaitanya Mullapudi, Ananth Balasubramanian, Nithin Ramesan, Tuc Nguyen, Harris Teague

Abstract: Quantization is a critical piece of the deployment pipeline in the era of large generative models. Low-bit quantization, particularly 2-bits or sub-2-bits, is a notoriously difficult problem, particularly when using a channelwise quantization scheme which has been shown as a leading candidate for hardware-efficiency. The key challenges with low-bit quantization range from limited quantization levels to saddle points or flat regions in the loss surface. In this paper, we look at the problem of flat quantization loss surfaces through a lens of dynamically changing bitwidth. Unlike existing techniques which either change the bit-precision abruptly or keep it fixed, we propose to gradually alter the bitwidth as the model trains. cross Rethinking Cross-Sensor Domain Adaptation for Bridge Point Cloud Semantic Segmentation

Authors: Linghui Huang, Zipeng Wang, Shunjie Chen, Peng Guan, Jiandong Wang, Siqing Li, Luming Yang

Abstract: Segmentation models for bridge point clouds lose accuracy when trained on terrestrial laser scanning (TLS) data and applied to mobile laser scanning (MLS) data. Adaptation results on this benchmark therefore depend on representation scale, source-model convergence, and evaluation variability.

cross Weight-space programming and mechanistic interpretability for model editing in particle physics

Authors: Rapha\"el Bonnet-Guerrini, Johann Ioannou-Nikolaides, Troels Petersen

Abstract: We demonstrate that weight-space programming and edits guided by sparse autoencoders can remove unwanted aspects of a particle-physics foundation model through direct edits to its weights. In a foundation model trained on jets, we observe that adding a single soft neutral particle causes large changes in jet classification, violating infrared safety. We locate the responsible attention heads with mechanistic analysis and trace the flaw's origin to pretraining on datasets with different reconstruction thresholds. cross Writing Weights or Training Them? Spatial Reasoning through Weight-Space Programming and Low-Rank Adaptation

Authors: Yalin Yang, Xiao Huang, Yanan Wu, Hao Yang, Ryan Zhenqi Zhou

Abstract: Weight-space programming lets a language model inspect and edit another model's weights, offering an alternative to gradient-based adaptation. The comparison reveals a trade-off between improving spatial task performance and preserving neighbouring skills: the public edit restored completion while preserving distance performance, whereas supervised adaptation produced much larger target gains together with interference across tasks.

cross Reasoning in Two Dimensions: Quantifying Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness and Efficiency

Authors: Chenruo Liu, Quan Zhang, Qi Lei

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning exposes intermediate steps, but a correct answer does not establish that an LLM relies on the dependence expressed by those steps or reasons efficiently. We operationalize CoT faithfulness and efficiency within a common framework. Specifically, we represent a CoT as a semantic reasoning graph and decompose the model's predictive dependence along two complementary axes: dependence explained by semantic support versus additional reasoning history, and dependence allocated to answer-reaching versus off-path reasoning. This decomposition yields global and step-level measures of unfaithfulness and inefficiency. Controlled interventions show that the proposed metrics respond to hidden dependence and induced off-path reasoning as intended. Across mathematical and multi-hop reasoning tasks, answer-level unfaithfulness is consistently associated with greater predictive uncertainty, whereas both unfaithfulness and inefficiency exhibit weak or inconsistent associations with answer correctness. We further derive theoretically grounded, graph-aware fine-tuning objectives that improve the targeted reasoning properties without directly optimizing the computationally expensive metrics or compromising answer accuracy. Results indicate that accuracy and brevity provide incomplete assessments of reasoning quality: reliable CoT evaluation should also consider whether the LLM's predictive dependence is faithfully represented and efficiently allocated.

cross Highly Efficient Vertical Federated Clustering with Triangle-Inequality-based Pruning

Abstract: In many real-world scenarios, multiple parties hold complementary feature subsets for the same samples but cannot share raw data. cross CTRL: Concurrent Training and Rollout for LLM Reinforcement Learning via GPU Co-Residency

Authors: Ruijia Yang, Ruikai Li, Zeyi Wen

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training of large language models runs two GPU workloads with different hardware demands: rollout decoding, which is dominated by memory access and whose batch shrinks as responses finish, and a dense learner. Current systems either time-share every GPU between them, paying serial phases and switches, or split GPUs into pools whose sizes change only in whole devices. We present CTRL, which makes co-residency a third RL execution layout: every GPU runs a learner rank beside a persistent rollout engine, sharing the device under CUDA MPS. A host-owned learner makes the layout fit: optimizer state stays in host memory, and policy weights stream layer by layer through a bounded GPU window. A publication gate trains each ready batch first and waits only before releasing the next policy, keeping a dropless staleness bound. A step-time bound guides compute-share selection, and a GPU-time ledger explains overlap and sharing costs. On H100, A800, and RTX 4090 GPUs with Qwen3 models, CTRL achieves 1.11-2.24$\times$ the throughput of the stronger of verl's asynchronous baselines across 24 workload settings with the staleness limit set to two, and 1.23$\times$ verl's colocate_async on eight H100 GPUs. With the same learner, co-residency is 1.10-1.26$\times$ faster than time-sharing on all three platforms and 1.10-1.30$\times$ faster than the best measured whole-GPU split on H100 DAPO-Math workloads at 256-528 trajectories per step, on four and eight GPUs.

cross Spatiotemporal Landslide Runout Reconstruction from Sparse Observations with LAPIS-SHRED

Authors: Xingyue Zhang, Yuxuan Bao, Jeffrey Keck, Erkan Istanbulluoglu, J. Nathan Kutz

Abstract: Reconstructing the spatiotemporal evolution of landslide runout from limited observations is a central challenge in geohazard assessment: transient flow dynamics are rarely observed directly, and available data are often confined to post-event deposits or brief monitoring windows. cross Git History Is a Pangenome: Repetition in Code Training Corpora and What Deduplication Removes, Keeps and Misses

Authors: Saurabh Chopra

Abstract: Code models are increasingly trained on the full history of software repositories, every version of every file, rather than on a single snapshot. The tools that deduplicate training data were built for snapshots, and what they do to a history has not been measured. We measured it on a pre-registered random sample of 306 public repositories, 25 mature projects, and the 12 repositories behind the SWE-bench benchmark. A history is mostly repetition: in the vocabulary of text indexing, a pangenome, one text in many near-identical variants. The history of a typical repository holds the equivalent of 19 copies of its current code, and a mature repository holds 89. The reason is that each edit changes a few lines and leaves the rest of the file in place: nine tokens in ten of a changed file already existed before the change. Exact-match deduplication removes none of these copies, since no two versions are identical. Large training pipelines already deduplicate their snapshots by grouping near-identical files and keeping one. Applied across versions, the same step brings the repetition down to about two copies. But two problems remain. The deduplicator keeps one version of each file, and in widely used implementations, including the one we measured, the rule for choosing that version ignores dates, so for half of all files the version kept for training is an old one. Sorting versions newest first before deduplicating fixes the problem at almost no cost. The second problem is benchmark answers. Benchmarks such as SWE-bench test a model on real bug fixes, and a merged fix survives in a median of 18 later versions of the files it changed. Therefore, removing benchmark fixes needs a separate step that checks every version. We turn the measurements into a short set of rules for teams that train on code history, and release the instruments and frozen outputs.

To address these issues, we propose a systematic taxonomy and hierarchical evaluation framework for SAR-related foundation models: five rating levels, six dimensions, and fine-grained criteria, with separate protocols for VFMs and VLMs. Beyond overall ratings, it yields interpretable multidimensional capability profiles and supports user-defined weighting, so rankings follow user priorities. cross FedQCal: Quadratic Programming with Dual-Space Calibration for One-Shot Federated Learning

Authors: Shunsei Koshibuchi, Hiroshi Kera, Kazuhiko Kawamoto

Abstract: One-shot federated learning must combine heterogeneous client models after a single upload. Different local class distributions make it difficult to decide which client updates to retain. We propose FedQCal, which uses a reference model trained on a small labeled server set to define alignment requirements for aggregation. The key idea is to retain sufficient reference alignment while avoiding unnecessarily large aggregation coefficients. A minimum-norm quadratic program (QP) enforces these requirements. cross A Frugal AI Framework for India: Responsible and Right-sized AI

Authors: Balaraman Ravindran, K. S. Venkataraghavan, Gokul S Krishnan, Balaji Srinivasan, Balkrishna C. Rao

Abstract: India's AI debate often swings between two insufficient options: replicating the compute-intensive, capital-heavy foundation-model programmes of the United States or China, or remaining a downstream adopter of foreign AI with localised interfaces and safeguards. Neither serves a country that must deliver AI to a very large, diverse population across uneven infrastructure and within constrained budgets. This white paper from the Centre for Responsible AI (CeRAI), IIT Madras, proposes a third path: Responsible Frugal AI, which applies principle-based frugal engineering alongside Responsible AI. Responsible Frugal AI is right-sized AI: it fixes the functional requirement first, asks whether AI is needed at all, and then chooses the smallest sufficient model, meeting the requirement with minimal resources while keeping a verified margin, robustness and responsible governance. The framework applies frugality across four dimensions (data, compute, model and operations) through seven levers spanning the AI lifecycle, each with practices and measures. It calls for public AI rails, vernacular-first design, measured resource use, human oversight for high-stakes decisions, and a cap on total resource consumption so that efficiency gains are not cancelled out by rising usage. We argue that India is resource-imbalanced rather than resource-poor, and that frugal principles offer the most viable route to population-scale deployment, fit naturally with India's digital public infrastructure, and make sovereignty operational by preserving India's capacity to specify, deploy, govern, inspect and adapt its AI systems. A seven-point adoption checklist and eleven policy statements translate the framework into practice, positioning AI in India as a responsible, frugal public-value system that maximises social value per rupee and per joule.

Authors: Kazuhiko Kawamoto, Keigo Watanabe, Hiroshi Kera

Abstract: Sharing one low-rank adaptation (LoRA) update across pretrained models simplifies deployment. cross Semiparametric Efficiency for Entropic Optimal Transport under Structured Distribution Shift

Authors: Yiming Ma, Weiwei Zhuang, Hang Liu

Abstract: Can structural information improve estimation of entropic optimal transport (EOT), and when is the gain zero? We study a fixed-dimensional exponential density ratio model (DRM) with a nonparametric baseline, compact support, and fixed positive regularization. At positive limiting sampling fractions, an exact projection identity quantifies the variance gain from the restriction. The pooled empirical-likelihood estimator attains the restricted bound and supports asymptotically valid confidence intervals when the bound is positive. Even under correct specification, the gain can be zero and can shrink as the tilt space expands. The same projection residual determines direction-specific first-order bias under local misspecification. When both samples grow and the target fraction vanishes, a zero first-order tilt derivative can leave source fluctuations and quadratic tilt error as the leading terms. Their balance gives a phase transition in the limiting distribution. A continuous example gives different convergence rates for two correctly specified bases at the same distribution pair. Controlled experiments assess the fixed-proportion theory. Natural-data comparisons distinguish variance reductions from improvements in reference RMSE driven by smaller reference bias.

cross Error-Corrected Physics-Informed Neural Operator for Optimal Control of Unmanned Aerial Vehicles under Uncertainty

Authors: Oliver Lundqvist, Fabricio Oliveira

Abstract: We solve open-loop optimal control problems under parametric uncertainty using a physics-informed DeepONet that returns trajectories for all sampled scenarios in one evaluation. Trained only on the residual at generic controls, the operator is reused across targets and horizons. Because a gradient-based optimizer exploits surrogate errors, we correct the prediction by one Newton step on the dynamics, computed for all scenarios and times by one parallel scan. The corrected state has a second-order error bounded by the residual alone, and the corrected cost is honest for every control up to the sampling error. On a fixed-wing UAV benchmark, the corrected operator matches the accuracy of a batched Adam solver and an Ipopt solver at equal sample size and is 8 times faster than the Adam solver on the same GPU at a few hundred scenarios and 15 times faster than Ipopt on a CPU.

cross Spiking Neural Networks Incrementally Track Firing-Rate Networks

Authors: Hyunseok Oh, Youngki Lee

Abstract: Understanding how spiking neural networks (SNNs) efficiently process data streams is key to (i) studying how biological brains conserve energy for lifelong predictions and (ii) enabling efficient continuous AI inference. In this work, we propose a theory on how an SNN incrementally tracks its corresponding firing-rate network, a continuous-valued neural network (ANN). We prove that the output of a discrete SNN continuously tracks the instantaneous ANN output implicitly determined by the SNN input. Based on this principle, we develop LIF-Stream, a conversion framework for efficient SNN inference over unbounded data streams. It enables the converted SNN to reuse its prior activation state and compute only the activation updates between frames, thereby reducing the average number of time steps needed per frame. Specifically, LIF-Stream controls the SNN to continuously track the reference ANN output, which gives a high-accuracy prediction for every frame. Evaluation on three large-scale streaming inference benchmarks with distinct modalities shows that LIF-Stream achieves the best efficiency-accuracy trade-off compared to high-accuracy SNN techniques.

cross Latent Jacobian Steer: Eliciting Contact-Rich Out-of-Distribution Behaviors from Pretrained Humanoid Controllers

Authors: S K Swaminathan, Shivam Sood, Shreyansh Kansal, Chengyang He, Guillaume Adrien Sartoretti

Abstract: Given a pretrained general latent-conditioned whole-body humanoid controller, say a behavioral foundation model (BFM), how can we elicit complex and contact-rich out-of-distribution behaviors? Raw joint-action exploration enables direct, task-specific adjustments but can disrupt coordination learned by the pretrained controller, whereas latent exploration retains that coordination but is difficult to steer toward desired physical effects. To combine the complementary strengths of these exploration spaces, we propose Latent Jacobian Steer (LJS), which couples sampling in direct joint-action space with the semantics of the learned frozen controller's input latent space. For a task specified through desired physical effects, we sample task-relevant joint-action perturbations and map them through a damped local inverse of the policy Jacobian to obtain candidate latent ($z$) commands. These commands are executed through the frozen pretrained policy, while exploration is directed toward the physical outcomes required by the new task. Candidate outcomes are evaluated in simulation and composed into latent-command sequences that accomplish the task. Ablations show that neither action-space nor latent-space exploration alone provides the same capability as their coupling. Across diverse out-of-distribution tasks, simulation-based searching using this method produces contact-rich behaviors, including hurdling, manipulating articulated objects, and opening doors through non-prehensile contact. Over ten seeds, LJS completes more of each humanoid task than when searching in either space alone. Beyond humanoids, it also steers a goal-conditioned dexterous hand policy through four assembly tasks better than searching in goal space directly.

cross AMARIS: Merging Generalist and Specialist LLMs via Adaptive Subspace Inheritance

Authors: Hwee Young Kang, Irwin De Tao Chin, Wen-Haw Chong, Hai Leong Chieu

Abstract: Domain specialization of large language models (LLMs) often improves target-domain performance but degrades broad capabilities. Model merging is an attractive training-free alternative, yet common parameter-space strategies (for example, interpolation and sparsified averaging) provide limited control over task-specific interference. We introduce Adaptive Model Alignment via Ranked Inherited Subspaces (AMARIS), a covariance-driven framework that transfers specialist updates only along selected activation directions. AMARIS formulates merging as budgeted subspace inheritance: it seeks high specialist activation capture under a regularized base-output perturbation budget, then builds a low-rank projection gate from ranked generalized eigendirections. This yields a single static merged checkpoint that concentrates transfer in high-utility, low-interference directions. We evaluate AMARIS on Qwen3-based merges (8B and 32B) across finance, instruction-following, and multilingual SEA specialization. These results support subspace-constrained merging as a practical route to compositional capability integration without additional training.

cross Neural variational Skyrmions as a topological benchmark: emergent symmetry, precision energies and KAN--MLP comparison

Authors: Koji Hashimoto, Masaki Murata, Gakuto Ogiwara

Abstract: We propose the three-dimensional Skyrme model as a topological benchmark for neural variational methods and construct fully three-dimensional Skyrmions with baryon number $B=1,\ldots,4$ without imposing a symmetry ansatz, with high precision. For $B=2,3,4$, the relaxation of these Skyrmions starts from $B$ unit-charge lumps with random positions and orientations, which carry no symmetry information. The known toroidal, tetrahedral, and cubic Skyrmions nevertheless emerge spontaneously. The resulting variational energies for $B=1,\ldots,4$ lie slightly below the published reference values. The neural method highlights the high accuracy for retaining the topological charge, differing from $B$ by at most $10^{-6}$ in the highest-precision solutions. Our numerical method goes through physical constraint tests such as the Derrick virial relation and the Faddeev--Bogomolny bound, together with evaluation / verification through independent quadratures and resolution sweeps. We then compare Kolmogorov--Arnold networks (KANs) with multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) in more than a thousand training runs, both with and without a rational-map ansatz. With default settings, KANs appear markedly superior, but most of the gap disappears once the MLP activation function and learning rate are tuned. KANs remain more parameter-efficient and more robust at fixed epoch budget, while MLPs with the tuned activation function perform at least as well at fixed wall-clock time. We also argue that continuous gradient descent cannot smoothly change the topological sector of our neural field, clarifying the origin of topological freezing observed in our neural variational solvers. Our results on the neural methods applied to Skyrmions pave a research ground for AI-agentic benchmarks for classical topological solitons, toward AI-assisted soliton physics.

cross ORBIT: Offset-based Activation Steering for Cross-lingual Multimodal Fact Verification

Authors: Huy Trieu, Duy-Minh Nguyen-Tran, Thanh-Dat Nguyen, Huy Tien Nguyen, Tung Le

Abstract: Multimodal fact verification faces a fundamental accessibility bottleneck: while large proprietary models exhibit cross-lingual robustness, small vision-language models suffer a severe, disproportionate accuracy collapse on low-resource languages. cross Practical insights into controlling exponential concentration in quantum machine learning

Authors: Fernando Mondragon-Sampedro, Diego Alvarez-Estevez

Abstract: Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models are often limited by exponential concentration, where quantum observables become insensitive to input variations as system size increases. In this work, we provide a comparative empirical study of this phenomenon across Fidelity Quantum Kernels (FQK), Projected Quantum Kernels (PQK), and Quantum Extreme Learning (QEL), within a unified observable-based framework based on expectation-value variance. We investigate how measurement locality, entanglement, and circuit expressivity shape concentration across different architectures. Global fidelity-based kernels exhibit strong concentration driven by Hilbert space dimensionality, whereas local-observable approaches reduce but do not eliminate this effect. In PQK and QEL, concentration is primarily governed by entanglement and quantum dynamical expressivity, with reservoir depth emerging as a key factor in QEL architectures. We further study bandwidth scaling as a general mechanism for controlling encoding expressivity across all models. Our results indicate that bandwidth scaling effectively reduces concentration in moderate regimes by restricting the effective expressivity of the encoding, though this protective effect diminishes in highly expressive structural settings.

cross Constraint-Aware Anticlustering Decomposition for Certifying Pairwise-Constrained Clustering

Authors: Anna Livia Croella, Veronica Piccialli, Antonio M. Sudoso

Abstract: Certifying the quality of feasible solutions to large-scale minimum sum-of-squares clustering with pairwise must-link and cannot-link constraints remains challenging, as it requires strong lower bounds on the optimal objective value. We propose a decomposition framework that partitions the data points into smaller subsets, called anticlusters, and solves the induced clustering subproblems to obtain a certified optimality gap for the original problem. To preserve pairwise information, we introduce the Constrained Anticlustering Problem (CAP), which assigns each connected component of the constraint graph to a single anticluster while seeking geometrically representative subsets. We formulate CAP as a mixed-integer second-order cone program and develop a variable neighborhood search heuristic to construct high-quality decompositions. The resulting lower bound remains valid even when the induced subproblems are not solved to global optimality. Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets with up to approximately 4,000 observations show that our approach can achieve mean optimality gaps below 1% at a modest computational cost.

cross Self-Supervised Continuous-Time Depth Learning and Ego-Motion Estimation from Asynchronous Events

Authors: Sadiq O. Macaulay, Dipon Kumar Ghosh, Hadfield Simon

Abstract: Event cameras register brightness changes pixel by pixel with microsecond precision, whereas the dense depth that could supervise them is costly to acquire and exists only at isolated instants. Condensing the stream into frames eases learning, but at the expense of the very timing that sets event sensing apart. We propose a self-supervised approach in which each event is assigned its own depth in continuous time, and the camera motion is recovered from the same observations. Supervision arises from geometry: lifted into 3D by its predicted depth and displaced by the camera motion at the instant it fired, an event should coincide with others produced by the same edge, so any misalignment exposes an error in depth or motion. Differentiable rendering then turns these per-event estimates into dense depth maps at target times within each window, faithful to the events behind them. On MVSEC, the approach outperforms prior event-only self-supervised methods in most sequences and depth ranges, with the widest margins at night, and recovers camera rotation more accurately than an earlier unsupervised event-based method. Ablations isolate the roles of local spatiotemporal context, the temporal backbone, and the alignment objective. These results position motion-compensated event alignment as a principled source of supervision for continuous-time depth and ego-motion estimation.

cross Deep Cross-Division Network for Product-and-Ratio Feature Interactions in Industrial Ranking

Authors: Mikhail Trapeznikov, Sofia Gulevskaia, Maksim Utushkin, Aleksandr Poslavsky, Alexander D'yakonov

Abstract: Learning effective feature interactions is essential for industrial recommender systems. DCN and DCNv2 efficiently automate polynomial feature crossing. However, their cross layers construct interactions using only non-negative integer powers of the input features, with the maximum degree controlled by network depth. Consequently, they cannot directly represent the reciprocal terms required for ratios, rates, and normalized counts unless such features are explicitly provided as inputs. We introduce DCDN, a Deep Cross-Division Network that augments a DCNv2-style cross layer with a division branch. DCDN jointly learns product, ratio, and mixed product-ratio interactions. We evaluate DCDN against a production-optimized neural-ranking baseline built around DCNv2 and supplied with manually engineered ratio features. Replacing the DCNv2 block with DCDN allowed us to eliminate the entire class of manually engineered ratio features from the model input, reducing the number of input features by $40.1\%$. DCDN is deployed in the production ranking stack of a major short-video service. In a live A/B test on this service, DCDN increased likes by $3.25\%$, shares by $16.26\%$, total view time (TVT) by $0.33\%$, and clicks to open fullscreen by $0.38\%$; all changes were statistically significant. The number of shown videos decreased slightly ($-0.24\%$). These results demonstrate that DCDN can improve user engagement while eliminating reliance on manually engineered ratio features. Our code is publicly released at https://github.com/rw404/DCDN.

cross Do DeepONet Trunk-Basis Preconditioners Need Training?

Authors: Jaemin Oh, Jerome Darbon, George Em Karniadakis

Abstract: Trunk-basis (TB) preconditioners build the coarse space of a two-level Schwarz preconditioner from the trunk functions of a trained deep operator network (DeepONet), restricted to subdomains. Their effectiveness has been attributed to spectral bias since networks learn low frequencies first, complementing a smoother that removes the high-frequency error. Spectral bias, however, is a property of training, whereas a randomly initialized trunk with a smooth activation is already smooth. We therefore ask whether TB preconditioners need training. In one dimension, we prove that on each subdomain the span of untrained trunk functions is close to the space of low-degree polynomials under suitable nondegeneracy assumptions. Numerically, for the Poisson equation and three-dimensional linear elasticity with constant coefficients, untrained TB and piecewise polynomial coarse spaces of equal dimension yield close conjugate gradient iteration counts, while training with the standard \(\ell^2\) loss sometimes increases them. Training can improve iteration counts when high-contrast coefficients make the low-energy modes non-smooth, and if the loss measures the error in the energy norm. For the Poisson equation with contrast \(10^5\), energy-trained TB coarse spaces need fewer iterations than piecewise polynomials of equal dimension, whereas \(\ell^2\)-trained ones do not. For a topology-optimized elastic cantilever with contrast \(10^6\), the energy-trained TB space enriched with the rigid-body modes gives the fewest iterations at larger coarse dimensions. This suggests that for neural operator-based hybrid solvers, a network's inductive bias and training objective should match the solver.

cross Beyond Resolution: Inference-Time Parameterized Temporal Consistency in Generative Regional Climate Downscaling

Authors: Luca Glawion, Julius Polz, Harald Kunstmann, Benjamin Fersch, Patrick Ludwig, Antonio S\'anchez-Ben\'itez, Christian Chwala

Abstract: GCM-to-RCM downscaling emulation is dominated by generative diffusion models, but most existing work evaluates skill only per timestep, treating each field as an independent snapshot. This leaves open whether generated sequences are temporally coherent or merely independently plausible, a gap that matters increasingly for sub-daily resolutions since downstream impacts depend on accumulation and persistence, not just on isolated states. This study asks whether autoregressive conditioning can deliver coherent rollouts without error accumulation and ensemble-spread collapse, and whether a single model can expose the resulting coherence-diversity trade-off as a controllable inference choice rather than a fixed design commitment. The noise parameter traces a smooth interpolation between ICON's autocorrelation and PTI generation. AR rollouts of up to six months show no progressive degradation, with ensemble spread contracting only slightly within the first 24 hours. In contrast, the same architecture trained on noise-free previous states diverges. For precipitation PTI underestimates maxima by up to 2x in daily aggregations, while AR tracks ICON's maxima closely. For temperature, differences between modes are negligible. These results show that in diffusion downscaling temporal consistency can be achieved architecture-agnostically and offered as a controllable, not fixed, inference choice.

cross Can pretrained Transformers deliver semiparametrically efficient inference?

Authors: Han Yan, Jin Zhu, Chengchun Shi, Tengyao Wang

Abstract: Semiparametric models provide a broad framework for statistical inference, yet efficient estimation typically relies on influence function correction tailored to each estimand and on nuisance estimation repeated for every sample. We show that a single pretrained Transformer can deliver efficient estimation and valid inference for a family of estimands in one forward pass, without deriving efficient influence functions or estimating nuisance functions. Pretrained on simulated data with the true parameter as the only label, the Transformer learns the posterior mean and covariance of the queried estimand and hence returns an estimate and a Wald-type confidence region. Theoretically, we prove that, for locally flat pretraining priors of slowly growing complexity, the posterior mean is efficient for every queried estimand. The Transformer inherits this efficiency with asymptotically exact coverage, in probability over evaluation populations and queries, even under distribution shift. We also reveal that the guarantee places much higher demands on pretraining when the data-generating distribution is fixed, as in classical frequentist inference. Experiments on causal inference, partial linear regression, and survival analysis show that the pretrained model generally achieves the lowest estimation error among competing methods, with nearly nominal coverage, at a computational cost of only about 0.02 seconds per dataset.

cross Spatiotemporal Graph Neural Networks for Fault Detection and Structural Learning in Chemical Processes: Use Case on the Tennessee Eastman Process

Authors: Rayane Ammar Khodja, Alexandre Voisin, Victor Costa, Fanny Casteran, Benoit Celse, Beno\^it Iung

Abstract: Classical Fault Detection and Diagnosis (FDD) methods, including many data-driven approaches, assume a static normal operating space and interpret deviations from a fixed reference as fault indicators. When Operating Conditions (OCs) vary over time, this assumption breaks down: legitimate transitions trigger false alarms while moderate faults go undetected. We propose a spatiotemporal Graph Neural Network (GNN) framework that decomposes the normal operating space into OC-specific subspaces linked by transition functions, with a dual learning objective combining reconstruction loss and a Deep SVDD one-class term. It learns adjacency matrices through Graph Attention Networks (GATs) and integrates spatial modelling with temporal encoding to represent process dynamics under evolving OCs. This paper evaluates the framework's foundational components (fault detection, GATv2 spatial graph learning and temporal encoding) on the Tennessee Eastman Process (TEP) benchmark, training only on fault-free data. The architecture achieves competitive detection from reconstruction error alone, with similarity-based feature selection improving both accuracy and graph structure diversity. We then evaluate the physical interpretability of the learned attention matrices against 22 ground-truth sensor pairs derived from the TEP control structure and process topology. GATv2 attention fails to recover all known physical pairs across multiple hyperparameter configurations, suggesting a structural limitation of reconstruction-driven attention rather than a tuning issue. This challenges a common assumption in GNN-based FDD: that learned attention weights provide a basis for fault diagnosis and root-cause analysis. The architecture detects faults effectively, but the learned graph does not encode the physical topology needed for interpretable diagnosis, motivating physics-informed graph construction.

cross Decision Models for Multilingual Emotion Classification: A Comparative Study Against LLMs and Encoders

Authors: Alessandro Piccolo, Francesco De Nicol\`o, Francesco Manco, Eufemia Lella

Abstract: Decision models receive a question with a closed list of answers and return a probability for each answer instead of free text. They promise low latency and robustness to prompt wording, but no study has compared several of them with fine-tuned encoders across languages. They beat encoders fine-tuned on another dataset by 8-15 points, though against XLM-EMO this may not extend to new datasets, and instructions in each text's language change their results by under 1 point. Exploratorily, they beat the strongest public emotion model used as is by about 6 points. OpenAI's decision endpoint matches GPT-6 Luna, the LLM it runs on, at a fifth of its median latency, and a 2-billion-parameter decision model beats a same-size general LLM by 10 points, owing to its training, not the decision format. The best zero-shot systems, the LLM included, have an SD of about 1 point across prompts. Without labelled data they are a strong default, and decision models add lower latency and, unlike generative LLM use, per-option probabilities. Fine-tuning won by a wide margin only on the largest dataset, GoEmotions.

cross Budgeted Subset Selection with a Transferable Learned Value Model

Authors: Jade Lejeune Herman, Johan A. K. Suykens, Peter Goos

Abstract: Subset selection is a fundamental task in machine learning, statistics and combinatorial optimisation, with applications in active learning, band selection, input selection and experimental design. The utility of a subset depends on information revealed only after the selection, such as labels or responses, and on interactions among selected candidates. Existing approaches combine value estimation and redundancy control in application-specific ways, which limits their use in other domains. This paper introduces Subset Acquisition with Learned Value Estimates and Search (SALVES), a framework that decomposes subset selection into two components. First, a value model is trained by supervised learning on tasks where the hidden information is known. On a new task, it predicts the utility each candidate would add to the current selection. Second, a selection step chooses the subset from these predictions, typically by simulated annealing with a diversity term penalising redundancy. The value model transfers to new classes, pretrained networks, datasets and sensors, and from synthetic to real data. On the four applications, SALVES matches or outperforms application-specific baselines on average. It requires substantially fewer bands for equal accuracy and remains reliable in input selection where individual baselines fail.

cross $\varepsilon$-SegFormer: Multi-Resolution Vision Transformers for Sparsely Supervised Segmentation in Electron Microscopy

Authors: Erfan Amidi, Sheida RahnamaiKordasiabi, Edoardo Giacomello, Florian Jug

Abstract: Segmenting organelles in electron microscopy (EM) volumes requires dense annotations that take experts months to produce, and every gain in imaging resolution adds to this cost. Sparsely supervised methods reduce this cost to a small fraction of labeled pixels, but the convolutional networks they build on see a limited neighborhood around each pixel, while the identity of an organelle often depends on context far beyond it. We introduce $\varepsilon$-SegFormer, a multi-resolution Vision Transformer for sparsely supervised semantic segmentation, which builds on the architecture of MuViT and extends the training method of $\varepsilon$-Seg. For each labeled pixel, $\varepsilon$-SegFormer samples views at three physical resolutions centered on it, embeds their patches into a single token sequence, and encodes every token's position in image coordinates, so that attention relates all views in one shared frame. A segmentation head classifies the pixel from the tokens around it; during training, the pixel's own token is hidden, so the label must be predicted from context rather than local appearance. The model is trained in a single stage, without pretraining, with an auxiliary reconstruction loss. On the BetaSeg FIB-SEM dataset, $\varepsilon$-SegFormer learns from about 10,000 labeled pixels among the 670 million voxels inside three cells, and, given the cell mask, segments nuclei, mitochondria and insulin granules in a slice of a fourth, unseen cell with an average Dice of 0.89 (0.91 after post-processing). Ablations show that coarser views improve accuracy and that a compact head on local tokens works best, with the wider context entering through attention in the encoder.

cross DOiT: Better Money-Flow Ranking Can Coexist with Worse Account Selection in Anti-Money Laundering Review

Authors: Gwangui Hong, Seonkyu Lim, Min-Soo Kim

Abstract: Anti-money laundering (AML) review selects suspicious accounts and reconstructs transfers. Downstream graph neural networks (GNNs) rank money-flow evidence but may also reorder the account queue. DOiT separates these decisions at equal review capacity: account selection determines where graph investigation starts. Across AMLWorld and AMLNet, GNN reordering harms higher-yield upstream orders but helps lower-yield ones. On AMLWorld, this harm coexists with higher transaction average precision (AP) in dimension-matched, per-run decoders, demonstrating separable ranking abilities. At small capacities, positive neighbors can displace a denser upstream prefix and lower yield; replacement costs fall as that prefix is exhausted, allowing the preferred order to reverse. A restricted-access synthetic benchmark informed by real transaction patterns reproduces this separation and validation-based upstream preservation. Capacity-matched combination protects upstream ordering while retaining GNN money-flow analysis, admitting GNN queue influence only where held-out evidence supports it.

cross SLP-Bench: Evaluating and Improving Acoustic Claims of Reasoning Audio Language Models on Clinical Speech

Authors: C\^ome Hosxe, Rachid Riad, Quentin Spinat

Abstract: Audio language models (ALMs) can answer clinical questions from speech, outperforming baselines built on handcrafted acoustic features. Reasoning ALMs generate a chain of thought that refers to properties of the speaker's voice before predicting. Each statement the model makes about the voice needs to be checked against clinicians' perceptual ratings of the same recording. On clinical speech, existing evaluations score only the final answer. We introduce Speech Language Pathologist Bench (SLP-Bench), a benchmark grading each acoustic claim in the chain of thought against clinicians' perceptual ratings of the same recording. It covers five clinician-rated datasets and 25 perceptual dimensions, and assesses claims for factuality, stability under resampling, and consistency across reworded and different questions. Across ten open-weight reasoning ALMs evaluated zero-shot, claim balanced accuracy ranges from 0.52 to 0.58, claims change under resampling and rewording, and the most consistent models are not the most factual. Fine-tuning a 7B ALM with GRPO on a reward for claims that agree with clinicians raises its claim balanced accuracy from 0.54 to 0.60 and its mean kappa from 0.39 to 0.61, while preserving answer accuracy on per-dimension questions. SLP-Bench is available upon request, subject to approval by the providers of each dataset.

cross Redesigning Neural Networks to Not be Confidently Wrong even to Strong Adversarial Attacks

Authors: Michael W. Spratling, Heiko H. Sch\"utt

Abstract: Early deep learning models attempted to simulate visual perception using a neural hierarchy that alternated between two types of layer: one modelled on cortical simple cells (that was designed to increase selectivity), and one modelled on cortical complex cells (that aimed to increase tolerance). We show that such a neural network (NN) architecture, when implemented with novel components that are better able to learn selective and tolerant representations, is capable of accurate and robust image classification and improved alignment between the behaviour of the model and the perceptual abilities of humans. Firstly, the proposed models are shallow, fewer than ten layer-pairs, which is more consistent with the rapid feedforward object categorisation performed by the biological visual system. Secondly, they are far less prone to producing classification predictions with high confidence for samples from unknown classes or for image consisting of pure noise, and hence, like humans are not easily fooled into perceiving objects that are not present in the image. Thirdly, for the proposed NNs adversarial samples cause predictions with low confidence, enabling them to be accurately identified. In contrast to humans, our networks still do not correctly classify adversarial samples. However, the behaviour of our networks is a significant step towards human-like behaviour compared to standard DNNs that produce highly confident, but incorrect, classification predictions for adversarial samples. The increased robustness to adversarial and unknown samples is achieved while maintaining competitive performance in terms of generalisation to novel samples from the training categories. This enables models of this type to be competitive with state-of-the-art DNNs across multiple different evaluation metrics and benchmark tasks.

cross Autonomous quantum machine learning via Riemannian gradient-based optimization

Authors: Kasidit Srimahajariyapong, Thiparat Chotibut, Suwannachad Suwannajitt, Tithivich Paka, Ricard Puig, Yudai Suzuki, Marek Gluza, Zo\"e Holmes, Supanut Thanasilp

Abstract: Quantum machine learning models typically rely on repeated quantum measurements and classical optimization during training, making the quantum-to-classical information-extraction process a fundamental bottleneck for scalability.
