replace Chain of Operators: An Inference-Time Harness for In-Context Operator Learning

Authors: Minghui Yang, Chenghan Wu, Ling Guo, Liu Yang

Abstract: While scientific foundation models show immense promise in accelerating physical simulations and numerical forecasting, they remain notoriously brittle when encountering out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. Adapting these generalist models to complex OOD tasks typically requires expensive parameter fine-tuning. In linguistic AI, this bottleneck is bypassed using "harnesses", which serve as external scaffolding such as reasoning chains and tool use to adapt frozen weights to complex tasks, yet designing an equivalent harness for physical domains remains an open frontier. To bridge this gap, we introduce Chain of Operators (CHOP), a framework that guides a frozen foundation model through complex OOD tasks without updating a single weight. By exploiting the in-context learning capability of In-Context Operator Networks (ICON), CHOP systematically decomposes unfamiliar problems into a sequence of explicit, closed-form mathematical operations and multiple model calls, translating OOD queries back into the model's learned operating regime. Across diverse benchmarks, including canonical PDE problems and real-world air-quality forecasting, CHOP consistently and substantially reduces inference errors compared to direct model evaluation. Crucially, these modular operator chains remain fully interpretable and can generalize across entirely distinct families of physical equations. Ultimately, this work demonstrates how frozen scientific models can adapt through programmable inference, establishing a modular, potential paradigm for agentic scientific computing.

replace Crossing the Validation Crisis: Cross-Validation Reduces Benchmarking Variance Surprisingly Well

Authors: C\'elestin Eve, Ga\"el Varoquaux, Thomas Moreau

Abstract: Modern machine learning progresses through empirical work, benchmarking new methods to evaluate relative performance. However, the statistical variability inherent in evaluation, exacerbated by the stochastic nature of many algorithms, often makes performance estimation unreliable with the limited test samples available. The result is a validation crisis, in which genuine advances are difficult to discern. Here, we show that doing more cross-validation markedly improves confidence when evaluating and comparing learning algorithm performances. We introduce the concept of sample gain, which quantifies the virtual data augmentation achieved by using multiple cross-validation splits to reduce benchmarking variance. Experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets (histopathologic scans and NLP fine-tuning) demonstrate that multiple splits can substantially improve the reliability and stability of performance estimates, with diminishing returns often setting in later than expected. We also introduce a procedure to dynamically early-stop cross-validation by estimating from the first few splits whether further splits will bring large sample gains. Our findings highlight the value of pushing cross-validation on available samples to achieve robust and reliable benchmarking, and lead to a simple recommendation: split repeatedly.

replace SaturQ: Saturation-Aware Mixed-Precision Quantization

Authors: Joshua Hill

Abstract: Mixed-precision quantization assigns each module of a language model its own precision format, such as NVFP4, FP8, or BF16, to minimize damage within a memory budget. Most allocators predict the damage of a configuration by summing per-unit sensitivities measured in unquantized networks. But the sensitivity of a unit \textit{saturates} as more units are quantized, so the sum overprices the heavily quantized configurations that tight budgets require. Assuming only that damage accumulates by an associative, increasing, continuous rule restricts it to the form $g\bigl(\sum_i b_i\bigr)$, with summed sensitivities as the case of linear $g$. We propose SaturQ, which uses a concave $g$, fits the per-unit parameters $b_i$ from a single Aumann--Shapley path integral, and allocates formats with a linear program. On Qwen3-30B-A3B, SaturQ lowers KL divergence to BF16 by 11--23\% relative to ModelOpt AutoQuantize at 4.2--6.0 effective bits, and it outperforms an interaction-aware allocator that measures up to 2{,}410 configurations while measuring only three. It also predicts the damage of unmeasured configurations with a median error of 6.9--11.4\%, lower than Gaussian-process and random-forest surrogates fit to 300 configurations, and served allocations of Qwen3.6-27B and GLM-5.2 (753B) run faster than vendor NVFP4 checkpoints at comparable accuracy.

replace Towards Understanding On-Policy Distillation through the Lens of Test-Time Scaling

Authors: Xinmu Ge, Zizhuo Zhang, Yu Huang, Jianing Zhu, Lin Yuan, Wanli Gu, Weichang Wu, Weiran Huang, Bo Han, Xiaolu Zhang, Jiangchao Yao

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as a promising post-training technique for enhancing LLM reasoning. Under the reverse KL objective, the idealized optimum of OPD aligns the student distribution with that of the teacher. When the teacher consistently outperforms the student, this naturally suggests that OPD should yield broad improvements over the pre-OPD student. However, do such improvements extend across the entire range of test-time sampling budgets? In this work, we revisit this expectation through the lens of test-time scaling by varying the sampling budget $K$ and evaluating performance with pass@$K$. Across multiple settings, we observe two distinct patterns: OPD can improve pass@$K$ at both small and large sampling budgets, but it can also improve small-budget performance while reducing large-budget pass@$K$. We show one condition that guarantees such a reversal and an idealized reverse KL counterexample where it occurs even when the teacher has higher accuracy on every problem. To choose between two candidate teachers at a target sampling budget, we propose the \textit{Teacher Advantage Score at $K$} (TAS@$K$), which can be computed before OPD training to predict which teacher will lead to a larger improvement in pass@$K$. Across three domains and thirteen benchmarks, the ordering predicted by TAS@$K$ agrees with the observed pass@$K$ improvements of the resulting OPD models in 83.6\% of experiments, providing a useful signal for teacher selection at the target pass@$K$.

replace Branch Geometry and Finite-Radius Sensitivity of Hard-ReLU Training

Authors: Xiaoyang Li, Runni Zhou, Xinghao Yan

Abstract: Two nearby initializations can activate the same ReLU unit one gradient descent (GD) step apart. When their separation is proportional to the GD step size $\eta$, that one step can determine which initialization gives the lower outer loss. We derive the integer crossing steps for piecewise-$C^2$ dynamics with finitely many separated transverse events. Euler error shifts the crossing clock, and rounding an earlier crossing to a GD step can shift later crossings. With a common positive margin from the rounding boundaries, the expansion predicts the trained state with uniform $O(\eta^2)$ error. In a strongly convex ReLU risk, the branch prediction converges on three fixed directions. The best single-vector predictor has positive limiting error. The recursion also gives a sufficient directional radius for preserving the training masks. In contractive affine regions, verifying those masks and bounding the finite remainder can certify candidate ordering. Scalar and coupled examples test the two rounding effects. Small-network experiments measure response accuracy, candidate selection, and how often multiple entries of the activation mask change in one GD update.

replace AhaBench: Do Agents Turn Experience into Reusable Insights? A Long-Horizon Benchmark for Continual Learning

Authors: Zerui Cheng, Jiawei Xu, Huacan Chai, Jiayang Sun, Pramod Viswanath, Maxm Pan

Abstract: Solving a task, even from a worked example, does not make an agent any better at the next one. As agents move from single tasks to long collaborations with people, turning experience into reusable insight becomes the capability that separates a tool from a collaborator that improves over time. AhaBench is a benchmark for this kind of continual learning. It simulates three patterns of long human-AI collaboration and holds a later test fixed while varying the experience before it: in Aha-Puzzle, the agent questions a partner who knows the answer to a new mystery after solving earlier ones; in Aha-Euler, a person teaches a computation and the agent applies it to new inputs; in Aha-Vending, the agent runs a delegated business for a simulated year. The learning mechanism is left open, so memories, skill libraries, and weight updates can be compared under common rules against a baseline of fixed-weight agents that see their experience in context. Our results show that today's frontier agents are not yet continual learners. Earlier Aha-Puzzle solutions help some models, most clearly Qwen 3.6 Plus, which gains +12.57 points over attempts on the same stories without earlier episodes, yet every model scores far below what it reaches with hints. On single-step Aha-Euler tasks, the same model solves 94.0% of requested held-out inputs when taught with an explanation and code but 6.0% when given only the question and answer, counting unanswered attempts as incorrect. In incident-free Aha-Vending operation, Doubao Seed 2.0 Pro ends the year at +495 USD, yet its profit averaged over the year is -10 USD because it recovers late. Exploring without help, reconstructing a method, and sustaining productive operation remain open problems, and AhaBench provides the paired tests a learning method must pass to solve them.

replace OmniHarness: Harnessing Generalizable Visual Generation via Symbolic Policy Learning

Authors: Xu Xu, Jinxiu Liu, Zhangbo Qiao, Cheng Song, Jiaxing Lu, Xiangyu Zhang, Yubin Gu, Fangwei Ning, Yan Shi

Abstract: Unified multimodal large language models and multi-agent systems have advanced visual generation. However, several limitations remain. (1) Experience acquisition is typically reactive, occurring only in response to downstream tasks. (2) Accumulated knowledge varies in relevance and reliability, making efficient and effective reuse challenging. To address these limitations, we introduce OmniHarness, a framework for generalizable visual generation via symbolic policy learning. Before downstream objectives are specified, self-directed inquiry autonomously generates and executes practice tasks, with selection guided by capability novelty and the current competence frontier. Verified workflows are abstracted into symbolic policies that encode experience for task families. Symbolic retrieval searches workflow metadata in stages rather than full parameterized workflows, using reliability tiers to prioritize reliable knowledge. Execution feedback updates these policies after each task, guiding subsequent retrieval and reuse while model parameters remain fixed. Experiments across six benchmarks demonstrate strong performance and generalizable visual generation capabilities. On ComfyBench's Creative tasks, OmniHarness achieves a 95.0% resolve rate, exceeding the strongest baseline by 27.5 percentage points.

replace Adaptive Determinantal Client Scheduling in Federated Learning

Authors: Wen Xu, Ben Liang, Gary Boudreau, Hamza Sokun

Abstract: Scheduling clients for model training is critical in federated learning due to both data and system heterogeneity. Most previous works focus on the quality of the scheduled clients to achieve faster convergence, shorter wall-clock convergence time, or better average model performance. They rarely consider the diversity of clients, which is important to counter heterogeneity and improve performance for the worst-off clients. In this work, we advocate the use of determinantal point processes (DPPs) to model and enhance the diversity in client scheduling. We first design the kernel matrices of DPPs using gradient information and quality scores, which inherently enables a flexible quality-diversity trade-off. Applying fast MAP inference over DPPs, we propose Adaptive Determinantal Client Scheduling (ADCS) in FL. We further quantify the gradient approximation error of ADCS and develop convergence analysis for general biased client selection in FL with non-convex loss functions. We conduct comparative numerical experiments showing that ADCS outperforms state-of-the-art client scheduling algorithms, including both quality-based and diversity-based ones.

replace SAVOR: Score Once, Learn Deeper in On-Policy Distillation

Authors: Keye Zheng, Hanyu Li, Zhan Cheng, Yuan Gao

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own samples with token-level scores from a stronger teacher, and it effectively transfers reasoning ability to small language models. Each training step pays for student rollouts and teacher scoring, yet common OPD configurations make a single pass over the scored batch and then discard it. We observe that reusing the batch alone is not enough, because no single fixed teacher target suits every batch. In our runs, a fixed $\alpha$ of 0.3 exceeds a KL envelope at every training step, while 0.2 leaves over a quarter of it unused. We propose SAVOR, which selects for each batch the strongest teacher target inside the KL envelope, freezes the scored batch, and reuses it for a fixed number of student updates without further teacher calls. On five math reasoning benchmarks with a 1.7B student and a 30B-A3B teacher, SAVOR raises macro accuracy over TOP-D from 15.41 to 17.78 with two passes and to 19.36 with three passes at about the same teacher budget. With three passes, SAVOR nearly matches the final accuracy of TOP-D after one third of training while using 67.2% fewer teacher-scored tokens. These results suggest budgeting reuse depth explicitly in OPD.

replace Reuse or Relearn? A Spectral View of Earth Observation Foundation Models

Authors: Mehmet Ozgur Turkoglu, Valerio Marsocci, Dominik J. M\"uhlematter, Dominik Senti, Konrad Schindler, Helge Aasen

Abstract: Foundation models are rarely used as generic, frozen feature extractors; instead, they are fine-tuned for the target downstream application. This practice is particularly prevalent in Earth observation (EO), and it raises a question that downstream accuracy alone cannot answer: does fine-tuning reuse the pretrained representation, or does it relearn a new one? We study this with spectral diagnostics that compare a model before and after adaptation, quantifying how well its dominant singular subspaces are preserved, how broadly the weight update is distributed, and how large it is. Using natural image models such as CLIP and DINO as a reference, we find that, under the evaluated fine-tuning settings, EO models undergo far larger, higher-rank updates and retain much less of their pretrained structure, so their downstream performance is often obtained with substantial changes to the pretrained weight structure. More broadly, foundation models, and EO foundation models in particular, should be assessed not only by benchmark accuracy, but also by how reusable their pretrained representation is.

replace Approximating Softmax in Pretrained LLMs: Model Sensitivity and Kernel Acceleration

Authors: Shangzhen Zhu, Muyan Hu, Tomasz Kozlowski

Abstract: On NVIDIA Blackwell B200, tensor-core throughput outpaces special-function exponential throughput by more than two orders of magnitude, exposing exponential evaluation in fused attention kernels. A pretrained Transformer, however, may not need it evaluated accurately at every element. We characterize what a pretrained model does need by approximating softmax at inference in ten frozen decoder-only models (0.5B-72B). The number of positions the softmax map assigns probability to and within-row resolution can be cut substantially, yet uniform weighting of the same positions is damaging. Where a fixed resolution budget is placed matters as much as its size, with resolution near the row maximum consistently favored. Perturbations matched on scalar distortion produce model-dependent responses of opposite sign. These findings motivate Rowmax-PoT, a coarse logarithmic weight representation anchored at each row maximum, and Rowmax-H15, its hardware specialization in FlashAttention-4. On B200, the patched FP8 attention forward is 12.4% faster at causal 8K and 25.8% faster at non-causal 8K in host-side call-latency measurements; board energy per forward falls by 8.4% at causal 16K. Measured separately on the BF16 kernel path at 2K, Rowmax-H15 increases perplexity by 0.091-0.492% across five models from three families.

replace Pretraining Transformers with Quantized Softmax in Attention

Authors: Shangzhen Zhu, Muyan Hu, Tomasz Kozlowski

Abstract: Low-precision Transformer systems increasingly quantize attention matrix multiplications, while softmax often remains at higher precision. During pretraining, an approximate softmax changes the gradients that train the model as well as its forward computation. We study this interaction with K-interval attention, which approximates the exponential using K+1 grid values. We vary per-row grid calibration, interpolation versus hard rounding, and the placement of a straight-through surrogate relative to normalization. We derive the corresponding backward rules, including calibration derivatives, and compare these choices in pretraining experiments matched on model, data, and optimizer. Detaching the row extrema leaves the forward computation unchanged but produces a delayed increase in validation loss, under both min-max and fixed-window calibration. With hard rounding at K=4, min-max calibration and a pre-normalization surrogate incur a large loss gap; changing either choice substantially reduces it. At 124M parameters and 2.5B training tokens, fixed-window calibration with interpolation ends +0.007 nats from softmax at K=4; with hard rounding it ends +0.019 nats at K=4 under a post-normalization surrogate and +0.004 nats at K=16 under a pre-normalization surrogate.

replace Co-Design Gym: A Unified Benchmark for Embodiment-Policy Co-optimization

Authors: Aviraj Newatia, Yordan Tsvetkov, Leonard Pleiss, Andrew Spielberg, Rika Antonova

Abstract: Finding an optimal behaviour policy within a given environment is a widely studied problem in domains as diverse as games, robotics, energy infrastructure, communication networks, and multi-agent systems. Numerous benchmarks have been developed to support such research, but the vast majority assume that the agent's embodiment (design) is fixed, focusing instead on policy learning alone. Lifting this assumption gives rise to a broader class of problems in which optimizing embodiment and policy separately is highly suboptimal. An agent's embodiment strongly shapes which control policies can be discovered, while the optimal embodiment is in turn defined by the policies it admits. To help the research community study this class of problems explicitly and systematically, we introduce Co-Design Gym - a suite of benchmark environments for jointly optimizing embodiment and policy. Our environments span domains such as robotic manipulation and locomotion, multi-robot cooperation, deformable and soft dynamics, video games, electricity grids, wireless networks, F1 racing, multi-agent warehouses, and optimal control, offering 20 environment families (domains), with over 85 distinct co-design presets in total. We further contribute a systematic evaluation of representative co-design algorithms, characterizing the current state of the art. Together, these contributions lay the groundwork for cumulative, comparable progress in co-design.

replace Poisson-GENERIC Neural Operators: Exact Metriplectic Structure in Function Space via Casimir Entropies

Authors: Jason Sulskis, Sathya Ravi

Abstract: Existing thermodynamically consistent neural operators enforce GENERIC degeneracy by projecting the reversible operator, introducing state dependence and generally forfeiting the Jacobi identity. We instead use the compatible Lie--Poisson pencil $L=\alpha D+\lambda(uD+Du)$ for nonlinear transport and constant Poisson multipliers otherwise. Augmenting $u$ with a latent entropy density $s$ makes $S=\int s$ a Casimir, so $L\,\delta S/\delta z=0$ without projection. The energy combines a fixed mechanical quadratic, a learned gauge-free potential, and a convex internal energy; the friction operator $M=AA^{\!\top}$ enforces $M\,\delta E/\delta z=0$ pointwise and supports diffusion and damping through local Onsager fluxes. Skewness, positivity, and both degeneracies hold algebraically for any parameters; the continuum pencil satisfies Jacobi, with discrete residuals at machine precision on alias-free inputs. Closed-form heat and damped-wave friction operators are exactly representable, and a discrete-gradient integrator preserves the first and second laws. Across four PDEs in one and two dimensions and three backbones (FNO, Transolver, CNO), the model wins $62/72$ seed-level comparisons against unconstrained baselines at equal optimizer steps, recovers exact transport and wave symbols, matches heat and Burgers dissipation within $12\%$, and dissipates nothing on advection. At equal wall-clock time, the unconstrained FNO is more accurate over ten frames. Over one hundred frames, in and out of distribution, our model never increases physical energy and conserves it when dissipation vanishes, whereas the unconstrained FNO loses a quarter of conserved energy out of distribution and projected GENERIC-FNO often creates energy. Limitations include late-time heat under-damping and explicit-integrator divergence on waves.

replace ThunderSync: Lossless Acceleration of Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Seil Kang, Hangoo Kang, Tarun Suresh, Youngeun Kim, Shreyas Pimpalgaonkar, Seong Jae Hwang, Azalia Mirhoseini

Abstract: Language models are moving beyond generating answers to pursuing long-horizon goals in interactive environments. Post-training these agents requires long, heterogeneous trajectories, and synchronous systems leave learner engines idle until rollout and verification finish. To squeeze out these pipeline bubbles, asynchronous training overlaps rollout and learning across updates, but comes at the cost of policy staleness. We introduce ThunderSync, which starts gradient computation as soon as all required inputs are fixed, without policy staleness. For group relative policy optimization (GRPO), ThunderSync computes each trajectory's score gradient as soon as the reward for that trajectory arrives, without waiting for the group. For on-policy distillation (OPD), it computes gradients for each completed agentic turn's teacher-scored actions while tool calls run in the sandbox. We prove that gradient streaming produces the same GRPO and OPD updates as batch-synchronous training, without changing either objective. On SWE-bench Verified and Terminal Bench 4.0, we train models to the same performance up to $1.9 \times$ faster than synchronous training. With zero policy staleness, ThunderSync also outperforms asynchronous training at a fixed budget by up to $2.47$ percentage points.

replace Unifying matrix completion across domains with a synthetically pretrained foundation model

Authors: Dwaipayan Saha, Jacob Feitelberg, Kyuseong Choi, Raaz Dwivedi, Anish Agarwal

Abstract: Matrix completion underlies problems from tabular imputation to causal inference. While matrix completion provides a common setup for these domains, the best methods in each domain are still specialized and do not transfer well across domains. We introduce MatrixFormer, a pre-trained matrix-native transformer that predicts a full distribution for every missing entry in a single forward pass. MatrixFormer is trained entirely on synthetic low-rank and latent-factor matrices under diverse missingness patterns. Applied zero-shot with fixed network weights, MatrixFormer achieves competitive performance on causal inference panel-data tasks, tabular imputation, and recommendation systems matrix completion. These results position MatrixFormer as a general-purpose foundation model for matrix completion.

replace A Broader Look at Model Merging: Rethinking Implicit Regularization Induced by Task Arithmetic

Authors: Sin-Han Yang, Shih-Cheng Huang, Chieh-Yen Lin, Yun-Nung Chen, Shao-Hua Sun, Hung-yi Lee

Abstract: Model merging aims to build a multi-task model cheaply by combining the weights of individual task-specific models. To perform well across multiple tasks, most existing merging methods use an additional dataset to find the coefficients for the best linear combination of task-specific weight updates. However, we identify an implicit regularization in this standard practice: searching over coefficients restricts the candidate models to a subspace spanned by task-specific weight updates. In this work, we investigate whether this regularization is actually useful. Surprisingly, empirical results show that in existing benchmarks, optimizing merged-model weights without this regularization significantly boosts the performance of common merging methods across multiple architectures and domains, even in an extremely data-limited scenario where only one instance is available per class. We further investigate strategies for using the additional dataset in different settings and discuss their practical use. We show that generalization is a key factor when choosing the regularization strength. Overall, this work calls for revisiting the existing model-merging pipeline, motivating a broader exploration of the weight space and a reconsideration of the implicit regularization induced by task arithmetic.

replace Stochastic Grouping Conformal Prediction for Effective Subgroup Reliability

Authors: Meihui Zhong, Wenxin Tai, Ting Zhong, Fan Zhou

Abstract: Conformal prediction offers a distribution-free coverage guarantee, making it especially attractive for clinical applications. Standard conformal prediction, however, provides such guarantees only at the population level, and its prediction sets can exhibit coverage disparities across clinically important subgroups. A natural remedy is to calibrate within predefined groups. However, this can require access to sensitive subgroup attributes and is prone to a worst-group bottleneck: protecting the most difficult subgroup can inflate prediction sets for all, increasing cognitive burden on decision makers. To this end, we propose Stochastic Grouping Conformal Prediction (SGCP), a conformal framework for subgroup-reliable uncertainty quantification. It learns a stochastic grouping map that allows each sample to draw calibration information from others with similar calibration behavior, yielding a local score law that boosts reliability across subpopulations. We prove that SGCP retains the standard coverage guarantee. Experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks show that it consistently reduces subgroup coverage gaps while achieving smaller or comparable prediction set sizes relative to existing baselines.

replace Softmax-Free Attention in Spiking Transformers as Zero-Temperature Two-Level Systems: Applications to XAI

Authors: Roberto C. Sotero, Pouya Taghipour, Jose M. Sanchez-Bornot

Abstract: Spiking Transformer architectures usually drop the softmax, and some threshold each query--key score into a binary attention map. For such maps, the statistical mechanics of softmax attention does not carry over, because no temperature of the softmax gives a binary map. We show instead that each query--key link of threshold attention is a two-level system, as a spiking neuron is. Thus, a spiking Transformer with threshold attention is a zero-temperature network of two-level systems. In this picture, the attention map is a ground state, and the surrogate gradients used in training are susceptibilities at a finite temperature. Cooling trained networks from high temperature then shows that their predictions form gradually as their units freeze. Using this cooling picture, we adapt thermodynamic integration to explainable AI (XAI). Specifically, we heat the binary units of each patch, its neurons and the links into it, to random states at their deployed occupation, then cool them back patch by patch and credit each change of the output to the patch just cooled. This thermal integration (ThI) needs no gradients or input baseline, and its scores sum exactly to the output change. On CIFAR-10, pixel-level integrated gradients with surrogate gradients instead miss the output change by about a quarter. On six deletion and insertion tests in the spiking Transformer, ThI's mean rank is second only to Shapley value sampling with blurred patches. Yet ThI never ranks below fourth, whereas Shapley sampling, with any choice of removed patches, drops to sixth or seventh on some tests.

replace Counting with Attention and LayerNorm: Norm Scaling and Representation Geometry

Authors: Ayyub Shaffy, Manoj Dhakal, Safal Shrestha, Keith W. Ross

Abstract: Counting is a simple task on which transformers can nevertheless fail at long sequence lengths. We study the cost of counting in a single attention layer, up to a maximum length $L$, focusing on how softmax attention and layer normalization shape the representation from which the count is recovered. We mainly focus on regression read-out but also consider classification read-out. For regression, we show unnormalized linear attention counts exactly at parameter norms independent of $L$. For softmax attention, regression cannot count exactly with finite parameters; it recovers the count after rounding, but only when the vector parameter norms grow with $L$. We measure this cost by $B_{\min}$, the smallest bound that can be placed on every trainable vector to ensure exact counting after rounding, and derive tight rates. At fixed model dimension, $B_{\min}$ grows as $L$ without normalization, as $L^{3/2}$ under bare post-normalization, and as $L^{3/4}$ with learned post-normalization. The last rate is unexpected: post-normalization discards the magnitude that carries the count and might be expected to make counting harder, yet with a learnable gain it counts at a smaller norm than softmax without normalization. Bare pre-normalization is different: it bounds the attention scores, so beyond a finite length no parameter norm suffices. A classification read-out keeps every norm bounded under every placement, but a dense head stores one output vector per count and costs more in storage. Experiments on the post-normalized model reproduce this split between the two read-outs. Together, our theoretical and empirical findings characterize how self-attention, layer-normalization placement, and read-out choice shape the representation geometry and parameter cost of counting in the minimal single-head architecture studied here.

replace CHSM8: A Chess Transformer that Follows Natural-Language Style Prompts

Authors: Ilia Badanin

Abstract: We present CHSM8, a 210M-parameter chess transformer that plays moves conditioned on a free-text description of a playing style, e.g. "go for the Sicilian Defense", "solid, safe, risk-free chess" or "You are Magnus Carlsen in his prime". The model is pretrained without search on 1.07B filtered Lichess games, using a factorized move representation and legal-move masking. It reaches 1837 Elo against a strength-limited Stockfish and, in 1,000-game matches, beat every other public model we tested that was likewise trained without preference tuning, rewards or search, including one with 13 times as many parameters. We then fine-tune it to follow text on a synthetic dataset: Gemma-4-31B describes the playing style of 3M game sides, a 1B Gemma-3 model distilled from the 2.4M of those descriptions that contradict nothing on the board labels the rest, and every one of 30M selected game sides gets five descriptions. Supervised fine-tuning attaches a frozen text encoder through gated cross-attention, and direct preference optimization (DPO) uses the same moves under a mismatched description as the rejected sample. After DPO, style adherence rises on every prompt we test (for example, queens traded early 53% -> 67%, pawn moves 29% -> 32% of all moves, queenside castling 97.3% -> 99.7%), and strength without a prompt stays within noise of SFT (1792 vs. 1797 Elo). Design choices at every stage were made by fixed-budget automated search loops on top of standard architectures and training recipes. We document the full pipeline and the decisions behind it, report the ablations that failed, and evaluate the models across many style prompts and kinds of games, with 1,000 games per setting. The work is fully open source and reproducible: weights, datasets with all training labels, prompts, configurations, inference code and playable demos are public and free to build on.

replace Computation, Not Memory: Chain-of-Thought and State Tracking in LLM Agents

Authors: Pedram Mouseli, Massieh Moayedi

Abstract: Language-model agents often reason before they act, and the value of monitoring that reasoning depends on how it relates to their actions. We ask how an agent's actions depend on its reasoning when the correct action depends on a changing state. In a small file-system task, background events announce which file's lock has been toggled but not its new value. The agent therefore has to reconstruct the current locks from its history before it can move files safely. With Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B, we separate two roles of reasoning: generating it before each action, and retaining earlier reasoning in the prompt. Most of the reduction in lock-violating commands comes from reasoning written at the moment of action. Retaining earlier reasoning adds little when the agent also reasons at each step, but it helps an agent that does not reason, and it helps in longer episodes. Retained notes have a limit: when a later event makes a note wrong, an agent without current reasoning responds little, while current reasoning makes the action follow the event. The benefit depends on what the reasoning says. Filler text, reasoning written for another history and an accurate table of the state do not reproduce it. When we give the agent reasoning written for a history that differs in one lock value, the agent acts on that reasoning even against its own history. For Qwen3-8B, this holds also when that value has since been toggled. Without reasoning, state readouts are less reliable after toggles, and Qwen3-8B's answers about the state favor the outdated value. The state answer and the action also depend on different parts of the prompt. In this setting, the benefit of reasoning for rule-following comes mainly from generating it at each step, where it makes the action respond to later events. The state that can be read out without reasoning is a limited guide to how the agent acts.

replace Tracing Subliminal Learning Through Pretraining

Authors: Angela Tang, Vishesh Anand, Gon\c{c}alo Paulo

Abstract: Large language models trained on data generated by other models can sometimes inherit traits unrelated to that data, a phenomenon called subliminal learning. We study this phenomenon under both hard-label distillation, where students train on numbers generated by a biased teacher, and on-policy soft-label distillation, where students generate the data and the teacher's logits provide the learning signal. Using Pythia, PolyPythia, and DataDecide OLMo models, we find that transfer persists in both settings across differences in weight initialization, pretraining data order, pretraining corpus, model size, and architecture, showing that none of these shared properties is necessary for transfer. Across pretraining checkpoints, the ability to transmit a preference and the ability to acquire one develop separately: some checkpoints are more effective teachers but weaker students, and vice versa. Consistent with this distinction, cross-model transfer can be strongly asymmetric in magnitude when teacher and student roles are reversed. To investigate what carries the preference signal, we construct synthetic number sequences that match the teacher's generated number frequencies but have no dependence on the prompt or preceding numbers and find that these still induce substantial transfer in the hard-label setting. In top-$K$ soft-label distillation, removing animal tokens from the loss reduces transfer, although not completely in the same-model setting. In our experiments, soft label distillation produces more consistent transfer with less extensive training than hard label distillation. The growing use of on-policy distillation in model post-training makes this a particularly relevant setting for assessing unintended preference transfer.

replace Benchmarks Are Surprisingly Compaction-Friendly

Authors: Jeanne Salle, Hardik Bhatnagar, Matthias Bethge, Sahar Abdelnabi, Maksym Andriushchenko

Abstract: Long horizon agentic tasks are now pushing LLM agents past their context window. To continue the task, the agent scaffold has to free up the context window, a process known as context compaction. Compaction is generally lossy by design: agents can silently lose task constraints, instructions, or their reasoning behind earlier decisions. Although compaction is a standard component of coding agent scaffolds, it has not been studied systematically with frontier models on realistic, long horizon agentic tasks. Previous work evaluate smaller open weight models on toy or synthetic trajectories. We fix the agent scaffold (OpenCode), implement five compaction strategies inside it, and evaluate three frontier models on three long horizon benchmarks (PaperBench, ProgramBench, PostTrainBench). For the two 1M context models, the compaction threshold does not measurably affect the task performance, whereas GPT-5.3-Codex, the model with the smallest context window, performs worse at lower thresholds on PaperBench and, less clearly, on PostTrainBench. No compaction method outperforms default self-summarization, and methods that discard more content cost some models up to 20 points while leaving other models the same. Compaction can, however, silently remove task constraints: on ProgramBench, the first compaction summary already drops about one third of the task's anticheating rules on average, and this rises monotonically with the number of compactions. On real user-agent sessions from SWE-chat, instructions about how the agent should work and what it should not do are dropped around 6 times more than specifications of what to build. We argue that current benchmarks are compaction-friendly: i.e., the objective is fixed and the agent's progress stays on disk, so most of what compaction discards can be recovered, except constraints that don't exist anywhere else.

replace Let the Gate Decide: Per-Lookup Precision for Quantized Conditional Memory in LLMs

Authors: Tianqi Chen, Xunchao Li, Linchuan Xie, Guanghua Yu, Jianchen Zhu, Peisong Wang, Jian Cheng

Abstract: Conditional memory layers, such as DeepSeek's Engram, attach large hashed n-gram tables to a language model, and removing them from DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash costs 36.8 PopQA points. Low-bit quantization loses long-tail facts first, and the standard remedy, giving more bits to frequently accessed rows, backfires: refining lookups by access frequency is worse than refining them at random. We trace this to the gate that scales each retrieved row. A lookup's quantization error is weighted by its gate, which is small for frequent n-grams, large for the rare n-grams of long-tail entities, and computable from a 1-bit code. GateRQ therefore stores each row as a ladder of 1-bit residual codes, keeps level 1 in GPU memory to compute the gate, and reads deeper levels only for the lookups the gate ranks highest. Without retraining, GateRQ cuts GPU table memory by 7.8x on DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, which raises vLLM throughput by 11%-31% over FP8 tables, while losing about 1 PopQA point at 1.50 bits read per lookup and 0.2 points at 2.10 bits across two models.

replace The Alignment Tax Is Graded: Task-Selective Capability Loss Under Length-Normalized DPO with SFT Retention

Authors: Ye Yuan

Abstract: Setting. This is an empirical characterization study; we propose no new method and run no comparisons against alternative objectives. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-4B with sparse LoRA and length-normalized DPO plus an SFT retention term (the RPO objective) on structured financial-filing tasks, and track downstream capability as the implicit reward margin grows to $\sim 7$ $\beta$-scaled units (nf4 training; $\sim 3$ under fp16 measurement on the unquantized base). Question. Does margin growth degrade capability uniformly across tasks, or selectively? Findings. In a pre-registered study (8 seeds, 6 unseen at freeze) with artifact-aware scoring and TOST equivalence testing, the alignment tax is graded and task-selective: extraction is preserved ($\Delta = -0.55$ pts, equivalent at $\pm5$ pts), json loses little ($\Delta = -3.52$ pts, equivalence fails on a clustered item-level interval), and router degrades severely ($\Delta = -30$ pts). Router's loss concentrates in a margin 1-3 damage window containing a transient format-break spike. The margin is accounted for almost entirely ($\geq 96\%$) by suppression of rejected completions, with chosen reward approximately flat, consistent with the retention term anchoring it; we do not attribute the behavioral damage to any single gradient channel. Gold-logp deflation is task-selective (extraction $-0.15$, json $-1.04$, router $-1.47$ nats/token under fp16), with gold selectively protected relative to incorrect controls on extraction (8/8 seeds). QLoRA reward margins differ up to $3.4\times$ between nf4 training and unquantized fp16 measurement, with an axis-dependent gap. Scope. Length-normalized rewards with SFT retention, sparse LoRA on 8/32 layers, one 4B model, easily-separable synthetic preferences, three structured tasks. A no-retention control and a measured format-alignment metric are left to future work.

replace When Scores Conflict with Preferences: Pre-Alignment Diagnosis and Adaptive Correction of Constraint Drift in DPO

Authors: Ping Liu, Yan Yan

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is increasingly used in settings where pairwise preferences coexist with pointwise criteria such as response length, answer correctness, or safety scores. When the two disagree, standard DPO treats pairs whose preferred output scores worse on the pointwise criterion (conflict pairs) in the same way as the remaining (matching) pairs. We characterize a systematic failure mode, constraint drift: for sufficiently small updates, conflict pairs tend to increase constraint cost while matching pairs tend to repair it, and the net change depends on their balance. This balance yields a closed-form pre-alignment diagnostic, $\rho^{*}_{\mathrm{eq}}$, computable from dataset statistics, that indicates whether a dataset is self-correcting or requires intervention. Building on this diagnostic, we propose Conflict-aware DPO (CoDPO), which down-weights conflict pairs with a strength set from $\rho^{*}_{\mathrm{eq}}$ rather than tuned, and adds an adaptive margin shift that addresses residual drift during training. Empirically, drift grows monotonically with conflict exposure on the conflict-dominated domains, and the intervention decision given by $\rho^{*}_{\mathrm{eq}}$ matches the observed drift on every domain, including one that it identifies as self-correcting. On all four domains of the main comparison, CoDPO reduces drift while largely preserving the preference learning of vanilla DPO. Compared with hard filtering and recent constrained and multi-objective baselines, CoDPO also achieves a more favorable drift-preference trade-off.

replace Equivariant Deep Learning in Direct-Product Space for Electronic-Structure Hamiltonian Prediction

Authors: Shi Yin, Zujian Dai, Xinyang Pan, Lixin He

Abstract: Most existing equivariant architectures for Hamiltonian prediction propagate features in direct-sum spaces of irreducible spherical tensors, whereas the target Hamiltonian is naturally an operator block in a direct-product representation over local orbital and spin bases. This representation mismatch fragments contiguous Hamiltonian blocks, obscures direct manipulation of orbital couplings, and organizes features into irregular angular-momentum components that require numerous transformations and are poorly aligned with efficient hardware execution and memory management. To address both the representation mismatch and computational bottleneck, we propose DP-Learner, an equivariant learning framework that performs message passing directly on Hamiltonian-like operator blocks in the direct-product space. Theoretically, we propose Lie-generator-based directional embedding and invariantly weighted left--right products and contractions that preserve equivariance to SO(3), SU(2), and spatial inversions, without explicit Clebsch--Gordan decomposition or inverse reconstruction. Methodologically, we instantiate this principle as a Transformer architecture, in which Hamiltonian-like feature matrices or tensors evolve through equivariant operators in the direct-product space, while non-linear invariant scalar routing controls structured gating over matrix units, directional propagation, attention, and environment-embedded context interactions. We further construct MP-HAM-110K, a large-scale Hamiltonian dataset comprising more than 110,000 material structures covering 68 elements with both Hamiltonian and wavefunction data. Experiments on MP-HAM-110K and Materials-HAM-SOC demonstrate that DP-Learner dramatically improves Hamiltonian prediction accuracy and inference efficiency over the state-of-the-art method.

replace Uncertainty-Aware Hybrid Offline Reinforcement Learning with Online Action Selection Enabled by Sparse Gaussian-Process Critics

Authors: Marco Chan (Sargent Centre for Process Systems Engineering, Department of Chemical Engineering, Imperial College London), Christos Evripidou (Sargent Centre for Process Systems Engineering, Department of Chemical Engineering, Imperial College London), Liqiu Dong (Sargent Centre for Process Systems Engineering, Department of Chemical Engineering, Imperial College London), Benoit Chachuat (Sargent Centre for Process Systems Engineering, Department of Chemical Engineering, Imperial College London), Mehmet Mercang\"oz (Sargent Centre for Process Systems Engineering, Department of Chemical Engineering, Imperial College London)

Abstract: Start-ups and grade changes remain largely operator-driven, while developing and maintaining dynamic models for model-based control requires substantial engineering effort. Hybrid Offline Learning and Online Optimisation (HOFLON) uses one-step offline reinforcement learning to select actions from historical transition data. In its original implementation, the online objective combines the predicted return from a tree-ensemble critic with an adversarial-autoencoder (AAE) penalty for unfamiliar state-action pairs. We examine the effect of this penalty and replace the critic and AAE with a sparse variational Gaussian-process (sGP) critic. Actions maximise $\mu - \kappa\sigma$, an uncertainty-adjusted estimate of the return from one action followed by the historical operating practice. On the HOFLON polymerisation-reactor benchmark, removing the AAE from HOFLON led to 4 failed start-ups in 15 runs over five critics. At its published weight, the variation in the AAE penalty is four orders of magnitude smaller than the variation in the tree-ensemble critic. Nevertheless, it changes actions when the predicted returns of competing candidates are nearly equal, and paired runs with and without the penalty diverged from the first move. With a Mat\'ern-5/2 sGP critic, $\kappa = 0$ led to failure in 13 of 15 runs, whereas with $\kappa = 3$ none of 15 runs (five critics, three noise realisations) failed. The median and lowest returns were 13,464 and 13,398 (HOFLON: 13,393 and 12,411). A squared-exponential sGP critic with learned inducing inputs needed $\kappa \geq 3$ to remain within the empirical data support in steady state, and then settled with a temperature offset of 2.0 K.

replace When Copying Is Hard to Beat: A Negative Result in Clinical Assessment Forecasting

Authors: Marius Fracarolli, Stefan Riezler

Abstract: We report a negative result in next-day clinical assessment forecasting: the evaluated models show no consistent advantage over strong persistence-based baselines. Clinicians can view the previous day's questionnaire answers, a plausible source of documentation persistence. Predicting 45 items on 29,715 ICU day-pairs with patient-disjoint splits, a physiology-informed gated model improves item-macro-F1 over a one-line clinical rule by +0.0019 [+0.0006,+0.0031], while losing accuracy. No macro-F1 advantage is established when the physiology window ends at the previous form's reference time (+0.0001 [-0.0006,+0.0008]), or when scoring excludes the 68.5% of cells derived from parent answers; combining both changes yields a small disadvantage. Four checkpoints across three families underperform persistence, and a fine-tuned one differs from it on only two cells. A categorical read-out raises changed-cell accuracy eightfold, but a state-only read-out scores higher overall than forecast-only decoding and matches a transition matrix. This negative result highlights how the documentation process, input cutoff, observation mask and decoding rule shape conclusions about forecasting recorded assessments.

replace Does Learning Order Explain Forgetting? The Lie Bracket as an Early-Warning Signal for Catastrophic Forgetting Without Old Data

Authors: Ephraim Kofi Ayi Kwapong

Abstract: A network trained on task A and then task B ends at different weights than one trained on B and then A. To leading order this gap is set by the Lie bracket of the two tasks' gradient fields, and Dherin (2023) conjectured that a non-vanishing bracket may be linked to catastrophic forgetting. I test this conjecture empirically. Across Split MNIST, Split Fashion-MNIST and Split CIFAR-10, the forgetting of A is almost exactly the difference in A's loss between the two training orders (Spearman rho = 0.96-1.00). I then ask whether the bracket can predict that forgetting early in training on B and without any of A's data, using only a small summary stored when A finishes: its gradient and its top 20 Hessian eigenpairs. When the old task is well learned and forgetting is slow (task-incremental), this stored bracket ranks forgetting with rho = 0.70-0.81, against 0.33-0.58 for the penalty of Elastic Weight Consolidation, and matches or exceeds re-evaluating A on its own data. When forgetting is fast (domain-incremental), the stored summary goes stale and the EWC penalty is as good or better. The bracket fails outright when the old task is only half learned; I trace this to a residual gradient on A an order of magnitude larger, which makes first-order (slope) damage matter. In five-task sequences with slow forgetting, the stored bracket outperforms the EWC penalty on MNIST and CIFAR-10 features (by 0.16-0.17) and ties it on Fashion-MNIST; it fades as summaries age on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST, where a Taylor estimate from the same summary holds up better. The bracket is therefore a useful early-warning signal for the quiet kind of forgetting, under a clear condition, provided the summary is kept fresh.

replace Cancellation-Free Constrained SquareCB for Safe Controller Selection

Authors: Kartik Pandit, Sourav Ganguly, Arnob Ghosh

Abstract: We study constrained contextual bandits, in which a learner chooses one of $K$ actions in each round to minimize loss while keeping the expected cost of its play below a budget. Existing reductions to online regression weight the cost by a Lagrange multiplier learned from past constraint residuals, so slack in some rounds can offset violations in others. We instead run SquareCB proposed in \citep{foster2020beyond} with a rectified weight on the cost: it is zero when the estimated cost is within the budget, and otherwise just large enough to bring it back to the budget. Under realizability and Slater's condition with margin $\delta$, the algorithm attains regret $O(\sqrt{KT\,\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathsf{Sq}}(T)}/\delta)$ and cancellation-free violation $\sum_t(V_c^{p_t}-b)^+=O(\sqrt{KT\,\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathsf{Sq}}(T)})$, where $\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathsf{Sq}}$ is the error of the regression oracle. The same bounds hold when the budget must be met in every context, potentially adversarially chosen contexts. In controller selection for a safe-control-gym quadrotor and a BOPTEST building, the violation is 6 to 9 times lower than that of primal-dual and virtual-queue baselines, with a lower or comparable regret.

replace Testing World Models on Actions They Have Never Seen: Held-Out Plans, Forecast Accuracy and Price Rules in Retail

Authors: Karthik Iyer

Abstract: A world model is trusted to answer "what if": how its world responds to an action it has never seen. We give a test of that trust for any action-conditioned world model: actions held out of training by design and scored against a known answer; whether forecast accuracy predicts accuracy on those actions; and the same structure given to simpler models. We run it in retail, where the action is a price and promotion plan, across 2,853 fitted models. First, forecast accuracy is a fair guide between kinds of model (rank correlation with error on new plans 0.32 to 0.66) and a poor guide between versions of one world model: where merchants plan into high-demand weeks the link reverses, down to -0.69. Second, training the world model to learn the effect of actions (JEPA pretraining, a latent consistency check, adaptation at the plan date) leaves its error on new plans unchanged. Third, in generated worlds the recorded regular price and three price rules improve it. Given the same rules, a log-linear model is best when its curve can match the truth; in a world whose curve lies outside the rules, the world model with the rules is ahead of the log-linear model with the rules in 36 of 40 worlds, and does as well without its recurrent cell. On real data (Breakfast at the Frat, The Complete Journey and M5) the rules lower the world model's error on pure price cuts deeper than any it saw (0.63 to 0.56 on the Frat; 0.45 to 0.44, within chance, on The Complete Journey), but not on lasting price moves. Fourth, a small share of random test plans helps every model a little. In a generated dosing world, a stress test in a second domain, the first finding repeats, and the built-up drug level lowers the world model's error (2.86 to 1.91) though it still predicts only 0.12 to 0.32 of the true gain. The test carries to any domain where a world model is asked about actions outside its history.

replace Variance-Optimal Counterfactual Policy Design for Sample-Efficient Online A/B Testing

Authors: Shashank Gupta, Subhadeep Maji

Abstract: Online A/B testing is the standard approach for evaluating changes to user-facing systems, such as a new recommendation or ranking policy, before deployment. While the difference-in-means estimator of a standard A/B test is unbiased, it often suffers from high variance. When the treatment and production policies choose the same actions with similar probabilities, the resulting observations can add noise while providing little information about the difference between the policies. Consequently, A/B tests can require large amounts of traffic and long durations to detect small treatment effects. Existing variance-reduction methods mainly improve the estimator after the data-collection scheme has been fixed, and recent overlap-aware designs only optimize a single traffic split between the two policies; thereby, they cannot direct traffic to the actions on which the policies disagree. In this paper, we jointly optimize how actions are sampled and how the resulting observations are used to estimate the treatment effect. Our contributions are two-fold. First, building on pairwise off-policy evaluation, we derive the variance-optimal action-level logging policy for estimating the average treatment effect between two policies, which has a closed-form solution for any fixed control variate. Second, we use historical logs to jointly optimize the logging policy and a control variate to further reduce variance. Our experimental results in simulated policy A/B tests show that our designs estimate the average treatment effect more accurately than existing variance reduction methods on every evaluated setting, while generally improving statistical power and reducing how often the worse policy is selected. As a result, our designs enable practitioners to reach reliable deployment decisions with considerably less online traffic.

replace PSTO: A PDE-structured transport operator with shared implicit updates for multiscale CDR systems

Authors: Kaiwen Li, Dezhi Zhou, Kun Wu, Xingcai Lu

Abstract: Multiscale convection-diffusion-reaction (CDR) systems couple spatial transport with local stiff reaction kinetics, making their numerical simulation computationally demanding. Conventional neural surrogates require costly coupled CDR training data, and their parameter counts scale with the number of transported variables, limiting scalability to high-dimensional CDR systems. Here we propose a PDE-Structured Transport Operator (PSTO) that learns convection--diffusion dynamics solely from reaction-free data. By embedding the PDE structure into the neural architecture, PSTO generalizes to full CDR systems at inference without retraining. A single transport operator block is shared across all transported variables and refined through relaxed fixed-point iterations, performing an implicit-like correction in latent space. This improves stability during recursive multi-step prediction without adding trainable parameters. We evaluate PSTO on three benchmarks: a canonical 2D CDR system, a multiscale Robertson counterflow flame, and a multiscale hydrogen planar jet flame. In every case, PSTO reduces the mean relative $L_2$ error after 100 coupled steps by at least 50% compared to the strongest neural baseline. Ablation studies on the fixed-point iterations and detailed error decomposition further confirm that PSTO achieves both high one-step accuracy and long-term stability in coupled rollouts. Time-step sensitivity analysis shows that PSTO maintains accuracy even with a tenfold larger time step, whereas baseline accuracies decay rapidly as the time step increases. This framework enables efficient, modular modeling of multiscale CDR dynamics by separating transport learning from the treatment of local kinetics.

replace When Do Rankings Beat Signs? Gradient Direction Recovery under Even Nuisance

Authors: Zhuangzhuang Pan, Yan Xia, Chee Seng Chan

Abstract: Comparison-based optimization minimizes an objective using only ordinal feedback without numerical values. Sign-based baselines evaluate symmetric perturbation pairs in isolation, retaining only within-pair signs and discarding cross-pair orders from the same ranked batch. We ask when complete batch rankings provably beat within-pair signs under an unknown differentiable even nuisance, such as quadratic curvature. We show that reversed cross-pair comparisons cancel shared nuisance offsets without estimating a Hessian, and introduce a geometric framework characterizing three nested direction sets, namely Mirror, Reverse, and Full. Rather than curvature magnitude or condition number, we identify relative shell variation $\tau_a$, the nuisance range on a radius-$a$ sphere divided by $a\|g\|$ for gradient $g\ne 0$ in $d$ dimensions, as a key quantity governing localization. For target angular accuracy $\varepsilon$ in radians, whenever $\tau_a\le c\varepsilon/\sqrt{d}$ for a small absolute constant $c$, one-round Reverse constraints from uniform directions localize every surviving direction within angle $\varepsilon$ of the gradient using $\widetilde{O}(d+\sqrt{d/\varepsilon})$ evaluations, versus $\Theta(d/\varepsilon)$ for signs. This whole-cell guarantee needs no variation bound as input and matches a lower bound up to log factors. Conversely, over well-conditioned quadratics with unbounded curvature relative to gradient scale, rankings offer no minimax evaluation advantage over signs, even across multiple rounds. Experiments certify strict Full-over-Reverse separation, and on two ridge tasks under joint evaluation and round caps, although Full shows no established gain beyond reversed constraints, Reverse consistently attains lower terminal gaps than tuned Mirror and unit-normalized ZO-RankSGD.

replace SKILL.PNG: Learning Agent Skills in Pixel Space

Authors: Maximilian Seeth, Chengzhi Martin Hu, Marie Kempkes, Paul R\"ottger, Leo Schwinn

Abstract: AI agents are commonly specialized for new tasks and domains using skills, i.e. natural language instructions placed in their context. However, the discrete nature of textual skills prevents continuous optimization. Gradient-based approaches such as finetuning, on the other hand, are typically model-specific, preventing skill transfer across different agents. Since many agents today are built on vision-language models (VLMs), we can address these limitations by introducing Skill$.$png: a method for obtaining skill-like image inputs whose pixels are optimized with gradient descent through the vision encoder of a frozen VLM. Image skills are compatible with any VLM, and a single image can be optimized jointly for an ensemble of models. Hybrid skills, i.e. text and image skills jointly optimized, further improve agent performance. Moreover, an image optimized with an ensemble of VLMs can successfully transfer across the models in the ensemble. Skill$.$png thus presents the first agent skill that can be optimized with gradient descent while retaining transfer benefits of text skills. Our code is available at: https://github.com/omseeth/skill.png

replace Learning to Defer under Expert Drift

Authors: Ziyao Mou, Andrea Wynn, Eric Nalisnick

Abstract: The learning to defer (L2D) framework enables safety in machine learning and AI systems by allocating difficult or critical decisions to a human expert. However, most prior work on L2D assumes fixed expert reliability, overlooking temporal fluctuations in human performance due to factors such as fatigue and cognitive biases. Humans commonly exhibit such substantial, systematic performance shifts across a wide range of domains, from high-stakes settings such as radiology, aviation, and judicial decision-making, to everyday tasks such as crowdsourced image annotation. We propose Expert-Drift-Adapted Learning-to-Defer (EDA-L2D), a novel expert-aware L2D framework that explicitly models these temporal shifts by conditioning the deferral head on recent histories of expert outcomes via a sequence model. This history-aware approach enables adaptive deferral policies that anticipate reliability fluctuations and better allocate decisions between human and machine over time. We provide comprehensive experiments across three diverse tasks -- image classification, medical diagnosis, and hate speech detection -- including generalization analyses under realistic deferral conditions and irregular shifts in expert reliability. Our results show that EDA-L2D adapts more effectively than prior L2D methods to diverse temporal patterns of expert reliability, resulting in improved system performance and more effective deferral decisions.

replace-cross OpinionGPT: Modelling Explicit Biases in Instruction-Tuned LLMs

Authors: Patrick Haller, Ansar Aynetdinov, Alan Akbik

Abstract: Instruction-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently showcased remarkable ability to generate fitting responses to natural language instructions. However, an open research question concerns the inherent biases of trained models and their responses. replace-cross Automatic Detection of LLM-Generated Code: A Comparative Case Study of Contemporary Models Across Function and Class Granularities

Authors: Musfiqur Rahman, SayedHassan Khatoonabadi, Ahmad Abdellatif, Emad Shihab

Abstract: The adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) for code generation risks incorporating vulnerable code into software systems. Existing detectors face two critical limitations: a lack of systematic cross-model validation and opaque "black box" operation. We address this through a comparative study of code generated by four distinct LLMs: GPT-3.5, Claude 3 Haiku, Claude Haiku 4.5, and GPT-OSS. Our analysis reveals that granularity effects dominate model differences by a factor of 8.6, with negligible feature overlap, indicating that function-level and class-level detection rely on fundamentally disjoint structural signatures. We discover critical granularity-dependent inversions: while modern models (Claude, GPT-OSS) are more detectable at the class level, GPT-3.5 is an anomaly that uniquely excels at the function level. SHAP analysis identifies the Comment-to-Code Ratio as the sole universal discriminator. However, its predictive magnitude varies drastically across models, explaining why detectors trained on specific LLMs fail to generalize. Our findings demonstrate that GPT-3.5's exceptional detectability (AUC-ROC 0.96) is unrepresentative of contemporary models (AUC-ROC approximately between 0.68 and 0.80). Robust detection requires moving beyond single-model studies to account for substantial diversity in structural fingerprints across architectures and granularities.

replace-cross Average Causal Effect Estimation in DAGs with Hidden Variables: Beyond Back-Door and Front-Door Criteria

Authors: Anna Guo, Razieh Nabi

Abstract: The identification theory for causal effects in directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) with hidden variables is well established, but methods for estimation and inference beyond the g-formula remain less developed. Previous studies have introduced influence-function-based estimators for broad classes of identifiable effects. When implemented through the observed-data density factorization, these estimators can require conditional density estimation and numerical integration, which can be statistically challenging and computationally demanding for continuous or multivariate variables. Prior work on related causal functionals has used sequential regressions and Bayes-rule representations of density ratios to avoid these requirements. Building on established identification and influence-function results, we make these reparameterizations explicit for effects identified under treatment primal fixability, Tian and Pearl's necessary and sufficient condition for identification of post-intervention law, encompassing settings beyond classical back-door and front-door identification. We construct one-step estimators under alternative nuisance parameterizations and characterize how these choices affect robustness properties and nuisance-specific L2(P) product-rate conditions for asymptotic linearity and root-n consistency. The estimators accommodate data-adaptive machine learning for nuisance estimation. To address potential violations of the target parameter bounds by one-step estimators, we also develop targeted minimum loss-based estimators (TMLEs) that respect these bounds. Our robustness characterizations and sufficient rate conditions apply to both the one-step estimators and TMLEs. Building on prior efficiency theory for hidden-variable DAGs, we further develop modifications of our estimators that exploit ordinary independence restrictions. replace-cross A context-adaptive policy framework for robust and reactive robotic manipulation via uncertainty-aware imitation learning

Authors: Tim R. Winter, Leonard Kl\"upfel, Ashok M. Sundaram, Werner Friedl, Maximo A. Roa, Freek Stulp, Jo\~ao Silv\'erio

Abstract: Generating robust and reactive manipulation strategies that can adapt to changing context information is a challenging task in robotics. Over the years, Learning from Demonstration (LfD) has emerged as an intuitive and effective solution for generating reactive policies, particularly by following dynamical-system(DS)-based approaches. However, most state-of-the-art DS-based approaches focus on addressing the robustness limitations, overlooking the modulation of policies in response to the environment. In this work, we build on existing work on policy fusion and uncertainty quantification to propose a context-adaptive policy framework that combines task-parameterized, robust and reactive manipulation. replace-cross Deep Learning-based Approaches for State Space Models: A Selective Review

Authors: Jiahe Lin, George Michailidis

Abstract: State-space models (SSMs) offer a powerful framework for dynamical system analysis, wherein the temporal dynamics of the system are assumed to be captured through the evolution of the latent states, which govern the values of the observations. This paper provides a selective review of recent advancements in deep neural network-based approaches for SSMs, and presents a unified perspective for discrete-time deep state space models and continuous-time ones such as latent neural Ordinary Differential and Stochastic Differential Equations. Abstract: Fine-tuning pre-trained generative models with Reinforcement Learning (RL) has emerged as an effective approach for aligning outputs more closely with nuanced human preferences.
