replace-cross SVarM: Linear Support Varifold Machines for Classification and Regression on Geometric Data

Authors: Emmanuel Hartman, Nicolas Charon

Abstract: Despite progress in the rapidly developing field of geometric deep learning, performing statistical analysis on geometric data--where each observation is a shape such as a curve, graph, or surface--remains challenging due to the non-Euclidean nature of shape spaces, which are defined as equivalence classes under invariance groups. Building machine learning frameworks that incorporate such invariances, notably to shape parametrization, is often crucial to ensure generalizability of the trained models to new observations. This work proposes \textit{SVarM} to exploit varifold representations of shapes as measures and their duality with test functions $h:\mathbb{R}^n \times S^{n-1} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}$. This method provides a general framework akin to linear support vector machines but operating instead over the infinite-dimensional space of varifolds. We develop classification and regression models on shape datasets by introducing a neural network-based representation of the trainable test function $h$. This approach demonstrates strong performance and robustness across various shape graph and surface datasets, achieving results comparable to state-of-the-art methods while significantly reducing the number of trainable parameters.

replace-cross Reconstruction Alignment Improves Unified Multimodal Models

Authors: Ji Xie, Trevor Darrell, Luke Zettlemoyer, XuDong Wang

Abstract: We discover a simple but effective way for visual understanding to help visual generation in a unified model. Unified multimodal models (UMMs) unify visual understanding and generation within a single architecture. However, conventional training relies on image-text pairs (or sequences) whose captions are typically sparse and miss fine-grained visual details, even when they use hundreds of words to describe a simple image. We introduce Reconstruction Alignment (RecA), a resource-efficient post-training method that leverages visual understanding encoder embeddings as dense "text prompts," providing rich supervision without captions. Concretely, RecA conditions a UMM on its own visual understanding embeddings and optimizes it to reconstruct the input image with a self-supervised reconstruction loss, thereby using visual understanding to improve image generation. Despite its simplicity, RecA is broadly applicable: across autoregressive, masked-autoregressive, and diffusion-based UMMs, it consistently improves generation and editing fidelity. With only 27 GPU hours, post-training with RecA substantially improves image generation performance on GenEval (0.73 $\rightarrow$ 0.90) and DPGBench (80.93 $\rightarrow$ 88.15), while also boosting editing benchmarks (ImgEdit 3.38 $\rightarrow$ 3.75, GEdit 6.94 $\rightarrow$ 7.27). Notably, RecA surpasses much larger open-source models and applies broadly across diverse UMM architectures, establishing it as an efficient and general post-training alignment strategy for UMMs.

replace-cross Improving Consistency in Retrieval-Augmented Systems with Group Similarity Rewards

Authors: Faisal Hamman, Chenyang Zhu, Anoop Kumar, Xujun Peng, Sanghamitra Dutta, Daben Liu, Alfy Samuel

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains where users expect outputs to be consistent across semantically equivalent queries. However, existing systems often exhibit significant inconsistencies due to variability in both the retriever and generator, undermining trust and reliability. In this work, we focus on information consistency, i.e., the requirement that generated outputs convey the same core content across semantically equivalent inputs. We introduce a principled evaluation framework that decomposes RAG consistency into retriever-level, generator-level, and end-to-end components, enabling systematic identification of inconsistency sources. To improve consistency, we propose Paraphrased Set Group Relative Policy Optimization (PS-GRPO), an RL approach that leverages multiple rollouts across paraphrased set to assign group similarity rewards. We leverage PS-GRPO to achieve Information Consistent RAG (Con-RAG), training the generator to produce consistent outputs across paraphrased queries and remain robust to retrieval-induced variability. Because exact reward computation over paraphrase sets is computationally expensive, we also introduce a scalable approximation method that retains effectiveness while enabling efficient, large-scale training. Empirical evaluations across short-form, multi-hop, and long-form QA benchmarks demonstrate that Con-RAG significantly improves consistency while preserving accuracy compared to baselines, even in the absence of explicit ground-truth supervision. Our work provides practical solutions for evaluating and building reliable RAG systems for safety-critical deployments.

replace-cross Efficient Subgroup Analysis via Optimal Trees with Global Parameter Fusion

Authors: Zhongming Xie, Joseph Giorgio, Jingshen Wang

Abstract: Identifying and making statistical inferences on differential treatment effects (commonly known as subgroup analysis in clinical research) is central to precision health. Subgroup analysis allows practitioners to pinpoint populations for whom a treatment is especially beneficial or protective, thereby advancing targeted interventions. Tree based recursive partitioning methods are widely used for subgroup analysis due to their interpretability. Nevertheless, these approaches encounter significant limitations, including suboptimal partitions induced by greedy heuristics and overfitting from locally estimated splits, especially under limited sample sizes. To address these limitations, we propose a fused optimal causal tree method that leverages mixed integer optimization (MIO) to facilitate precise subgroup identification. Our approach ensures globally optimal partitions and introduces a parameter fusion constraint to facilitate information sharing across related subgroups. This design substantially improves subgroup discovery accuracy and enhances statistical efficiency. We provide theoretical guarantees by rigorously establishing out of sample risk bounds and comparing them with those of classical tree based methods. Empirically, our method consistently outperforms popular baselines in simulations. Finally, we demonstrate its practical utility through a case study on the Health and Aging Brain Study Health Disparities (HABS-HD) dataset, where our approach yields clinically meaningful insights.

replace-cross Convergence Rate of the Last Iterate of Stochastic Proximal Algorithms

Authors: Kevin Kurian Thomas Vaidyan, Michael P. Friedlander, Ahmet Alacaoglu

Abstract: We analyze two classical algorithms for solving additively composite convex optimization problems where the objective is the sum of a smooth term and a nonsmooth regularizer. The first algorithm is the proximal stochastic gradient method for a single regularizer; the second is the randomized incremental proximal method, which uses the proximal operator of a randomly selected function when the regularizer is given as the sum of many nonsmooth functions. We focus on relaxing the bounded variance assumption that is common, yet stringent, for getting last iterate convergence rates. We prove the $\widetilde{O}(1/\sqrt{T})$ rate of convergence for the last iterate of both algorithms under componentwise convexity and smoothness, which is optimal up to log terms. Our results apply directly to graph-guided regularizers that arise in multi-task and federated learning, where the regularizer decomposes as a sum over edges of a collaboration graph.

replace-cross Surprisal-R\'enyi Free Energy

Authors: Shion Matsumoto, Benjamin Prada, Raul Castillo, Ankur Arjun Mali

Abstract: The forward and reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergences arise as limiting objectives in learning and inference, yet they induce markedly different inductive biases that cannot be explained by expectation terms alone. We introduce the Surprisal-R\'enyi Free Energy (SRFE), a normalized R\'enyi-Chernoff log-moment functional of the likelihood ratio. Although SRFE is algebraically related to R\'enyi divergence for fixed values of the order parameter, its endpoint-symmetric normalization yields a distinct free-energy viewpoint for analyzing how forward- and reverse KL behavior emerges near singular limits. We show that SRFE recovers the forward and reverse KL divergences as endpoint limits and derive local expansions around both regimes in which the variance of the log-likelihood ratio appears as a first-order correction away from the KL objective. These expansions reveal an explicit mean-variance tradeoff governing departures from KL-dominated behavior. We further establish a Gibbs-type variational characterization in terms of weighted KL projections and prove that SRFE controls large deviations of excess code length through Chernoff-type bounds, giving a Minimum Description Length interpretation of the functional. We evaluate SRFE on two synthetic settings (two-dimensional Gaussian-mixture variational inference and multi-class classification) and the long-tailed CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 (CIFAR-LT) datasets. On CIFAR-LT, retraining the best configuration of each loss over five seeds, SRFE matches cross-entropy accuracy on CIFAR-10-LT and gives mixed results on CIFAR-100-LT, improving few-shot accuracy at one of three imbalance factors. Together, these results position SRFE as a variance- and tail-sensitive, normalized R\'enyi-Chernoff free energy that clarifies the geometric, statistical, and large-deviation structure underlying the transition between forward- and reverse-KL limits.

replace-cross Power-Law Spectrum of the Random Feature Model

Authors: Elliot Paquette, Ke Liang Xiao, Yizhe Zhu

Abstract: Scaling laws for neural networks, in which the loss decays as a power-law in the number of parameters, data, and compute, depend fundamentally on the spectral structure of the data covariance, with power-law eigenvalue decay appearing ubiquitously in vision and language tasks. A central question is whether this spectral structure is preserved or destroyed when data passes through the basic building block of a neural network: a random linear projection followed by a nonlinear activation. We study this question for the random feature model: given data $x \sim N(0,H )\in \mathbb{R}^v$ where $H$ has $\alpha$-power-law spectrum ($\lambda_j( H ) \asymp j^{-\alpha}$, $\alpha > 1$), a Gaussian sketch matrix $W \in \mathbb{R}^{v\times d}$, and an entrywise polynomial $f$ of degree $p$, we characterize the eigenvalues of the population random-feature covariance $\mathbb{E}_{ x }[\frac{1}{d}f(. W ^\top x )^{\otimes 2}]$. We prove matching upper and lower bounds: for all $1 \leq j \leq c_1 d \log^{-(p+1)}(d)$, the $j$-th eigenvalue is of order $\left(\log^{p-1}(j+1)/j\right)^{\alpha}$. For $ c_1 d \log^{-(p+1)}(d)\leq j\leq d$, the $j$-th eigenvalue is of order $j^{-\alpha}$ up to a polylog factor. That is, the power-law exponent $\alpha$ is inherited exactly from the input covariance, modified only by a logarithmic correction that depends on the degree $p$ of the activation. The proof combines a dyadic head-tail decomposition with Wick chaos expansions for higher-order monomials and random matrix concentration inequalities; a Gaussian integration-by-parts argument then extends the result from monomials to polynomials

replace-cross TopoPilot: Reliable Conversational Workflow Automation for Topological Data Analysis and Visualization

Authors: Nathaniel Gorski, Shusen Liu, Bei Wang

Abstract: Recent agentic systems demonstrate that large language models can generate complex codebases from natural language prompts. Despite this progress, they present reliability concerns for scientific visualization due to invalid operations, subtle errors, and failure to resolve underspecified inputs. In this work, we introduce TopoPilot, a reliable and extensible agentic framework for automating complex scientific visualization workflows. We focus on topological data analysis and visualization as a primary use case, while designing the framework to generalize to broader visualization domains. TopoPilot employs a two-agent architecture: an orchestrator translates user prompts into workflows composed of atomic backend actions, while a verifier evaluates workflows prior to execution. Its modular design isolates components and supports new topological descriptors and domain-specific workflows without modifying the core system. It also employs numerous deterministic guardrails to ensure semantic consistency and accurate execution. To systematically improve reliability, we develop a taxonomy of failure modes and employ targeted safeguards for each class of error. Across 2,000 simulated multi-turn conversations spanning 100 prompts, including adversarial and infeasible requests, TopoPilot achieves a success rate exceeding 95%, compared with under 50% for a baseline without comprehensive guardrails and verification. We also show that TopoPilot outperforms Claude Code and Codex on similar prompts in terms of accuracy, completion time, and token usage.

replace-cross Exploring Urban Land Use Patterns by Pattern Mining and Unsupervised Learning

Authors: Zdena Dobesova, Tai Dinh, Pavel Novak

Abstract: Comparative planning needs reproducible methods for identifying recurring land-use configurations across cities. Using Urban Atlas 2018 data for 100 European urban areas, we construct 290,396 focal-neighborhood transactions and 1,543 frequent-itemset support features at 10% minimum support. Ward clustering is applied in the original normalized feature space, with UMAP used only for visualization. A seven-cluster descriptive solution is retained through multi-criterion evaluation and 500 paired feature-subsampling repetitions. Sensitivity analyses show stable city-similarity geometry across 5-15% thresholds but greater membership sensitivity to representation choices and non-artificial land-use content. The framework supports peer-city comparison while making scale and boundary limitations explicit.

replace-cross Free Decompression with Algebraic Spectral Curves

Authors: Siavash Ameli, Chris van der Heide, Liam Hodgkinson, Michael W. Mahoney

Abstract: Tools from random matrix theory have become central to deep learning theory, using spectral information to provide mechanisms for modeling generalization, robustness, scaling, and failure modes. While often capable of modeling empirical behavior, practical computations are limited by matrix size, often imposing a restriction to models that are too small to be realistic. This motivates the inference of properties of larger models from the behavior of smaller ones. Free decompression (FD) is a recently proposed method for extrapolating spectral information across matrix sizes, but its utility is currently limited by strong assumptions that preclude its implementation on more realistic machine learning (ML) models. We use algebraic spectral curve theory to provide a general FD methodology for spectral densities whose Stieltjes transform satisfies an algebraic relation, a modeling assumption that is more likely to hold in practice. This recasts FD as an evolution along spectral curves which can be readily integrated. Our framework enables the expansion of spectral densities that have multiple or multi-modal bulks, that exist at multiple scales, and that contain atoms, all characteristic of real-world data and popular ML models. We demonstrate the efficacy of our framework on models of interest in modern ML, including Hessian and activation matrices associated with neural networks and large-scale diffusion models.

replace-cross Fast Training of Mixture-of-Experts for Time Series Forecasting via Expert Loss Integration

Authors: Btissame El Mahtout, Florian Ziel

Abstract: We propose a novel Mixture-of-Experts framework, referred to as Expert Loss Integration MoE (EliMoE), for time series forecasting. EliMoE addresses the optimization problem arising from small gating weights by incorporating expert-specific losses, which provide each expert with a direct learning signal independent of the gate-assigned weight. Specifically, the overall objective comprises the base forecasting loss and expert-specific losses, allowing individual expert prediction errors to directly influence parameter updates alongside the aggregate forecasting error. The framework also encourages different experts to learn from different temporal segments of the data. The proposed framework is further combined with a partial online learning strategy that enables efficient incremental updates of model parameters. By integrating expert-level loss information with partial online learning, EliMoE improves forecasting performance while retaining computational efficiency. Empirical results across five benchmark datasets from diverse application domains with different sampling frequencies show that the proposed approach generally outperforms state-of-the-art supervised neural forecasting models, including Transformer-based architectures such as PatchTST, as well as zero-shot time-series foundation models such as TimeMoE. Furthermore, ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of the expert-specific loss integration strategy, highlighting its contribution to enhancing predictive performance.

replace-cross RefDecoder: Enhancing Visual Generation with Conditional Video Decoding

Authors: Xiang Fan, Yuheng Wang, Bohan Fang, Zhongzheng Ren, Ranjay Krishna

Abstract: Video generation powers a vast array of downstream applications. However, while the de facto standard, i.e., latent diffusion models, typically employ heavily conditioned denoising networks, their decoders often remain unconditional. We observe that this architectural asymmetry leads to significant loss of detail and inconsistency relative to the input image. To address this, we argue that the decoder requires equal conditioning to preserve structural integrity. We introduce RefDecoder, a reference-conditioned video VAE decoder by injecting high-fidelity reference image signal directly into the decoding process via reference attention. Specifically, a lightweight image encoder maps the reference frame into the detail-rich high-dimensional tokens, which are co-processed with the denoised video latent tokens at each decoder up-sampling stage. We demonstrate consistent improvements across several distinct decoder backbones (e.g., Wan 2.1 and VideoVAE+), achieving up to +2.1 dB PSNR over the unconditional baselines on the Inter4K, WebVid, and Large Motion reconstruction benchmarks. Notably, RefDecoder can be directly swapped into existing video generation systems without additional fine-tuning, and we report across-the-board improvements in subject consistency, background consistency, and overall quality scores on the VBench I2V benchmark. Beyond I2V, RefDecoder generalizes well to a wide range of visual generation tasks such as style transfer and video editing refinement.

replace-cross GENSTRAT: Toward a Science of Strategic Reasoning in Large Language Models

Authors: Vartan Shadarevian, Kia Ghods, Alex Kenich, Anany Kotawala

Abstract: Fixed game suites for evaluating strategic reasoning in large language models (LLMs) saturate and are hard to keep free of contamination. We introduce GENSTRAT, a system for evaluating the strategic capabilities of LLMs driven by procedural generation of two-player zero-sum imperfect-information betting games, allowing an evaluator to draw novel, fresh games on demand. Each game is characterized on a scale of five complexity axes (state space, temporal contingency, opponent dependence, private information, precision) and measured by Monte Carlo simulation. Using this generator, we construct a 50-game benchmark and hold a tournament of twelve frontier and open-weight models, gathering data from over 40,000 LLM vs. LLM matches. We further construct a composite complexity axis, and find that, on the eight hardest games, the gap between the best and worst model is 1.7x its value across the whole set of games (1.6x with held-out weights or with hard games chosen by the outcome-free temporal axis alone, and 1.35x with payoffs scaled by their random-play standard deviation). Ablations show that a higher thinking budget, disclosing the opponent's strategy, and higher reasoning matched to a game's specific demands (for private information and planning depth) were associated with higher margins for the LLM player. On 27 solved games, frontier models play at the value of the equilibrium while weaker models lose to it, and the leaderboard predicts strength on fresh games and on six new games, even after controlling for a general-capability leaderboard.

replace-cross Growing a Neural Network in Breadth, Depth, and Time

Authors: Eivinas Butkus, Kedar Garz\'on Gupta, Nikolaus Kriegeskorte

Abstract: Brains and AI systems alike must achieve their goals with limited space, time, energy, and data. Here we focus on space and time, defining differentiable costs for breadth (channels), depth (layers), and time (recurrent steps) in a recurrent convolutional network. We optimize these costs jointly with task error via backpropagation. Pricing each resource differently, we grow a diverse set of computational graphs through optimization. We find that breadth, depth, and time can be traded off against each other at a given level of accuracy. Networks grow with task complexity and, when allowed to adaptively allocate time per input, take more recurrent steps if inputs are occluded. The time the model spends on each image also correlates with human reaction times, despite no training on human data. Our work takes a step toward a normative account of how spatial and temporal resource costs jointly shape neural architectures in brains and machines.

replace-cross Reactivity-Informed Machine Learning for Performance Prediction and Design Space Exploration of Alkali-Activated Slag

Authors: Qiyao He, Zhanzhao Li, Kai Gong

Abstract: Establishing quantitative relationships among mix design, raw material properties, curing conditions, and performance remains a long-standing challenge in cementitious materials, particularly for alkali-activated materials with variable precursor and activator chemistry. Here, we curated the largest literature-derived alkali-activated slag (AAS) dataset to date, comprising over 3100 compressive strength records, 155 chemically distinct ground granulated blast-furnace slags (GGBSs), and 24 attributes incorporating precursor chemistry, fineness, and reactivity. Multiple machine learning (ML) algorithms were benchmarked across progressively enriched feature scenarios, demonstrating that integrating GGBS compositions, fineness, curing conditions, and specimen geometry improves predictive performance. The average metal oxide dissociation energy (AMODE), a physically interpretable representation of precursor reactivity, provides a compact alternative descriptor to explicit oxide compositions while enabling comparable predictive performance. Model interpretation revealed physically consistent trends from heterogeneous data, including non-monotonic effects of Na2O dosage and silicate modulus, reduced predicted strength at higher water content and larger specimen size, and coupled oxide-level effects more coherently represented by AMODE than by individual oxide contents. Statistically constrained design space exploration reveals reactivity-dependent trade-offs among strength, embodied CO2 emissions, and cost. The design maps identify high-strength regions with substantially lower CO2 emissions than OPC-based references at similar cost. Overall, this work demonstrates how reactivity-informed ML can extract physically meaningful trends from heterogeneous AAS data and guide source-dependent binder design. The curated dataset is publicly accessible to support advances in cement and concrete research.

replace-cross Auditing Reward Hackability in Code RL Training Environments

Authors: Shreshth Rajan

Abstract: Code RL rewards a policy for passing a task's tests, so a test suite that accepts a wrong patch trains the wrong behavior. We measure how often open code RL environments accept wrong patches and how reliably hackable tasks can be detected and repaired, checking each claim by execution against the task's gold patch. First, 22.4% of 49 SWE-bench Verified tasks accept a Docker-verified patch that behaves differently from the gold patch, and 57.8% against a stronger attacker. The gold-patch check is necessary: an attacker not shown the gold patch returns it for 31% of its passing "exploits". Second, across 134 public SWE-bench submissions, models pass hackable tasks 28.6 pp more often than tasks of the same difficulty, mostly because those tasks are passable at all; pooled over three attacker runs, hackable tasks still exceed matched passable ones (p = 0.014). Third, repair is harder to verify than detection: 61.9% of LLM-written repair tests fail on the gold patch, and even behind a gate an LLM judge is wrong on 38% of the verdicts execution can check. Finally, of 873 training trajectories behind an open coding agent run beside the gold fix, 19.4% answer differently on a case the issue describes; an LLM review that can only demote these executed differences judges 6.9% to be wrong fixes the tests rewarded (6.4-10.5% across three model reviews), and 5.4% in a second public training set (R2E-Gym). Audits and repairs of code RL environments should be decided by execution wherever execution can decide.

replace-cross AlphaWiSE: Adaptive Weight Interpolation for Continual Multimodal Representation Learning

Authors: Sarthak Jain, Qiran Hu, Zhen Zhu, Yaoyao Liu

Abstract: Multimodal retrieval models align audio, images, and text in a shared embedding space, but sequential adaptation can degrade cross-modal relationships learned in earlier phases. Existing continual-learning procedures produce a single final checkpoint, so every retrieval direction shares the same stability-plasticity trade-off. We introduce AlphaWiSE, a post-hoc weight-space interpolation method that combines a sequential fine-tuning endpoint with a companion endpoint obtained with a stability-oriented continual-learning method. AlphaWiSE fits one scalar interpolation coefficient for each corresponding parameter tensor on an exemplar memory while both endpoints remain frozen. The interpolated tensors are then materialized into a single checkpoint, so the deployed model retains the architecture and inference cost of a single backbone. On continual audio-image-text retrieval, the fused model improves on the better of its two endpoints in 13 of 18 average retrieval measures and on the fixed blend of the same checkpoints in 25 of 36 measures. Its pairings achieve the strongest average audio-to-text and image-to-audio retrieval among all compared methods. Project page: https://alphawise.cvmlgroup.web.illinois.edu/

replace-cross An Analytically Trained Variational Quantum Circuit using the Output Probability Distribution of Quantum Phase Estimation on NISQ Hardware

Authors: Mousumi Kundu, Ashish Kumar Patra, Anurag K. S. V., Ruchika Bhat, Sai Shankar P., Alok Shukla, Jaiganesh G

Abstract: Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE) is the standard algorithm for molecular ground-state energy estimation and often serves as a subroutine in larger quantum algorithms, but its deep circuits make it impractical on current noisy hardware. A previous variational approach replaces the QPE subroutine with a shallow Variational Quantum Circuit (VQC) trained to reproduce the QPE measurement distribution, but generates the training target by classically simulating the full QPE circuit, which is prohibitive for molecules. We reformulate this idea, computing the training target analytically from the Dirichlet kernel, using only a reference energy, the number of ancilla qubits and the evolution time, so the controlled-unitary and inverse-QFT operations of QPE are never simulated. Using H$_2$, we select the VQC stepwise by comparing its IBM Quantum hardware output with the target using four metrics. We first choose the entangler (linear or full) and number of layers of an $R_Y$-$R_Z$-$CZ$ ansatz, with and without dynamical decoupling (DD). As DD degraded performance with depth, we then choose, without DD, the ansatz ($R_Y$-$R_Z$-$CZ$ or $R_Y$-$CZ$) and the training simulator (ideal or noisy). The configuration fixed on H$_2$, a single-layer linear-entangler $R_Y$-$CZ$ ansatz trained on the ideal simulator, is validated on LiH, H$_2$O, BeH$_2$, NH$_3$, CH$_4$, O$_2$ and N$_2$, recovering the dominant QPE bitstring on hardware with errors within chemical accuracy ($1.59 \times 10^{-3}$ Ha) for all eight molecules at the chosen evolution times. We also analyze the hardware side lobes of both ans\"atze, and a noise analysis of the selected ansatz at 8 and 64 layers examines how DD performs with depth. As training requires a known reference energy, the surrogate is not meant for standalone energy estimation, but can replace the QPE subroutine where a larger algorithm uses only its measurement outcome.

replace-cross A Multi-Center Dataset and Benchmark for SYNTAX Segmentation of Coronary Angiograms

Authors: Dominik Bernard Lau, Jerzy Szyjut, Hubert Malinowski, Adam Brzeski, Tomasz Dziubich, Natalia Zieli\'nska, Tomasz Figatowski, Rados{\l}aw Targo\'nski

Abstract: Dense pixel-level classification of coronary angiography is essential for automated cardiovascular diagnostics, yet the computer vision community lacks gold-standard protocols and robust datasets to tackle this topologically complex, heavily long-tailed domain. To bridge this gap, we introduce CARDIAG: a rigorous, multi-center benchmark dataset designed to evaluate deep learning architectures on fine-grained SYNTAX segment classification. Unlike prior efforts, CARDIAG systematically prevents patient- and center-level data leakage while providing an unprecedented suite of annotations: highly imbalanced multi-class labels, explicit catheter and stenosis masks, expert uncertainty maps, temporal sequence frames, and spatial metadata. Using CARDIAG, we conduct the most extensive architectural sweep in this domain, benchmarking 24 models spanning classic CNNs, Vision Transformers (ViTs), and State-Space Models (SSMs). Moving beyond naive overlap metrics, we evaluate topological preservation (clDice), vessel diameter error, and uncertainty error-detection. Our analysis reveals an insight: modern large-kernel CNNs (particularly ConvNeXt V2 with DeepLabV3+) still outperform recent ViTs and SSMs under a shared training recipe, establishing a Dice = 0.456. By ensembling complementary models, we push the baseline to Dice = 0.479. Through leave-one-center-out cross-validation and data efficiency analyses, we explore the current limits of out-of-distribution generalization. Ultimately, CARDIAG establishes a foundation for advancing medical computer vision for cardiology across many downstream tasks, including lesion detection, temporal frame interpolation, and 3D reconstruction.

replace-cross Tokens are All You Need: Dual-purpose Semantic IDs for Achieving LLM-Level I/O Efficiency in recommendation systems

Authors: Baolei Li, Yiping Yuan, Yilin Zheng, Likang Yin, Ling Liu, Fabio Soldo, Romer Rosales, Xinyang Yi, Lichan Hong

Abstract: Large-scale recommendation systems face "Memory Wall" bottlenecks due to massive, dense embedding tables. While generative retrieval uses discrete tokens for IDs, high-dimensional context still relies on inefficient dense formats. Inspired by computer vision data compression, we propose Dual-purpose Semantic IDs to achieve LLM-level I/O efficiency. Our methodology uses hierarchical quantization to condense continuous embeddings into discrete Semantic IDs performing two concurrent roles: (1) Collaborative Identity: modeling user-item interactions via learnable embedding table; and (2) Content Reconstruction: using a lightweight Semantic Decoder for on-the-fly embedding approximation. This approach replaces massive vector storage with on-demand reconstruction, reducing system overhead and data footprints. We demonstrate the efficacy of our framework through offline evaluations and successful online deployment in production-scale ranking and retrieval systems at a major video sharing platform, showing that discrete tokens are indeed all you need for highly efficient, content-rich recommendation.

replace-cross Agentic Coding in the Wild: Characterizing GitHub Copilot Traces at Production Scale

Authors: Banruo Liu, Haoran Qiu, \'I\~nigo Goiri, Rodrigo Fonseca, Ricardo Bianchini, Esha Choukse

Abstract: AI coding agents (e.g., GitHub Copilot, Claude Code, Codex) interleave multi-step LLM inference with tool execution, creating a workload different from chatbots. We present the first production-scale characterization of coding agents using sampled GitHub Copilot traces from June-July 2026, comprising 7.4M users, 819M conversations, 5.7B LLM calls, and 946T tokens. Our analysis reveals distinctive workload properties with unique systems implications: e.g., sparse user-initiated turns unfold into autonomous agent loops with nearly 1:1 coupling between LLM calls and tool execution; parallel LLM calls are rare but exist in turns that trigger subagents; KV cache hit rates average 90% within a turn, but fall to 52% across turn boundaries and to 6% after model switches; workflows and user behaviors exhibit diverse, long-tailed resource footprints. We further distinguish short agentic turnaround times from minutes-long user idle periods between turns, and develop a lightweight idle-time predictor that captures 87-89% of total idle time. We demonstrate two use cases: (1) a harness-side LLM API keep-alive policy that preserves provider-side context KV cache, reducing token costs by 14.9% on turn boundaries; and (2) a serving-side KV cache residency policy that uses predicted idle time to guide DRAM offloading, reducing time to first token (TTFT) by 3-33% compared with LRU during a one-week evaluation with GitHub Copilot traces.

replace-cross Augmented Inverse Hybrid Weighting: Robust Inference under Deterministic and Random Distribution Shifts

Authors: Ying Jin, Dominik Rothenh\"ausler

Abstract: Reweighting source samples to match a target covariate distribution is a standard response to distribution shift when generalizing evidence across populations. It is well suited to deterministic, learnable covariate discrepancies, but can fail when source--target differences extend beyond covariate shift or when estimation of the density-ratio weights is unstable. We introduce a new model where the discrepancy between two population laws comprises a deterministic covariate shift and a non-systematic component, modeled as random perturbation of the probability space. This separates systematic shifts, treated as bias and corrected by reweighting, from random perturbations, treated as distributional uncertainty and reduced by dataset pooling. Under pure random perturbations, this principle yields Augmented Inverse Distance Weighting, which combines regression augmentation with variance-optimal dataset-level pooling and attains asymptotic variance no larger than exact covariate balancing. When both components are present, we develop Augmented Inverse Hybrid Weighting, which interpolates between AIDW and standard augmented importance weighting. Both estimators tradeoff sampling and distributional uncertainty via a distributional distance quantifying the strength of random perturbations. We establish asymptotic normality of both estimators and provide tuning and diagnostic tools. On three real-world multi-site datasets exhibiting diverse distribution shifts, the proposed methods consistently reduce mean-squared error compared with weighting baselines, and improve empirical coverage when covariate-shift adjustment alone undercovers.

replace-cross SiMDex: Mining Similar Egocentric Videos for Cross-Embodiment Dexterous Manipulation

Authors: Nie Lin, Takehiko Ohkawa, Sijin Chen, Ruoshi Wen, Zhuohang Li, Liqun Huang, Zhengming Zhu, Yiming Bao, Yunfei Li, Minjie Cai, Xiao Ma, Wei Xu, Yoichi Sato

Abstract: Recent years have witnessed an explosive trend of scaling ego-centric human videos for robot manipulation, yet it remains unclear which data actually benefits dexterous manipulation. We present SiMDex, a similarity-based data mining framework that casts human data selection for VLA post-training in dexterous manipulation as a recommendation problem. For each robot demonstration, SiMDex runs a recall-ranking-re-ranking cascade over ~32M egocentric human samples: two stages, recall and ranking, select a task-relevant subset in a morphology-agnostic action space, and re-ranking verifies it on pixels, with no change to VLA architecture or training. Against a strong baseline trained with an equal amount of randomly sampled human data, SiMDex uses only ~1.49M mined samples (<5% of the pool) yet improves the overall success rate from 47.7% to 61.7%, showing that selective curation outperforms indiscriminate data mixing.

replace-cross Why Does CLAUDE.md Keep Growing? Catastrophic Remembering in Agentic Coding

Authors: Kushal Chakrabarti

Abstract: Agentic prompts like CLAUDE.md grow without bound in real repositories, stopping only when history ends or someone rewrites the file wholesale. We trace this to imperfect recall: appending an instruction is always cheap, but once an instruction's latent reasoning is gone, deleting it without risking a regression costs O(2^|D|) in a prompt of |D| instructions. Successive edits destroy that latent reasoning, additions outrun deletions and prompts grow until agents become non-functional -- catastrophic remembering. First, we characterize this phenomenon across 247,694 instruction lifetimes in 1,867 repositories: agentic prompts grow nearly monotonically, more than tripling over their lifetime (+226%) at +4.7 net instructions per commit. Deletion hazard decays geometrically with instruction age, consistent with imperfect recall and with no other rival we test. Then, we show that prompt comments can halt the growth: in verifiable worlds built by inverting IFEval, comments encoding latent reasoning remove 99.3% of excess size (+211.3% to +1.4%). Finally, we show comments yield smaller real-world and coding-convention prompts than existing context maintainers, grow more effective with model capability and, at small scale, improve instruction-following in real repositories by 17.7%. If English is the new code, why don't we have comments yet?

replace-cross Fractal dimension predicts quantum kernel collapse in angle-encoded data

Authors: Ana Paula Appel

Abstract: Angle-encoded quantum kernels on tabular data collapse when the feature map is wider than the intrinsic dimension of the data. We propose the correlation fractal dimension D2 as an a priori qubit budget: encode D2 coordinates chosen by FD-ASE instead of the PCA-95% width or all E attributes. On nine data sets and a statevector simulator (n= 32), a one-layer ZZ fidelity kernel at q=D2 stays geometrically alive while the same kernel at the PCA-95% width has already collapsed. The budget is map-dependent: product-state and IQP maps overshoot it; a second ZZ layer undershoots it. Packed dense-angle and re-uploading encodings still live at the fractal q, but not when PCA-95% features are stacked onto those qubits. Shrinking the angle bandwidth moves the ZZ knee later; stretching it kills the kernel earlier. On IBM Quantum (ibm_fez, 256 shots, n=8) the one-layer ZZ kernel at the fractal width matches the exact kernel (MAE 0.021); past that width both hardware and simulator have collapsed. The ceiling is a property of the map-data pair at a stated bandwidth, not of the classical table alone.

replace-cross Self-Supervised Learning for Robust Resonance Mass Regression in Cascade Decays

Authors: Ho Fung Tsoi, Alex Yang, Luis Felipe Gutierrez Zagazeta, Shion Chen, Dylan Sheldon Rankin

Abstract: Reconstructing the mass of a heavy resonance from its decay products with missing energy is one of the central tasks that directly determine the sensitivity in new physics searches at collider experiments. Supervised learning approaches to this problem often struggle to generalize well due to the presence of various systematic uncertainties and distribution shifts. Exhausting all possible variations in the labeled data can be very compute-intensive, while a failure of the model to generalize can corrupt the reconstructed resonance widths that are critical in peak-hunting analyses. In this work, following the foundation model paradigm, we use a self-supervised approach to pre-train a transformer encoder with VICReg to learn an embedding invariant to various corruptions, then fine-tune it for mass regression on a heavy resonance with masses ranging from 2.5 to 6.5 TeV and a SUSY-like cascade decay into an eleven-body final state. We show that the pre-trained model reconstructs sharper resonance peaks and has a more stable performance under various realistic corruptions, compared to a supervised model of the same architecture trained on the same augmented data from scratch.

replace-cross Similarity Pairing with Energy Mover's Distance for Self-Supervised Pre-Training at the LHC

Authors: Ho Fung Tsoi, Dylan Sheldon Rankin

Abstract: Many self-supervised methods for training foundation models at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) rely on data augmentations to encourage the model to embed events into a representation space invariant to certain physical or detector symmetries. A common challenge arises from the large freedom in choosing a proper set of augmentations on which downstream performance depends. The implementation of augmentations involves either modifying existing events, potentially breaking the event fidelity, or simulating more event variants, which is computationally intensive. In this work, we present a data-driven method of pairing events by their similarity via the energy mover's distance (EMD), which measures how similar two events are in terms of the work required to transform one into the other. With this approach, distinct events are sampled and matched by their similarity to serve as views for learning invariance, keeping the physics content of each event intact without handcrafted distortions. We demonstrate this augmentation-free pairing method by pre-training on QCD jets via self-distillation and show that it can yield semantic jet embeddings with downstream discrimination power comparable to or better than an augmentation-based baseline.

replace-cross AECSF: Adaptive Ensemble Conditional Score Filtering for High-Dimensional Nonlinear Data Assimilation

Authors: Yangwen Zhang, Shiwei Ni, Xiaoping Zhang, Xiaofei Guan, Lili Ju

Abstract: In high-dimensional filters, particle weights can concentrate on a few members, while Gaussian updates can miss non-Gaussian posterior structure. A diffusion filter updates a forecast ensemble by gradually denoising particles in light of a new observation. Each noisy particle can correspond to several system states, and these states can have very different likelihoods. A score correction based on a single state can therefore misrepresent the posterior. We propose the Adaptive Ensemble Conditional Score Filter (AECSF), which uses the conditional Tweedie identity to express the required score through the mean state given the particle and the observation. All reverse particles share a set of proposals, but each assigns its own weights to estimate its conditional mean. As denoising proceeds, the reverse particles guide which proposals are resampled. We characterize when fixed proposals yield the exact noisy posterior score and bound the contribution of conditional-mean errors to the final sampling error. In controlled Gaussian experiments, AECSF samples the posterior more accurately than the tested likelihood-guidance rules. With 20 forecast members in 10,000-dimensional Lorenz-96, AECSF has lower RMSE and CRPS than the tested tempered SMC and kernel-density filters. On Kuramoto-Sivashinsky, the analysis ensembles also improve subsequent forecasts over the tested baselines at comparable cost. For Navier-Stokes flow, we learn a forecast prior that captures spatial dependence in vorticity and use it to reconstruct the full field from sparse velocity measurements.

replace-cross Multi-Task Self-Evolving Harnesses with the Agent as Its Own Optimizer

Authors: Qiankai Xu

Abstract: A harness is the code around a language model agent that organizes prompts, calls tools, manages context, and controls execution. As models grow stronger, recent work has begun to let agents improve their own harnesses, a line of work known as self-evolving harnesses. In most existing methods, a separate proposer running on a human-designed harness modifies the solver's harness, and a separate harness is evolved for each benchmark. Real-world tasks come from many domains, so both the evolution and the evaluation of a harness should cover a diverse range of tasks. We propose a framework close to recursive self-improvement: the same frozen model, on the same version of the harness, first solves tasks as the solver and then, as the proposer, reads the complete run records and directly edits the harness that runs it. Each evolution batch draws tasks from five benchmarks in different domains. To measure generalization, training and held-out tasks are strictly separated, and we additionally evaluate on five out-of-distribution benchmarks not used in the first stage. The design borrows from deep learning training and has two stages, multi-task pretraining and continual training. Starting from a 49-line seed harness, the harnesses evolved by GLM-5.3 and GPT-5.6 Sol gain about 5 points on the in-distribution benchmarks. They are on par with Claude Code and Codex on these benchmarks, and the GPT-5.6 Sol harness is also on par with Codex on five out-of-distribution benchmarks. The weaker model of each family benefits less: GLM-5.3-Flash gains less than 2 points, and the harness evolved by GPT-5.6 Luna scores below the seed. Continued evolution on Claw-Eval in the second stage further improves GPT-5.6 Sol and GLM-5.3-Flash on its multimodal tasks. We also analyze where the gains come from and the mechanisms that emerged during evolution. Code: https://github.com/xqkkk/self-evolving-harness.

URLs: https://github.com/xqkkk/self-evolving-harness.

replace-cross A Dynamical Theory of LoRA in Continual Learning

Authors: Th\'eo Marchetta, Filippo Alessandroni, Alessandro Breccia, Alessandro Ingrosso, Federica Gerace

Abstract: Despite the widespread use of Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), little is known about its dynamics in continual learning and the mechanisms by which low-rank updates affect catastrophic forgetting. We provide an asymptotically exact dynamical characterization of LoRA in a solvable two-task teacher-student model. In the high-dimensional online-learning limit, we derive a closed system of ordinary differential equations for a finite set of macroscopic order parameters, yielding exact expressions for the generalization errors throughout both the initial Task 1 learning phase and the subsequent LoRA fine-tuning on Task 2. The theory quantitatively matches finite-dimensional simulations and exposes two characteristic effects of LoRA: when the first-task representation is not yet specialized, low-rank adaptation reduces interference with features learned on the first task, but its initialization slows adaptation to the second task. Building on this mechanistic picture, we analyze a state-dependent masking strategy that freezes hidden units carrying the strongest first-task representations and restricts adaptation to the complementary subspace. This structural partitioning markedly reduces forgetting, while preserving plasticity on the new task. Our framework further clarifies the role of adapter rank: transfer improves only up to the intrinsic dimensionality of the target task and saturates beyond it, while forgetting continues to grow with rank. These results provide a dynamical and geometric account of how low-rank adaptation organizes information across sequential tasks, and are qualitatively reproduced on sequential MNIST and CIFAR-100 using a shallow network and ResNet-18, respectively.

replace-cross Disentangling Self-Distillation: Measuring and Modeling Acquisition and Retention

Authors: Luis Zuin, Alexis Huet, Dario Rossi, Zied Ben Houidi

Abstract: Self-distillation with privileged context adapts a language model from demonstrations by letting the model, once conditioned on a reference response, teach its context-free copy token by token. Our taxonomy reveals existing methods differ along three entangled axes: (i) the rollout source (student or teacher), (ii) the teacher coupling (frozen, or an exponential moving average of the student at some coupling rate) and (iii) the token-level divergence (here, reverse or forward KL), yet these axes are usually studied in fixed combinations and have led to conflicting conclusions. We formalize a unifying framework that covers both self-distillation and classic supervised fine-tuning: we train every combination of the three axes, on Qwen2.5-7B and Ministral-3-3B across ordinary and contradictory tasks, totaling 1,200 adaptation runs, to systematically investigate the impact of the above axes. We propose a controlled model of the same objective to interpret the resulting acquisition-retention trade-offs. We find that (i) the rollout source matters mostly where the task contradicts the pretrained behavior: there teacher rollouts raise acquisition (the accuracy gained on the new task) well above what student rollouts achieve, with almost no change in retention (the change in accuracy on general benchmarks); (ii) the teacher coupling changes acquisition most, on every task: acquisition rises with the coupling rate, then falls past a task-specific rate; (iii) switching the KL direction costs retention in one model but not the other, so which axis to tune first depends on the model. The controlled model reproduces the three trends.

replace-cross AI Safety Considerations for Agents With Limited Time to Act

Authors: Leo Zeitler, Jack Richings, Victoria Nockles

Abstract: In the wake of the increasingly public discussion about AI alignment, recent work has tried to propose specific AI architectures that behave safely. However, the proposed arguments that seemingly demonstrate proved alignment mostly neglect the environment the agent needs to act in. We discuss theoretical bounds for agent-agnostic safety guarantees in environments that can only be partially observed and within which an action is required within limited time. We introduce two realistic scenarios, one with an infinite state space and one with signal mixture. In these scenarios, we prove that even a perfect agent cannot guarantee safe behaviour. It will be argued that for any proof of AI safety or alignment, the environment and associated safe actions need to be specifically considered together with the agent.

replace-cross NucleoNet: A Nuclear-Aware Determinant Ansatz for Multi-Geometry Neural Variational Monte Carlo

Authors: Qiao Gao, Yang Huang, Enze Hou, Yuzhi Liu, Jingrun Chen, Han Wang

Abstract: Neural-network variational Monte Carlo provides an accurate route to solving the electronic Schr\"odinger equation, but many neural wavefunctions are optimized for a single nuclear geometry, requiring separate calculations to map out a potential energy surface. We introduce NucleoNet, a nuclear-aware determinant ansatz that jointly evolves electronic and nuclear representations through a shared backbone, with antisymmetry enforced by electronic determinants. Across eight equilibrium small-molecule benchmarks, NucleoNet achieves energies comparable to established neural-wavefunction baselines with substantially fewer trainable parameters, totaling 0.10--0.13 million. For each molecular system, a single model optimized jointly across geometries describes the N$_2$ dissociation curve, Li displacement in LiCN, and the ethane and formamide minimum-energy paths for bond dissociation. Models trained on sparse geometry sets generalize to unseen configurations in N$_2$ and LiCN without further optimization. For N$_2$, training on six geometries yields a mean absolute error (MAE) of $1.13\ \mathrm{m}E_h$ on a 32-point evaluation grid, compared with $1.07\ \mathrm{m}E_h$ when optimized on all 32 geometries. Ablation studies on N$_2$ show that propagating nuclear representations improves geometric generalization under both shared and nucleus-specific envelope parameterizations.

replace-cross CoCoNet: Convex Conformal Network for Multi-Dimensional Uncertainty Regions

Authors: Alireza Moradi, Thomas Bruys, Pascal Van Hentenryck, Reza Zandehshahvar

Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) provides distribution-free coverage guarantees under exchangeability, but the efficiency of its prediction regions depends critically on the choice of the conformity score. This is particularly important in multivariate regression, where dependencies across output dimensions induce complex joint uncertainty structures. This paper introduces CoCoNet (Convex Conformal Network) for learning conformity scores that induce flexible and data-adaptive prediction regions. The main mechanism is an input-dependent convex score learned end-to-end: CoCoNet trains the score on held-out data by directly minimizing the size of its sublevel sets, while enforcing convexity with respect to the residual so that every prediction region is inherently convex. CoCoNet then applies split-conformal calibration to retain finite-sample coverage guarantees. The paper presents two variants of the method: 1) CoCoNet-Spline, based on piecewise-linear scores aggregated by an $\ell_\infty$ or an $\ell_1$ norm, which induce hyper-rectangular and polytope prediction regions, respectively; and 2) CoCoNet-ICNN, based on Input Convex Neural Networks, which induces more flexible convex regions. Experiments compare CoCoNet with other CP baselines over synthetic and real-world datasets with output dimensions ranging from 2 to 16. These results demonstrate the importance of learning input-adaptive conformity scores directly from the data.

replace-cross Can Agents Keep Secrets They Were Never Told? Benchmarking Latent Privacy Risks Across Sessions with MemJoin

Authors: Qishun Yang, Shu Yang, Difei Xu, Di Wang, Meng Ding

Abstract: To support sustained interactions and personalized assistance, agents use long-term memory to retain and combine user information across sessions. Reusing such memories can lead an agent to state a sensitive relation while completing an ordinary task that does not require it. The relation to protect and the combination of clues that reveal it may remain hidden at memory write time, when later records and tasks are still unknown. In this paper, we introduce MemJoin, a privacy benchmark of 288 synthetic analysis cases in which the complete history entails a sensitive relation, but every individual session is insufficient. We evaluate six memory systems with the same answer-generating model, alongside full-history and no-history controls. Each system writes and updates memories in session order and later retrieves context for an ordinary task, while target annotations remain with the evaluator. We separately check which target attribute values appear in stored records and retrieved context, and whether the final answer states the complete, person-linked relation. In a separate paired test on a prespecified subset, removing shared identifiers and other connecting clues can change complete-relation output while all checked target attribute values remain visible. These findings show why checking attribute visibility alone is insufficient to evaluate what records from different sessions reveal together. The benchmark and code are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MemJoin-01BF/.

replace-cross Right On Q: Recovering Reasoning under Quantization via Self-Generated Trajectories

Authors: Boyuan Zheng, Jing Xiong, Chenwei Wu, Hui Shen, Zesen Zhao, Hei Ting Una Chan, Xueshen Liu, Zhen Zhang, Liyue Shen, Zechun Liu, Z. Morley Mao

Abstract: Quantization-aware training (QAT) aims to recover reasoning capabilities in low-bit large language models (LLMs). Existing recipes typically supervise the quantized student with the original full-precision model on fixed response sequences. However, the resulting low-bit quantized models still struggle on long-horizon reasoning tasks and often fail to terminate within a token budget that suffices for the full-precision model. We identify two challenges in closing this gap. First, fixed-response supervision leaves a mismatch between training prefixes and those encountered during student generation, and this trajectory divergence grows as responses lengthen. Second, on its own prefixes, the student underpredicts some next-token candidates and overpredicts others, and this distributional misalignment becomes more frequent at high-entropy prefixes. We present OnQ, a low-bit recovery pipeline that combines on-policy distillation and candidate-wise Dynamic KL. Starting from block-wise calibration, the quantized student generates responses and receives full-precision teacher supervision on its own prefixes. Dynamic KL weights forward and reverse KL by each candidate's teacher-student probability ratio to balance correction of under- and overprediction. Across Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B at 2- and 3-bit precision, OnQ achieves the highest nine-benchmark average, with the largest gains on long-horizon reasoning benchmarks. At 2 bits, it outperforms every baseline on every benchmark at every model size, improving the Qwen3-8B average by 10.26 points over the strongest baseline. It also produces shorter responses with fewer truncations and repetitive errors.

replace-cross ReDence: A Hybrid Agentic and System One Model Framework for Rapid, Evidence-Grounded Detection of Social Engineering Scams

Authors: Jaishnoor Kaur, Josef Hor\'a\v{c}ek, Ian Waskom, Sayak Saha Roy

Abstract: Detecting online social engineering (SE) scams often requires examining website workflows, verifying claims against external sources, and connecting evidence across multiple pages. When deployed on a real-world URL stream over the period of 28 days, ReDence identified 4,042 new SE scam websites across 17 categories, of which at least 71% were missed by all four popular anti-scam blocklists evaluated. replace-cross Reinforcement Learning Under Runtime Confounding: Policy Learning and Off-Policy Evaluation

Authors: Raphael Kim, Michele Santacatterina, Ramin Zabih, Keith Barnatchez

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is increasingly used to train robust, adaptable systems across healthcare, e-commerce, and robotics. In these settings, legal, ethical, or logistical concerns may preclude certain key variables that drive decisions from being collected during deployment time. This gives rise to a setting known as runtime confounding: the logged data hold full observability, but deployment time lacks knowledge of certain variables, known as runtime confounders. Unlike single timepoint and non-adaptive settings, runtime confounding under reinforcement learning has the potential to propagate over time, as earlier decisions affect future states, observations, and action assignments, leading to biased learning. We address these problems with three contributions: we (1) develop policy learning algorithms under runtime-confounding, (2) develop semi-parametric efficient off-policy estimation procedures, and (3) provide diagnostics for understanding the price of runtime confounding.

replace-cross Why Tabular Foundation Models Excel on Relational Data They Have Never Seen

Authors: Linjie Xu, Yiqun Hu, Jixiang He, David Wipf

Abstract: Given a relational database (RDB) storing heterogeneous tabular information, how can we predict missing (or future) values in some target column of interest? As candidate targets are numerous in enterprise settings, it is preferable to avoid learning a new model from scratch each time there is a new prediction task. Frozen foundation models (FMs) based on in-context learning (ICL) and RDB-specific pre-training provide a viable solution, but the ideal design still remains largely unresolved. Perhaps unexpectedly, preliminary evidence has recently shown that existing single-table FMs (aka TFMs), when combined with suitable parameter-free relational encoders, can be quite competitive in practice despite zero exposure to RDB pre-training or relational data of any kind. In this work we first empirically confirm this phenomenon with a more diverse array of experiments. We then turn to our main contribution, namely, surfacing multiple complementary explanations for why TFMs behave so effectively. These explanations ultimately hinge on innate synergies whereby parameter-free encoders map relational effects to fixed-length embeddings that loosely mimic distributions for which TFMs were originally designed; in so doing, desirable single-table properties transfer to relational settings (e.g., convergent posterior predictive distributions). Collectively, our analysis forges new pathways for improving RDB FMs, particularly when we prioritize interpretability and inevitable integration within agentic systems.

replace-cross Unsatz: Evaluating Scientific Discovery Without a Given Ansatz

Authors: Ethan Ji, Wei Wang, Yizhou Sun

Abstract: Before a scientist fits any parameter, they must decide what the model contains: which quantities exist, which are hidden, how they interact, and how they are measured. Physicists call such a trial form an ansatz. Benchmarks for automated equation discovery mostly supply it, by fixing the variables, a library of candidate terms, or a law to be modified. We introduce Unsatz, a benchmark in which an LLM agent must construct the ansatz itself. Each task is a simulated system, generated from a private seed as a program in a small modeling language: ordinary, delay, or partial differential equations, discrete maps, static and causal models, or stochastic processes, possibly with latent states, distorted sensors, and uninformative measurement channels. The agent buys experiments within a budget, writes candidate programs in the same language, obtains their coefficients from the benchmark's fitter, and is scored only on how its final program predicts experiments it never saw: conditions outside the ordinary ranges, interventions, and longer runs. Across 80 systems in 20 domains, nine language models solve between 21% and 60%. On the 64 deterministic systems, the two strongest agents solve 39 and 31, and classical and neural baselines together 14. The difficulty lies in finding the structure, not in the data: in 55% of the agents' failed investigations, the experiments they bought suffice once the true program is given with unknown coefficients. We also start agents on a related system from the program of a first system, either the one they discovered or the true one; agents holding a correct program solve the related system in 15 of 42 investigations, and agents without one in 5 of 64. Stronger agents make fewer unscientific errors; beyond these, agents that find a system's variables still miss how they act, and prefer revisions that lower the training error to ones that predict better.

Authors: Mateo Cl\'emente, George Z. Xue, Amin Abyaneh, Liheng Ma, Rui Heng Yang, Tongtong Cao, Zhanguang Zhang, Yingxue Zhang

Abstract: World-action models adapt pretrained video generators, and vision-language-action models build on vision-language backbones, tying policy architecture and inference cost to large transformers designed for generation rather than control. Its S and B policies run two to five times faster than Fast-WAM and Faster-WAM while outperforming both on LIBERO-Plus and RoboTwin 2.0, and La-JEPA outperforms the closest JEPA-based methods, LeapBot-WA and JEPA-WAM, without their pretrained policy backbones. On four real-robot tasks, its average success is comparable to $\pi_{0.5}$. A design study shows that JEPA features outperform video VAE latents, most clearly out of distribution, and that training-time future prediction improves the policy even when skipped at inference. Tailoring the policy's design, including its latent space, to control can thus matter more than the knowledge transferred from a pretrained generative backbone, suggesting that robot foundation models may benefit from architectures designed for control rather than inherited from generation.

replace-cross SLA-Aware Task Offloading in the IoT Edge-Cloud Continuum: Class-Weighted Deep Reinforcement Learning Against an Exact Optimum

Authors: Shilpa Golla, Tharun Kumar Korine Palli, Urmila Bandaru

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (RL) is often used to decide where Internet of Things (IoT) tasks with different latency limits run in the edge-cloud continuum, yet some methods give every missed task the same penalty and few compare their agents with an optimal policy. replace-cross Audit Before You Merge: Provenance, Probing, and Continual LoRA Composition

Authors: Lenny Aharon, Neta Glazer, Lior Aharon

Abstract: Post-hoc merging of public LoRA adapters is an attractive gradient-free path to continual adaptation, but reliable measurement of catastrophic forgetting in this setting depends on infrastructure that prior work has not addressed. The same protocol replicates on Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, where 2 of 4 candidate adapters fail the audit and the audited merge reproduces the same merge-method ranking on GSM8K, supporting that our findings are not Llama-specific.

replace-cross The Approximation Ratio of $k$-means++ Is $2\ln k+O(1)$

Authors: Aravind Reddy

Abstract: Given a finite set of points in Euclidean space, $k$-means clustering seeks $k$ centers that minimize the sum of squared distances from each point to its nearest center. The $k$-means++ algorithm chooses its first center uniformly at random from the data points and each subsequent center with probability proportional to its squared distance from the nearest center already chosen. Let $R_k$ be the worst-case ratio between the expected cost of the $k$ seeds chosen by $k$-means++ and the optimal $k$-means cost. In their seminal paper introducing $k$-means++, Arthur and Vassilvitskii (SODA 2007) showed that $2\ln k\le R_k\le8(\ln k+2)$, and Makarychev, Reddy and Shan (NeurIPS 2020) improved the upper bound to $5(\ln k+2)$. We prove that $R_k=2\ln k+O(1)$; more precisely, $2H_{k-1}+2\le R_k\le 2H_{k-2}+5$ for $k\ge2$, where $H_j$ is the $j$th harmonic number, so the two bounds differ by less than $3$. We also determine $R_2=5$ and $R_3=185/32$, with a unified proof of both upper bounds, and give a second family of lower bounds that exceeds $2H_{k-1}+2$ for $2\le k\le18$. Previous analyses bound the expected cost of a cluster just after it is first sampled by a constant times its optimal cost, and then charge that cost for the rest of the run. We track how a cluster's cost evolves after its first hit. At each subsequent draw, the probability of sampling that cluster again is exactly its share of the current cost. We show that the cost reductions from these later hits suffice, in expectation, to bring the leading coefficient down to $2$.

replace-cross AscendGYM: A Cost-Aware Kernel Optimization Benchmark for Real-World Workloads on Ascend NPUs

Authors: SLAI AI Training Platform Team, Bingwei Zhang, Jing Li, Amir Abdullin, Sihang Chen, Xin Chen, Liangjun Feng, Yingmeng Gao, Tao Guo, Junjie Huang, Dongfang Li, Zhanglian Lin, Jiajun Liu, Zekun Liu, Zhengxuan Lu, Xiaodong Luo, Jian Meng, Ivan Nosov, Linyuan Qiu, Weijian Sun, Wanrui Yan, Zhicheng Zhou, Baotian Hu, Min Zhang, Haizhou Li, Zhiquan Luo

Abstract: Kernel optimization is essential for efficient large language model (LLM) training and inference on Ascend NPUs. Existing Ascend kernel benchmarks primarily focus on kernel generation, with less emphasis on the ability of agents to optimize existing implementations under input configurations drawn from real workloads. To address this requirement, we introduce AscendGYM, a benchmark whose initial suite comprises 48 kernels across three difficulty levels. Each kernel is paired with test cases whose data types and tensor shapes are collected from real-world training and inference tasks. Consequently, performance improvements on AscendGYM map directly to the corresponding workloads. In terms of usability, two evaluation modes are supported in AscendGYM: model evaluation and model-harness evaluation. The kernel optimization capabilities of both trained models and custom agents are allowed to be measured. It provides a complete execution environment that closely reproduces how Ascend kernels are invoked in practical pipelines. For model evaluation, it additionally includes a built-in harness equipped with search orchestration and experience accumulation. Finally, we propose a new metric, the Speedup-Token Score (STS), which quantifies performance gains relative to token cost. By jointly considering speedup and cost, STS enables the assessment of practical kernel optimization capability of a model or harness under an acceptable budget. AscendGYM is available at https://github.com/SLAI-AITP/AscendGYM.

URLs: https://github.com/SLAI-AITP/AscendGYM.

replace-cross Finite speed Ornstein-Uhlenbeck-Kac processes and their applications in generative modeling

Authors: Richard Duong, Janis Auffenberg, Gabriele Steidl

Abstract: We study the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck-Kac (OU-K) process, where instead of a Brownian motion, a finite-speed Kac process defines the driving process. The OU-K process can be analyzed through a rescaled, time-inhomogeneous Kac process, characterized by a switching rate from the Kac part, and a convergence rate from the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) part. The law of this inhomogeneous Kac process describes the solution of a wave equation, the Euler-Poisson-Darboux (EPD) equation. For Kac switching rates being multiples of the OU convergence rate, we can explicitly derive this EPD solution via the sum of (the derivative of) two Legendre polynomials, restricted between two Dirac points representing the wave fronts. Using the framework of piecewise-deterministic Markov processes (PDMP), we describe the conditional velocity fields of the OU-K process via our polynomial-law-formula. This allows us to apply the OU-K process in a generative flow model. Since the OU-K velocity fields prove to be bounded by the finite Kac speed, the usual time truncations of diffusion models become unnecessary. We demonstrate advantages over diffusion and flow matching models in terms of generation sharpness on singular data sets, while achieving competitive results on CIFAR-10 image generation.

replace-cross Training Reasoning Models in Simulation to Natively Use Real Robots

Authors: Jiaming Tang, Zhuoyang Zhang, Junxian Guo, Fangzhou Zhao, Yilong Zhao, Zekai Li, Xingyang Li, Yuyang Zhao, Song Bian, Zhijian Liu, Song Han

Abstract: Robot-use agents (RUAs) have emerged as a promising approach to general-purpose robot control. However, strong performance has so far been achieved only by frontier proprietary models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.2, whereas open-weight models remain substantially less capable. We show that an open-weight 27B reasoning model can be trained to use real robots natively through reinforcement learning, entirely in simulation and without demonstrations. Like a computer-use agent (CUA), the model observes the environment, reasons in a chain of thought, and generates the next end-effector pose, without any harness. We build a Post-training Engine for Agentic Robotics (PEAR) and use it to train the model on simulated robot tasks at scale with robot-use reinforcement learning (RURL), mirroring how frontier models acquire computer use. We find that the skills acquired in simulation generalize well beyond the training tasks: they transfer to an unseen robot, to the real world, and to novel instructions. After training on LIBERO and RoboDojo in simulation, the model succeeds in 60% of our trials on a real dual-arm robot, whereas the base model succeeds in none. On LIBERO, training raises the success rate from 1.6% to 74.4%, approaching GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. Compared with $\pi_{0.5}$, a vision-language-action model fine-tuned on demonstrations, our model is substantially more robust to changes in the task itself. On LIBERO-Pro, $\pi_{0.5}$ succeeds in only 0.75% of episodes when assigned a new task in a familiar scene whereas our model succeeds in 57.4%. Finally, the model retains its general capabilities, with no measurable degradation on nine reasoning, coding and vision benchmarks. These results highlight robot-use reinforcement learning as a promising component of foundation-model post-training.

replace-cross SPARC: Evidence-guided adaptive model composition for materials discovery

Authors: Sissi Feng, Yuchen Han, Runze Zhang, Jiaru Bai, Yixi Li, Brandon Sutherland, Ali Shayesteh Zeraati, Jason Hattrick-Simpers, Varinia Bernales, Al\'an Aspuru-Guzik, Yang Bai

Abstract: Closed-loop experimentation requires more than selecting the next experiment. It must also determine which existing measurements and model-derived relationships are sufficiently supported to influence that decision. Here we introduce SPARC (Sparse Predictive Aggregation of Regression Components), a framework that continually revises this support as experimental evidence accumulates. Competing model families are evaluated through out-of-fold predictions, so that each model is tested on observations not used in its corresponding fit. Sparse posterior composition assigns model influence according to predictive contributions supported by the out-of-fold evidence, retaining complementary contributions from several model families. Candidate-dependent predictive dispersion and residual variability define trust, which modulates exploration alongside the predicted response. Independent diagnostics also prioritize existing measurements for prospective verification, with model-derived trust reported alongside them. Across eight published materials datasets, SPARC achieved higher $R^2$ than the corresponding literature-reported values in six, with relative gains of up to 77% without the density-functional-theory- or experiment-derived descriptors used by several published pipelines. In an automated alkaline oxygen-evolution-reaction campaign, 8 of 11 SPARC-selected experiments reached overpotentials below 300 mV, with a best value of 242 mV, compared with 18 of 125 initial experiments. Measurements prioritized for verification before repetition subsequently deviated by 76 mV on average, compared with 27 mV for matched controls. SPARC therefore turns experimental evidence into a basis for revising model influence, directing exploration and identifying measurements that warrant verification.

replace-cross Grounding Language Models in Speech Does Not Fix "AI Flavor": Calibrated LLM Judges, Goodhart Traps, and a Preference-Tuning Fix

Authors: Dongwu Qian

Abstract: Chinese outputs of large language models carry a perceptible "AI flavor": formulaic sentence frames, stacked written-register connectives, repetition loops, and layout leakage. A popular hypothesis blames text-only training for the missing inner-speech layer of human writing; injecting speech or prosody signals should therefore reduce it. We test it with controlled interventions on a 0.6B model; all three fail: (i) phoneme-interleaved continued pretraining yields no spokenness gain under text-matched controls while raising repetition (+0.13 [+0.03, +0.23]); (ii) prosody-plan conditional generation reaches an adherence of only 0.257 against a pre-registered 0.50 gate; (iii) a rule-based reward built on TTS prosody-feature distance is not distinguishable in double-blind evaluation by either humans (8/16) or a calibrated LLM judge (9/16). A root cause is evaluation: single-rater blind evaluation at n~20 only detects extreme (>=85%) preferences. We therefore build a calibrated LLM-judge protocol: the judge passes catch trials on known-answer pairs (16/16; pilots 22/24 and 24/24, versus 0.75 for the human rater), then annotates with two independent judges under A/B swap, disagreement counted as a tie. Using the judge's agree labels directly as DPO pairs (no reward model), we obtain the first positive result: held-out blind win rate 0.833 (tie=0.5; bootstrap CI [0.708, 0.938]; hard-agree 0.900 [0.699, 0.972]), repetition 0.114 to 0.000, MMLU within noise. We further identify a Goodhart channel: the judge's length preference transduces into output-length collapse via pairwise DPO (median 75 chars vs a 300-char prompt); an SFT-anchor repairs it (w is a style knob: w=0.1 preserves gains). Scaling preference data from 89 to 240 pairs yields no additional judge-visible gain. We release RA-Eval v0.1: a three-layer Chinese AI-flavor evaluation framework with 382 judge-annotated preference pairs.

replace-cross Represented but misread: Post-training changes how language models read out what they represent

Authors: Jochen Laubrock

Abstract: Does post-training change what a language model represents, or how this representation is read out into an answer? We compared linear probes with the models' own answers, analyzed with signal detection theory, in six open-weight base models and their post-trained versions. For judgments of whether German texts were written by a human or an AI, probes and word n-gram classifiers separated the classes well, but the answers did not and often ordered the texts inversely. Post-training lowered the sensitivity of the answers on average ($\Delta d' = -0.34$ across model families; -0.29 across three prompts). It also changed the use of a stated base rate: Base models shifted their log-odds by less than the stated change in prior odds, most post-trained models by more. In two models with stage-wise checkpoints, the change occurred during supervised fine-tuning, model stitching implicated the early layers, and from mid-depth on internal states at positions after the text, not those of the text, transferred the change between checkpoints. Our corpora cannot be shared, so we add a case reproducible from public data, the truth of negated statements. Here, post-training acted in the opposite direction: Probes decoded truth at or near ceiling in every model, the answers of several base models did not use it, and post-training repaired them; by mid-depth the repair resided at the positions after the statement; below it, the representation of the statement contributed, strongly in one of four models. In a training study, adding 2,000 correctly labeled authorship judgments of news texts to otherwise identical fine-tuning data improved the answers on our newspaper and textbook corpora and inverted them on the chats, where they followed a news-trained probe of the base model. Post-training thus changed how a judgment is read out of what a model represents, and a small training signal can set its direction.
